# Paper Set Evidence Digest

Number of papers in this paper set: 253

Each item below is a compact paper-level analysis digest. Use only this digest.

- source: Lo Iacono, S., Przepiorka, W., Buskens, V., Corten, R., van Assen, M., & van de Rijt, A. (2023). The competitive advantage of sanctioning institutions revisited: A multilab replication. *Pnas Nexus*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: Across seven European labs, allowing participants to choose between a sanctioning institution (SI) and a sanctioning-free institution (SFI) in a repeated public goods game leads to the majority of participants migrating to SI. In five labs, SI groups achieve significantly higher average profits and contributions than SFI groups, replicating the original findings. In the other two labs, the effect is weaker but still in the same direction. The payoff advantage of SI emerges after several rounds, as peer punishment sustains cooperation. The results are robust to variations in lab, language, and data collection method, but all parameter settings (e.g., group size, MPCR, punishment cost) are held constant as in the original study.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in a 12-player, 30-round linear public goods game with endogenous institution choice, MPCR of 0.133, and a peer punishment institution with a 1:3 cost-to-impact ratio, enabling punishment leads to higher group efficiency (average profit) compared to no-punishment control. The effect is robust across multiple European labs. The design dimensions are tightly controlled and fixed, so the findings are most directly applicable to similar parameterizations. The paper does not vary key design dimensions (e.g., punishment cost, group size), so it cannot inform how the effect would change under different settings, but it provides a strong baseline for the canonical design.

- source: Castillo, J. G., & Hamman, J. (2021). Political Accountability and Democratic Institutions: An Experimental Assessment. *Journal of Experimental Political Science*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The paper finds that centralized punishment increases group efficiency (measured as profit) compared to the no-punishment baseline. There is no significant difference in group efficiency or profit between groups with democratically elected managers and those with exogenously appointed managers, regardless of whether managers face repeated elections (political accountability) or not. The presence of punishment, not the method of authority selection or accountability, is the main driver of increased efficiency. The only notable behavioral effect is that managers in the democracy-with-accountability condition do not free ride more than other group members, unlike in other treatments.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in a public goods game with centralized punishment, enabling punishment increases group efficiency relative to a no-punishment baseline. However, the specific design features of how the punishing authority is selected (democratic vs. exogenous) and whether there is repeated selection (accountability) do not further increase efficiency. For prediction, the main effect to expect is a positive jump in efficiency when punishment is enabled, but little or no additional effect from varying the selection or accountability mechanism for the punisher. The game design dimensions (player count, MPCR, punishment cost/tech, etc.) are clearly specified and can be mapped directly to the prediction task.

- source: Nockur, L., Pfattheicher, S., & Keller, J. (2021). Different punishment systems in a public goods game with asymmetric endowments. *Journal of Experimental Social Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The study finds that introducing punishment (peer, democratic, or central) in a standard public goods game with either symmetric or asymmetric endowments increases relative contributions compared to no punishment. However, only democratic and central punishment systems avoid a significant reduction in group profit (efficiency) compared to no punishment; peer punishment reduces profit due to high punishment costs. The effect of punishment on efficiency does not significantly differ between symmetric and asymmetric groups. Democratic punishment yields the highest satisfaction and fairness ratings. Punishment systems also reduce profit inequality between advantaged and disadvantaged group members.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct evidence that, in a 4-player, 24-round PGG with MPCR=0.4, adding punishment increases contributions but does not always increase efficiency: peer punishment reduces group profit, while democratic and central punishment do not significantly change profit compared to no punishment. The effect of punishment on efficiency is robust to whether endowments are symmetric or asymmetric. For prediction, the presence and type of punishment system (peer, democratic, central) are critical moderators: peer punishment may reduce efficiency, while democratic and central punishment are neutral on average. Control efficiency (profit) is a good baseline; adding peer punishment may lower it, while democratic/central punishment may leave it unchanged. Other design dimensions (e.g., player count, rounds, MPCR, punishment cost/tech) are well specified and should be matched to this context for prediction.

- source: Harrell, A., & Simpson, B. (2016). The Dynamics of Prosocial Leadership: Power and Influence in Collective Action Groups. *Social Forces*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: In a repeated public goods game, groups of four first played six rounds without punishment, then six rounds with punishment under one of three conditions: (1) only a prosocial leader could punish, (2) only a proself leader could punish, or (3) all members could punish (peer-punishment). The introduction of punishment increased contributions in all conditions, but over time, prosocial-led groups maintained the highest contributions and group earnings, followed by peer-punishment and proself-led groups. Peer-punishment groups experienced much higher punishment costs, leading to lower overall efficiency (group earnings) than prosocial-led groups, and even lower than proself-led groups. The influence of the leader's contribution behavior on the rest of the group was a key mechanism. The study demonstrates that the design of the punishment institution (centralized vs. peer) and the social value orientation of the leader are critical moderators of punishment's effect on efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in a standard repeated public goods game, enabling punishment increases efficiency relative to no-punishment, but the effect is highly sensitive to the punishment institution's design. Peer-punishment (all can punish all) increases contributions but leads to high punishment costs and lower efficiency than centralized punishment by a prosocial leader. If the leader is proself, efficiency gains are limited. For prediction, the presence of peer-punishment should be expected to yield lower efficiency than centralized, prosocial leadership, due to over-punishment and coordination problems. The paper's detailed reporting of group earnings, contributions, and punishment costs under each condition makes it directly informative for predicting treatment efficiency from game design dimensions and control efficiency.

- source: Gächter, S., Kölle, F., & Quercia, S. (2017). Reciprocity and the tragedies of maintaining and providing the commons. *Nature Human Behaviour*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: Across four lab experiments, the authors compare public goods games where the group must either maintain an existing public good (maintenance) or create one from scratch (provision). Without punishment, cooperation and efficiency are systematically lower in maintenance than provision, despite identical incentives. When a costly punishment option is introduced (partners with punishment), both maintenance and provision games see a dramatic increase in the effective size of the public good, with efficiency rising to near-optimal levels and no significant difference between the two conditions. The punishment mechanism allows each player to assign up to five punishment points to each other, costing 1 MU per point and reducing the target's earnings by 3 MU per point. The paper also finds that negative reciprocity (punishment in response to low contributions) is stronger in maintenance than provision, and that the response to being punished (increased cooperation) is also stronger in maintenance. Overall, punishment is highly effective at restoring efficiency in both types of public goods dilemmas.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in a standard linear public goods game with four players, 27 rounds, and a punishment stage (cost 1 MU, impact 3 MU), the introduction of punishment increases group efficiency from low levels (especially in maintenance) to high levels, nearly matching the social optimum. The effect is robust across both 'maintenance' and 'provision' variants, and the punishment mechanism is highly effective at overcoming the decay in cooperation seen without punishment. For prediction tasks, this paper supports the expectation that enabling punishment in similar lab PGG designs will substantially increase efficiency, regardless of whether the dilemma is framed as maintenance or provision. The specific punishment technology (cost/impact) and the absence of communication or rewards are important contextual features.

- source: Gordon, D. S., & Puurtinen, M. (2021). High Cooperation and Welfare Despite-and Because of-the Threat of Antisocial Punishments and Feuds. *Journal of Experimental Psychology-general*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The study demonstrates that enabling peer punishment in a repeated public goods game with full information and fixed groups results in high cooperation and group earnings, even when punishment is costly (1:1 cost:impact ratio). All punishment treatments (cost-free, costly, asymmetric cost, and asymmetric immunity) yield significantly higher group earnings than the no-punishment control. However, when a group member is immune from punishment, that individual contributes less, and group earnings are reduced relative to other punishment treatments. The actual use of punishment is low, and antisocial punishment is rare, likely due to the high visibility of all actions and the possibility of long-term retaliation. The results suggest that the mere threat of punishment, in a context where all can be held accountable, is sufficient to maintain high efficiency, even if punishment is not cost-effective. The exception is when immunity allows a powerful individual to exploit the group, reducing efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in repeated public goods games with fixed groups, fixed identities, and full information about others' actions, enabling peer punishment (even if costly and inefficient) substantially increases group efficiency compared to no-punishment controls. The effect is robust across symmetric and asymmetric punishment cost structures, as long as all players are subject to punishment. However, if any player is immune from punishment, group efficiency drops due to free-riding by the immune individual. For prediction tasks, this suggests that the presence of punishment, full information, and universal accountability are key moderators of positive efficiency effects, and that the cost-effectiveness of punishment is less important in such settings. Control efficiency (no-punishment) is a strong baseline, but the addition of punishment in this design predicts a large positive shift in efficiency, unless immunity is present.

- source: Barclay, P. (2004). Trustworthiness and competitive altruism can also solve the tragedy of the commons. *Evolution and Human Behavior*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: This laboratory experiment with Han Chinese and Mongolian Chinese students uses a standard public goods game with and without a costly punishment stage. Each group has 4 players, and each subject plays 10 rounds without punishment and 10 rounds with punishment. The marginal per-capita return (MPCR) is 0.4. Punishment costs 1 token to the punisher and reduces the punished's payoff by 3 tokens. The main findings are: (1) Punishment increases mean contribution in all treatments, but the efficiency of punishment (measured as the increase in contribution per unit of punishment) varies by group composition. (2) In all-Han and mixed-ethnicity groups, punishment is efficient (marginal effect > 1), while in all-Mongolian groups, punishment is inefficient (marginal effect < 1). (3) Ethnic diversity (mixed groups) boosts punishment efficiency for both Han and Mongolian Chinese. (4) Mongolian Chinese punish more harshly within their own group due to cultural norms ('Anda' tradition), leading to lower efficiency. (5) Han Chinese show strong ingroup bias, contributing more in all-Han groups, especially with punishment. (6) Cultural fusion (Mongolian Chinese with Han cultural background) increases contribution in mixed groups. (7) Overall, the effect of punishment on efficiency is positive in all-Han and mixed groups, but not in all-Mongolian groups.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct evidence for predicting the effect of introducing punishment on efficiency in public goods games with different group compositions. For games with 4 players, 10 rounds, MPCR 0.4, and a punishment cost/impact ratio of 1:3, punishment increases efficiency in all-Han and mixed-ethnicity groups (marginal effect > 1), but is inefficient in all-Mongolian groups (marginal effect < 1). If the control (no-punishment) efficiency is known, the expected efficiency with punishment will increase substantially in all-Han and mixed groups, but only modestly or not at all in all-Mongolian groups. The results are robust to a 'clear-cut' Han Chinese sample. The findings are most applicable to lab settings with similar design parameters (player count, rounds, MPCR, punishment cost/impact, no chat, continuous contribution, no rewards, anonymous punishment, and known round count). The paper is highly informative for the prediction task, especially regarding the moderating role of group composition and cultural background.

- source: Krügel, J. P., & Maaser, N. (2025). How do higher-order punishment institutions shape cooperation and norm-enforcement?. *Review of International Organizations*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=mixed | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The experiment compares three conditions in a repeated linear public goods game with third-party punishment: Baseline (third party can punish), Fourth Party (third party can be sanctioned by an external observer), and Competition (two third-party candidates compete for selection by proposing punishment plans). Both Fourth Party and Competition increase average contributions and group earnings relative to Baseline. Fourth Party leads to the highest contributions and most severe/widespread punishment, but Competition achieves the highest average earnings for public good players, as punishment is more targeted and less frequent. Regression analyses confirm that both higher-order institutions significantly increase earnings and contributions, with Competition outperforming Fourth Party in terms of player payoffs. The results are robust across all periods and when focusing on later periods only.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct, high-quality evidence for predicting the effect of enabling punishment (and higher-order punishment institutions) on efficiency in public goods games. The experimental design is a standard repeated linear PGG with three players, 20 rounds, no chat, continuous contributions, and full feedback. The Baseline (punishment-only) condition provides a control for efficiency, and the two treatments show how additional institutional features (Fourth Party monitoring, Competition) further increase efficiency. The findings support the prediction that enabling punishment increases efficiency, and that the design of the punishment institution (especially targeted, competitive punishment) can further enhance efficiency by reducing unnecessary punishment. The paper's detailed reporting of payoffs, contributions, and punishment allows for quantitative calibration of efficiency effects under different institutional designs.

- source: Gross, J., De Dreu, C. K. W., & Reddmann, L. (2022). Shadow of conflict: How past conflict influences group cooperation and the use of punishment. *Organizational Behavior and Human Decision Processes*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: In a lab experiment with groups of four, participants either experienced an asymmetric intergroup conflict (attacker-defender game) or not, and then played a repeated public goods game with peer punishment. The main findings are: (1) Prior conflict does not significantly reduce average cooperation rates, but does reduce group earnings (efficiency) due to less effective punishment. (2) When previous conflict roles are identifiable, punishment is less likely to be targeted at free-riders and is less effective at increasing cooperation, especially when administered by previous attackers. (3) Hiding previous conflict roles (resetting group tags) increases both cooperation and group earnings over time, and punishment regains its effectiveness. (4) The negative effect of conflict on efficiency is not due to lower cooperation per se, but to maladaptive punishment patterns that reduce the welfare gains from cooperation.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct evidence that, in a standard repeated public goods game with peer punishment, the presence of a prior asymmetric conflict (with identifiable roles) reduces group efficiency (earnings) even if average cooperation rates are not significantly lower. The reduction in efficiency is due to less effective punishment: free-riders are punished less, and punishment by previous attackers is less effective. If prior conflict roles are hidden, efficiency increases and punishment regains its effectiveness. For prediction tasks, this suggests that the effectiveness of punishment in increasing efficiency is moderated by the social context—specifically, whether there is a 'shadow of conflict' and whether past roles are identifiable. In standard PGG designs without such conflict history, punishment is more likely to increase efficiency. The game design dimensions (player count, rounds, MPCR, punishment cost/tech, identifiability) are all well specified, and the control efficiency is directly comparable.

- source: Ertör-Akyazi, P. (2019). Formal versus informal institutions: Extraction and earnings in framed field experiments with small-scale fishing communities in Turkey. *Marine Policy*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: In a framed field experiment with Turkish small-scale fishers, four treatments were compared: baseline (open-access), face-to-face communication, monetary penalty (punishment for exceeding quota), and purchase of fishing rights (market-based over-quota payment). All groups played a linear PGG extraction game for 15 rounds (pre-treatment), then 14 rounds under one of the four treatments. The monetary penalty (punishment) significantly reduced extraction and moderately increased group earnings compared to baseline, but the effect was smaller than that of communication, which yielded the highest earnings and lowest inequality. The purchase of fishing rights (identical cost to penalty) did not significantly reduce extraction or increase earnings. Thus, punishment can improve efficiency, but its effect is sensitive to framing and is less than that of informal communication.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct evidence that introducing a monetary penalty (punishment) in a linear PGG extraction game with small groups (n=5) and low MPCR (0.4) increases group efficiency (earnings) relative to open-access, but the effect is moderate. The effect is smaller than that of enabling face-to-face communication. The framing of the punishment matters: a market-based 'purchase of rights' with identical cost is less effective than a penalty. For prediction, this suggests that punishment will generally increase efficiency over baseline, but the magnitude depends on the punishment's framing and the presence of communication. Control efficiency in this context is already above the selfish prediction, so the incremental gain from punishment is moderate. The results are most applicable to small groups, continuous extraction, no chat in baseline/punishment, and low but certain punishment.

- source: Gintis, H., Bowles, S., Boyd, R., & Fehr, E. (2003). Explaining altruistic behavior in humans. *Evolution and Human Behavior*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: Experimental public goods games consistently show that when participants can punish defectors at a personal cost, cooperation rates remain high or even increase over time, and group payoffs (efficiency) are substantially higher than when punishment is not allowed. In the Fehr & Gächter (2000) experiments, for example, allowing punishment led to a 40% increase in net payoffs to all subjects, even after accounting for the costs of punishment. The effect is observed even under strict anonymity and in one-shot or non-repeated interactions, indicating that the mechanism is not driven by expectations of future reciprocity. The paper also reviews cross-cultural evidence and evolutionary models, but the core experimental result is that punishment opportunities robustly improve group efficiency in public goods game environments.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong, direct evidence that enabling costly punishment in public goods games increases group efficiency relative to control conditions without punishment. The effect is robust across standard lab PGG designs with 4 players, 10 rounds, no chat, continuous contributions, and known MPCR. The magnitude of the efficiency gain is substantial (e.g., 40% increase in net payoffs in Fehr & Gächter 2000). The findings are directly relevant for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency given the control game design and efficiency, especially in standard PGG settings. The paper also discusses the importance of group structure (e.g., partner vs. stranger matching), but the positive effect of punishment on efficiency is consistent across these variations.

- source: Duell, D., Mengel, F., Mohlin, E., & Weidenholzer, S. (2024). Cooperation through collective punishment and participation. *Political Science Research and Methods*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: In a repeated public goods game with imperfect monitoring (noisy signals about individual contributions, but perfect information about total group contribution), the authors experimentally compare five regimes: no punishment, peer-to-peer punishment, collective punishment, peer-to-peer punishment with commitment, and collective punishment with commitment. The key finding is that only collective punishment with commitment (where group members can opt in to a regime that automatically punishes the whole group if total contributions fall short) leads to significantly higher group payoffs and cooperation rates compared to both no punishment and peer-to-peer punishment. Peer-to-peer punishment does not improve payoffs or cooperation relative to no punishment in this noisy environment. The positive effect of collective punishment with commitment is especially pronounced in the later rounds, suggesting a learning or coordination effect. The study also shows that the ability to participate in the decision to implement collective punishment (i.e., voting/commitment) is crucial for its effectiveness.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong, directly relevant evidence for predicting the effect of enabling collective punishment with commitment in a repeated public goods game with imperfect monitoring. If the control (no-punishment) efficiency is known, enabling collective punishment with commitment is likely to increase efficiency (group payoff) substantially, especially over time, provided the punishment is collective, pre-committed, and participation in the institution is possible. The effect is not observed for peer-to-peer punishment or for collective punishment without commitment. The experimental design and reporting allow for direct mapping to prediction tasks involving the effect of punishment on efficiency, given the game design dimensions.

- source: Hauser, O. P., Kraft-Todd, G. T., Rand, D. G., Nowak, M. A., & Norton, M. (2021). Invisible inequality leads to punishing the poor and rewarding the rich. *Behavioural Public Policy*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=mixed | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: In repeated public goods games with unequal endowments, allowing punishment and/or reward, the visibility of income distribution has a strong effect on sanctioning and contribution behavior. When incomes are hidden, participants tend to punish the poor (for low absolute contributions) and reward the rich (for high absolute contributions). When incomes are revealed, this pattern reverses: the poor are rewarded (for high percentage contributions) and the rich are punished (for low percentage contributions). Revealing incomes increases total contributions to the public good and reduces final payoff inequality (lower Gini coefficient). The effect is robust whether incomes are assigned randomly or earned via performance. The paper does not report group efficiency as a ratio to the fully cooperative outcome, but the increase in total contributions and reduction in inequality suggest a more positive effect on efficiency when income is revealed and punishment/reward is available.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in public goods games with unequal endowments and punishment/reward options, making income information visible increases total contributions and reduces payoff inequality, especially by shifting sanctioning away from the poor and toward the rich. For prediction tasks, the presence of income transparency (showing others' endowments) is a key moderator of punishment's effect on efficiency-related outcomes. The game design dimensions most directly supported are player count (5), number of rounds (10), presence of punishment and reward, unequal endowments, and the manipulation of information about others' incomes. The findings suggest that, all else equal, enabling punishment in a context where income is visible will have a more positive effect on efficiency and equality than when income is hidden. However, the paper does not report efficiency as a ratio to the fully cooperative outcome, so the exact magnitude of the effect is not available.

- source: Chen, J. C., D'Attoma, J., Fonseca, M. A., & Malezieux, A. (2025). Promoting Cooperation in an Unequal World: Experimental Evidence on the Role of Transparency and Punishment. *Journal of Business Ethics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=mixed | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: Across two lab experiments using a four-player linear public goods game with unequal endowments (determined by merit and luck), the paper finds that: (1) Peer punishment increases contributions and group payoffs, but only when endowments are transparent. (2) When endowments are secret, punishment has a limited effect on cooperation and does not reduce inequality; advantaged participants contribute a lower fraction of their endowment and earn much higher payoffs. (3) When endowments are made public, punishment becomes much more effective at increasing contributions and reducing inequality, especially among the advantaged. (4) Willingness to pay for secrecy is higher among advantaged participants, especially when their advantage is due to luck. (5) Overall, transparency is necessary for punishment to improve efficiency and mitigate the negative effects of inequality.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games is highly dependent on whether endowments (wealth) are transparent. In games with secret endowments, punishment has little effect on group payoff or efficiency; in games with public endowments, punishment significantly increases contributions and group payoffs, especially among the advantaged. For prediction, the presence of transparency (showing endowments or relevant summaries) is a key moderator: punishment is more likely to improve efficiency when transparency is high. The game uses continuous contributions, no chat, and a fixed MPCR of 0.4. Punishment is costly (cost 1, impact 3), and there is no reward option. The findings are most directly applicable to games with similar design features.

- source: Barclay, P., & Raihani, N. (2016). Partner choice versus punishment in human Prisoner's Dilemmas. *Evolution and Human Behavior*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=mixed | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: Across four studies, the paper finds that (1) punishers are rated as more trustworthy and group focused than nonpunishers; (2) after repeated PGG rounds, punishers are trusted more and receive more money in trust games, but this effect is absent after a single round; (3) justified punishment (punishing free riders) leads to increased trust and monetary benefits, while unjustified punishment leads to reduced trust and earnings; (4) the reputational and monetary benefits of justified punishment can offset or even exceed the cost of punishing, especially in repeated interactions. The paper does not directly report group efficiency ratios, but does show that punishment can increase individual and group monetary outcomes via increased trust and cooperation.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in repeated PGGs with punishment, justified punishment can increase the monetary benefits to punishers via enhanced trust and subsequent transfers, partially or fully offsetting the cost of punishment. The effect is contingent on repeated interaction (number of rounds) and the justification for punishment. For prediction tasks, this suggests that enabling punishment in repeated PGGs (with visible identities and reputation) is likely to increase group efficiency and individual payoffs, especially when punishment is justified and recognized as such. In single-round games, these benefits do not materialize. The paper does not provide direct group efficiency ratios, but the monetary outcomes and trust game results are closely related and informative for predicting efficiency changes due to punishment.

- source: Muthukrishna, M., Francois, P., Pourahmadi, S., & Henrich, J. (2017). Corrupting cooperation and how anti-corruption strategies may backfire. *Nature Human Behaviour*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=mixed | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: This paper experimentally demonstrates that allowing bribery in an institutional punishment public goods game (IPGG) substantially reduces public good provisioning (by about 25%). In the standard IPGG (control), stronger leaders (higher punishment multiplier) increase contributions and public good provisioning. However, when bribery is possible (bribery game, BG), stronger leaders actually decrease public good provisioning, as they use their power to extract bribes rather than enforce cooperation. Anti-corruption strategies (partial/full transparency, leader investment) can increase contributions in some contexts (strong leaders or high economic potential), but may have no effect or even reduce contributions further in contexts with weak leaders and poor economic potential. The main outcome is mean contribution to the public good, which is a close proxy for efficiency. The results show that the effect of punishment on efficiency is highly context-dependent and can be reversed or undermined by corruption opportunities.
  prediction_guidance: For predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, this paper provides strong evidence that the presence of corruption opportunities (e.g., bribery) can reverse the usual positive effect of institutional punishment on efficiency. The effect of punishment depends critically on whether leaders can be corrupted, the strength of the leader's punitive power, and the economic potential (mpcr). In standard IPGGs, stronger punishment increases efficiency, but in bribery-enabled games, stronger punishment can decrease efficiency. Anti-corruption interventions (transparency, leader investment) can help, but only under certain structural conditions. When using this paper for prediction, it is important to consider whether the game design allows for corruption/bribery, the strength of punishment, and the economic context. Control efficiency (without punishment) is not reported directly, but the relative changes are clear: punishment increases efficiency in standard IPGG, but decreases it when bribery is possible.

- source: Peysakhovich, A., & Rand, D. G. (2016). Habits of Virtue: Creating Norms of Cooperation and Defection in the Laboratory. *Management Science*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: Exposure to environments that support cooperation (via repeated PDs with high continuation probability and favorable payoffs) leads to higher prosociality in subsequent one-shot games, including the public goods game, trust game, dictator game, and ultimatum game. In a second experiment, exposure to cooperative environments increases third-party punishment of selfishness and reduces antisocial punishment in public goods games with punishment. These effects are strongest among subjects who rely on intuitive (heuristic) decision-making. However, the study does not report efficiency, group payoff, or welfare as outcomes, so the direct effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games is not assessed.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that institutional environments can shape prosocial and punishment behaviors in public-goods-game-like settings, and that these behaviors spill over into new contexts. However, because the study does not report efficiency or group payoff, it does not directly inform the prediction of treatment efficiency from game design dimensions plus control efficiency. The findings are most useful for predicting changes in cooperation and punishment rates, not efficiency. The design details (e.g., group size, punishment cost/tech, MPCR) are reported and can inform mapping to other studies, but the lack of efficiency outcomes limits direct predictive value for the downstream task.

- source: Chen, X. P., Pillutla, M. M., & Yao, X. (2009). Unintended Consequences of Cooperation Inducing and Maintaining Mechanisms in Public Goods Dilemmas: Sanctions and Moral Appeals. *Group Processes & Intergroup Relations*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: Sanctions (punishment or reward) in public goods games increase cooperation while present, but when removed, cooperation drops sharply—sometimes to levels below those in groups that never had sanctions. Moral appeals also increase cooperation, but this effect is more persistent after the appeal is removed. The drop in cooperation after removing sanctions is mediated by reduced trust in others' cooperation. The study does not report efficiency or group payoff, so findings are based on contribution rates, not direct payoff outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the presence of punishment (and reward) increases cooperation in the short term, but this effect is not sustained after removal, and may even backfire. However, because the paper does not report efficiency or group payoff, it cannot be used directly to predict efficiency outcomes for games with punishment enabled versus disabled. The findings are most useful for predicting contribution rates and for understanding the psychological mechanisms (trust) that may moderate the effect of punishment on cooperation. For efficiency prediction, the results suggest caution: increased cooperation under punishment may not translate to sustained efficiency gains, especially if punishment is later removed.

- source: Kiyonari, T., & Barclay, P. (2008). Cooperation in social dilemmas: Free riding may be thwarted by second-order reward rather than by punishment. *Journal of Personality and Social Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The main finding is that, in one-shot public goods games, participants are much more likely to support cooperation through reward (both first- and second-order) than through punishment. First-order punishment of defectors occurs, but second-order punishment of nonpunishers is rare or absent, and punishers are not socially endorsed. In contrast, both first- and second-order rewards are common, and rewarders are socially endorsed. The presence of both punishment and reward options does not increase support for punishment. The paper does not report on group efficiency or total payoffs, so the effect of punishment on efficiency cannot be directly assessed.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in one-shot, four-player public goods games with binary contribution and surprise sanctioning opportunities, punishment is not socially supported at the second order, and reward is more likely to be used and socially endorsed. However, because the paper does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes, it does not directly inform the prediction of efficiency changes when punishment is enabled. The behavioral results suggest that, in similar game designs, punishment may not be self-sustaining and may not improve efficiency unless supported by other mechanisms. The findings are most relevant for predicting the likelihood of punishment and reward behaviors, not their direct impact on efficiency.

- source: Villatoro, D., Andrighetto, G., Brandts, J., Nardin, L. G., Sabater-Mir, J., & Conte, R. (2014). The Norm-Signaling Effects of Group Punishment: Combining Agent-Based Simulation and Laboratory Experiments. *Social Science Computer Review*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The experiment demonstrates that when punishment is inflicted by the entire group, human subjects are more likely to cooperate (i.e., contribute to the public good) than when punishment is uncoordinated, even though the material cost to defectors is held constant. The authors attribute this to the norm-signaling function of group punishment, which is hypothesized to activate both instrumental and normative motivations for compliance. Agent-based simulations support the idea that norm salience, as modeled in the EMIL-I-A architecture, can explain the observed human behavior. However, the study does not report group efficiency, total earnings, or welfare, so the findings are limited to behavioral outcomes (contribution rates).
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the structure of punishment (specifically, whether it is coordinated by the group or uncoordinated) can significantly affect cooperation rates in public goods games, even when the material incentives are held constant. For prediction tasks focused on efficiency, the paper suggests that group punishment may lead to higher efficiency than uncoordinated punishment, but this is inferred from contribution rates rather than directly measured. The absence of direct efficiency or payoff data limits the ability to calibrate quantitative predictions of efficiency gains from punishment. The findings are most useful for understanding the qualitative impact of group punishment structure and norm-signaling on cooperation, which may be relevant for predicting efficiency when combined with other sources.

- source: Pfattheicher, S., Keller, J., & Knezevic, G. (2017). Sadism, the Intuitive System, and Antisocial Punishment in the Public Goods Game. *Personality and Social Psychology Bulletin*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The studies show that activating the intuitive system increases antisocial punishment among individuals with high sadism, while inhibiting intuition reduces it. This effect is specific to antisocial punishment and not to punishment of defectors or to contribution behavior. The presence of punishment in the game enables antisocial punishment, but the paper does not analyze the effect of punishment on group efficiency or total payoff. The findings are relevant for understanding the psychological moderators of antisocial punishment but do not provide direct evidence about efficiency or welfare outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence about the psychological and cognitive moderators of antisocial punishment in public goods games with punishment, but it does not report or analyze efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes. For the downstream prediction task (predicting efficiency with punishment enabled), this paper is only indirectly informative: it confirms that antisocial punishment can occur in standard PGGs with punishment, and that its frequency is affected by individual differences and cognitive processing mode. However, it does not quantify the impact of punishment on efficiency or provide control vs. treatment efficiency data. The game design dimensions are well specified, so the paper can help confirm the mapping of design features, but not the efficiency effect of punishment.

- source: Harrell, A. (2018). Competition for Leadership Promotes Contributions to Collective Action. *Social Forces*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The paper finds that the opportunity to compete for an elected leadership position (with punishment power) increases contributions to the public good before the leader is chosen, but only when voters can observe and use prior contribution behavior in their decision. After the leader is installed, groups with elected leaders maintain higher contributions than those with appointed leaders. The leader's use of punishment does not differ significantly between elected and appointed conditions. The main outcome is contribution rate; no explicit group payoff or efficiency measures are reported.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the process of leader selection (election vs. appointment) and the visibility of contribution history can significantly affect contribution rates in public goods games with punishment. However, because the paper does not report group payoff, efficiency, or welfare outcomes, it cannot be used directly to predict efficiency changes due to punishment. Its findings are most useful for predicting how leader selection mechanisms and information about contributions affect cooperation, which may indirectly inform efficiency predictions if contribution rates are assumed to map closely to efficiency in similar designs.

- source: Molleman, L., Kölle, F., Starmer, C., & Gächter, S. (2019). People prefer coordinated punishment in cooperative interactions. *Nature Human Behaviour*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The experiments show that in a one-shot, three-player binary public goods game with a costly punishment stage, a substantial fraction of participants prefer to punish free riders only if another group member also punishes (coordinated punishment). This preference is especially prevalent among those who do not punish unconditionally. The presence of coordinated punishment is robust across two large samples and is not correlated with conditional cooperation preferences. The study provides detailed behavioral evidence on how punishment decisions are conditioned on others' actions, but does not report on group efficiency or total payoffs with or without punishment.
  prediction_guidance: This paper is highly informative about the behavioral mechanisms of punishment in public goods games, especially the prevalence and motivations for coordinated punishment. However, it does not provide direct evidence on the effect of punishment on group efficiency or total payoff, nor does it compare efficiency between punishment-enabled and punishment-disabled conditions. For prediction tasks focused on efficiency, this paper suggests that the structure of punishment (e.g., whether it is coordinated) may matter, but does not provide quantitative or comparative efficiency data. The game design dimensions are well specified, which may help in mapping to other studies that do report efficiency outcomes.

- source: Kanitsar, G. (2019). Solidarity through punishment: An experiment on the merits of centralized enforcement in generalized exchange. *Social Science Research*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: Centralized punishment substantially increases the frequency of cooperation in both public good and generalized exchange games. However, the main focus is on how punishment affects solidarity, not efficiency. Solidarity (measured affectively, behaviorally, and via beliefs) increases with punishment in generalized exchange but not in the public good setting. The positive effect of punishment on solidarity is largely mediated by increased cooperation frequency. The experiment does not report group efficiency or total payoff, and the only payoff-related outcomes are cooperation rates and within-group payoff variation. Thus, the findings are only indirectly relevant to predicting efficiency effects of punishment.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that centralized punishment increases cooperation rates in both public good and generalized exchange games with four players and 16 rounds. However, it does not report efficiency or group payoff, so it cannot be used directly to predict the effect of punishment on efficiency. The design dimensions (player count, rounds, no chat, binary choice, etc.) are well specified, and the manipulation of punishment is clear (centralized, probabilistic, fixed fine). The main value for efficiency prediction is the robust increase in cooperation frequency with punishment, which is typically associated with higher efficiency in standard PGGs, but the absence of direct efficiency or payoff analysis limits the strength of inference.

- source: Mischkowski, D., Glöckner, A., & Lewisch, P. (2018). From spontaneous cooperation to spontaneous punishment - Distinguishing the underlying motives driving spontaneous behavior in first and second order public good games. *Organizational Behavior and Human Decision Processes*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The paper demonstrates that in both one-shot and iterated public goods games with a punishment option, individuals who decide to punish quickly invest more in punishment, especially if they contributed more than their peers and feel highly upset. The spontaneous punishment effect is not moderated by dispositional pro-sociality (SVO), but by situational factors (above-average contribution and negative affect). The study does not analyze or report group efficiency, welfare, or total payoffs, so it does not provide evidence on whether punishment increases or decreases efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides detailed evidence on the psychological and situational determinants of punishment behavior in public goods games, but does not report or analyze efficiency or payoff-based outcomes. Therefore, it does not directly inform the prediction of treatment efficiency from game design dimensions plus control efficiency. However, it does confirm that punishment is used more by above-average contributors and those experiencing negative affect, and that the cost-to-impact ratio of punishment is 1:4. The game design is a standard PGG with continuous contributions, four players, two rounds (Study 1) or one round (Study 2), no chat, and full information about others' contributions. The absence of efficiency or payoff analysis limits the paper's direct utility for efficiency prediction tasks.

- source: Eisenkopf, G., & Walter, C. (2022). Leadership with Imperfect Monitoring. *Leadership Quarterly*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The experiment finds that in a three-player, 20-round public goods game, punishment by a leader only increases contributions when the leader can observe individual follower contributions (individual monitoring). Under group monitoring (only aggregate info), punishment does not increase contributions compared to a simple recommendation. Leading-by-example increases coordination (alignment of contributions) but not aggregate contributions relative to recommendations. The study does not report efficiency or group payoff, so the effect of punishment on efficiency cannot be directly determined. However, since contributions are higher with punishment under individual monitoring, it is likely that efficiency would also be higher in that condition, assuming standard PGG payoff structure. The effect of punishment is thus strongly moderated by the monitoring technology.
  prediction_guidance: For prediction tasks, this paper provides strong evidence that the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games depends critically on whether the punisher can observe individual contributions (i.e., the 'punishment_tech' and monitoring dimension). If only aggregate (group-level) monitoring is available, punishment does not increase cooperation or likely efficiency. If individual-level monitoring is available, punishment increases cooperation and likely efficiency. The paper does not provide direct efficiency or payoff data, so predictions must be inferred from contribution rates. Other design dimensions (player count, number of rounds, MPCR, etc.) are clearly specified and match standard PGG setups. The absence of communication and rewards, and the use of continuous contribution choices, are also informative for prediction.

- source: Alt, M., Gallier, C., Kesternich, M., & Sturm, B. (2023). Collective minimum contributions to counteract the ratchet effect in the voluntary provision of public goods. *Journal of Environmental Economics and Management*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The experiment demonstrates that the effectiveness of punishment in promoting cooperation in public goods games depends critically on the perceived probability of enforcement. When punishment is enforced with high probability (p=1 or p=0.8), contributions are higher and sustained over time. When enforcement is weak (p=0.2), contributions decline and punishment does not deter free-riding. The study also finds that punishment behavior is backward-looking: individuals who escaped punishment in the past are more likely to be punished later. However, the paper does not report efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes directly.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the credibility of punishment enforcement is a key moderator of punishment's effect on cooperation in public goods games. While it does not report efficiency or group payoff directly, the contribution results imply that efficiency gains from punishment are only realized when enforcement is highly credible. For prediction tasks, this suggests that the effectiveness of punishment in increasing efficiency is contingent on the probability of enforcement. However, the lack of direct efficiency or payoff data limits the precision of quantitative predictions.

- source: Pleasant, A., & Barclay, P. (2018). Why Hate the Good Guy? Antisocial Punishment of High Cooperators Is Greater When People Compete To Be Chosen. *Psychological Science*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The study finds that when participants in a public goods game with punishment are competing to be chosen for a subsequent trust game, antisocial punishment (punishing high contributors) is much higher than in a standard PGG with punishment. This effect is present from the first round and persists across rounds. In the control condition, contributions increase over rounds (as is typical when punishment is available), but in the markets condition, contributions do not increase, suggesting that antisocial punishment suppresses the escalation of cooperation. The study does not report group efficiency or total payoffs, but the behavioral pattern implies that the efficiency gains typically associated with punishment may be undermined or reversed in contexts with high antisocial punishment driven by partner competition.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the context of partner competition (biological markets) can substantially increase antisocial punishment in public goods games with punishment, which in turn suppresses the usual increase in cooperation seen with punishment. While the study does not report efficiency or group payoff directly, the findings imply that in environments where partner choice or competitive altruism is salient, enabling punishment may not improve and may even harm group efficiency, as antisocial punishment undermines cooperation. For prediction tasks, this suggests that the presence of partner competition or similar social comparison incentives is a critical moderator: in such settings, the efficiency benefit of punishment is likely reduced or reversed, even if control efficiency is known. However, the lack of direct efficiency or payoff data limits quantitative prediction.

- source: Grossman, G., & Baldassarri, D. (2012). The Impact of Elections on Cooperation: Evidence from a Lab-in-the-Field Experiment in Uganda. *American Journal of Political Science*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The introduction of a centralized sanctioning authority (monitor) in a PGG increases contributions to the public good compared to a baseline with no punishment. When the monitor is elected rather than randomly assigned, contributions increase further, suggesting a legitimacy effect. Both types of monitors punish defectors at a personal cost, but the actual punishment strategies are similar between elected and random monitors. The study does not report group efficiency or total earnings, so the direct effect on efficiency is not measured. The findings are robust to controls for monitor characteristics and are supported by observational data linking legitimacy to cooperation in real-world farmer groups.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that introducing centralized punishment in a PGG increases contributions, and that the legitimacy of the punisher (via election) further increases cooperation. However, because the main outcome is contribution rather than efficiency or group payoff, the findings should be used as indirect evidence for predicting efficiency effects. The game design dimensions are well specified, and the experimental manipulation is clear, but the lack of direct efficiency or payoff reporting limits the precision of predictions for efficiency outcomes.

- source: Jin, S. X., Columbus, S., van Lange, P. A. M., & Balliet, D. (2024). Conflict, cooperation, and institutional choice. *Journal of Experimental Social Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: Across two experiments, higher conflict of interests (lower MPCR) leads to lower cooperation, lower beliefs about others' cooperation, and lower normative expectations and personal norms of cooperation. However, the degree of conflict does not increase the likelihood that individuals or groups vote to establish a sanctioning institution (peer punishment, centralized punishment, or gossip/ostracism). Centralized punishment is more likely to be chosen than peer punishment or gossip/ostracism, but this is not moderated by conflict level. The main outcomes are behavioral (contributions, beliefs, norms, votes), not efficiency or group payoff. There is no direct evidence on how enabling punishment affects efficiency or group payoff relative to a no-punishment baseline.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence on how conflict of interests and institutional choice affect cooperation, beliefs, and norms in public goods games, but does not report efficiency or group payoff as a function of punishment. Thus, it does not directly inform the prediction of treatment efficiency from game design dimensions and control efficiency. However, it does show that the mere availability of punishment institutions (when endogenously chosen) is not more likely under high conflict, and that cooperation declines more rapidly under high conflict. The lack of efficiency or payoff analysis means this paper should be used for context and mechanism, not for direct quantitative prediction of efficiency effects.

- source: Mulder, L. B., van Dijk, E., De Cremer, D., & Wilke, H. A. M. (2006). Undermining trust and cooperation: The paradox of sanctioning systems in social dilemmas. *Journal of Experimental Social Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The paper finds that sanctioning systems in public goods games can undermine trust in others' intrinsic motivation to cooperate. When the sanctioning system is removed, both trust and cooperation decrease, especially among participants with high initial trust. The negative effect on cooperation is mediated by the reduction in trust. The effect is stronger for large sanctions and when initial trust is high. However, the paper does not report efficiency or group payoff, so the direct effect on efficiency cannot be determined from the reported results.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the presence of a punishment system can undermine trust and, after its removal, reduce cooperation, especially when initial trust is high. For prediction tasks, this suggests that the effect of punishment on efficiency may depend on baseline trust levels and that the removal of punishment can have negative aftereffects on cooperation. However, because the paper does not report efficiency or group payoff, it cannot be used to directly predict efficiency outcomes. The findings are most useful for understanding behavioral mechanisms and potential moderators (such as initial trust and punishment size), rather than for quantitative prediction of efficiency.

- source: Krupp, D. B., Debruine, L. A., & Barclay, P. (2008). A cue of kinship promotes cooperation for the public good. *Evolution and Human Behavior*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The study shows that subtle cues of kinship (facial self-resemblance) increase cooperation in a public goods game with punishment. Participants contributed more to the public good as the number of self-resembling (kin-cue) group members increased, but did not change their punishment behavior toward free riders based on kinship cues. The experiment used a yoked control design to isolate the effect of kinship cues. The main outcomes are contribution and punishment levels; efficiency or group payoff is not directly measured. The findings suggest that kinship cues can promote cooperation, but do not alter punitive responses to free riding.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence that, in a standard public goods game with punishment, increasing perceived kinship among group members increases contributions but does not affect punishment rates. While efficiency is not directly measured, higher contributions generally imply higher efficiency, unless offset by excessive punishment costs. Since punishment rates did not increase, it is reasonable to infer that efficiency would likely increase with kinship cues. However, the paper does not manipulate or analyze any of the core game design dimensions (such as MPCR, punishment cost, or player count) except for the kinship cue. Thus, its direct value for predicting efficiency as a function of game design dimensions is limited, but it does suggest that social context (kinship cues) can moderate cooperation independently of punishment mechanics.

- source: Xu, Z. C. (2023). Obedience to the symbol of authority: Experimental evidence on the symbolic source of legitimate authority*. *European Journal of Political Economy*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The experiment finds that introducing a symbolic scepter for the authority significantly increases citizens' support for the authority's punishment decisions, regardless of the payment scheme (appropriation vs. fixed salary) or information transparency. However, the symbol does not affect the frequency or style of punishment by authorities, nor does it affect citizens' contribution rates or group/individual payoffs. The mechanism is psychological: the symbol increases intrinsic obedience and perceived legitimacy, not actual cooperation or efficiency. There is no evidence that the symbol improves efficiency, welfare, or total group earnings.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that symbolic manipulations of authority can increase perceived legitimacy (support for punishment) in public goods games with punishment, but do not affect efficiency, group payoffs, or contribution rates. For the downstream prediction task, the presence or absence of a symbolic authority is unlikely to affect the efficiency outcome when punishment is enabled, holding other game design dimensions and control efficiency constant. The main moderators (payment scheme, transparency) do not interact with the symbol to affect efficiency. Thus, symbolic authority should not be expected to improve efficiency in similar lab PGG environments.

- source: Chen, F. D., Nave, G., & Wang, L. (2025). Calculated Punishment. *Journal of Business Ethics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The experiment demonstrates that both prosocial and antisocial punishment rates are strongly affected by the cost and impact of punishment. When punishment is free, antisocial punishment increases substantially. The study does not directly analyze efficiency or group payoff, but the findings imply that the design of punishment (especially cost) can influence the prevalence of antisocial punishment, which is typically detrimental to group welfare. The main contribution is a process-level account of punishment decisions, showing that people are not intuitively predisposed to punish, and that punishment is a calculated response sensitive to incentives.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the cost and impact (effectiveness) of punishment are critical moderators of punishment behavior in public goods games. While it does not report efficiency or group payoff directly, the finding that antisocial punishment increases when punishment is free suggests that low-cost punishment can undermine group welfare. For prediction tasks, the results support the importance of including punishment cost and effectiveness as key dimensions, and caution that enabling low-cost punishment may not always improve efficiency due to increased antisocial punishment. The paper does not provide direct quantitative estimates of efficiency, so its value is mainly in highlighting moderators and mechanisms.

- source: Lubell, M., & Scholz, J. T. (2001). Cooperation, reciprocity, and the collective-action heuristic. *American Journal of Political Science*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The experiments show that both reciprocity and niceness in the environment increase cooperation rates, but their effects depend on whether subjects are initially predisposed to cooperate or defect. Past experience with reciprocity increases cooperation even when current reciprocity is absent, while past nonreciprocity does not reduce cooperation when reciprocity is present. The introduction of punishment (penalties) increases cooperation among defectors and in nonreciprocal environments, but can reduce cooperation among initial cooperators in reciprocal environments. The effect of punishment is thus asymmetric: it is most beneficial in environments lacking reciprocity, but may undermine cooperation where social capital is already high. The main outcome is cooperation rate, not direct efficiency or group payoff, but the authors discuss the implications for the evolution of cooperation and social capital.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the effect of punishment on cooperation (and by implication, efficiency) depends on the underlying level of reciprocity and the initial disposition of players. Punishment increases cooperation in environments where reciprocity is low (nonreciprocal), but can reduce cooperation among those already inclined to cooperate in high-reciprocity environments. For prediction tasks, this suggests that the marginal effect of punishment on efficiency is likely to be positive in low-cooperation, low-reciprocity settings, but may be neutral or even negative in high-cooperation, high-reciprocity settings. However, since the paper does not directly report efficiency or group payoff, these implications are inferred from cooperation rates. The design dimensions (player count, rounds, punishment cost, etc.) are well specified, but the main outcome is behavioral (cooperation), not payoff-based.

- source: Balliet, D., & Van Lange, P. A. M. (2013). Trust, Punishment, and Cooperation Across 18 Societies: A Meta-Analysis. *Perspectives on Psychological Science*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: Punishment opportunities increase cooperation in public goods games, with a moderate average effect (d=0.77, 95% CI [0.63, 0.91]), but with substantial cross-societal variation. The effect of punishment is significantly stronger in high-trust societies. Methodological moderators show that punishment is more effective in partner-matching protocols (fixed groups) and with more iterations (rounds), but not significantly affected by group size. Other societal variables (GDP, democracy, market competition, religious participation, norms of civic cooperation) have weaker or non-significant effects after controlling for trust. The outcome measured is behavioral cooperation/contribution, not efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in the standardized Fehr & Gächter public goods game, enabling peer punishment increases cooperation rates, especially in high-trust societies. However, the outcome is contribution/cooperation, not efficiency or group payoff, so direct prediction of efficiency is not possible from this paper alone. The findings suggest that, for lab PGGs with 3-7 players, 6-50 rounds, and standard costly peer punishment (1 MU cost to reduce 3 MUs), punishment increases cooperation, with larger effects in partner-matching and longer games. The effect on efficiency is likely positive if higher cooperation translates to higher group payoffs, but this is not directly measured. The paper is highly informative for predicting behavioral cooperation under different game designs and societal contexts, but less so for direct efficiency outcomes.

- source: Hilbig, B. E., Zettler, I., & Heydasch, T. (2012). Personality, Punishment and Public Goods: Strategic Shifts Towards Cooperation as a Matter of Dispositional Honesty-Humility. *European Journal of Personality*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The presence of punishment in a PGG increases individual contributions to the public good, replicating prior findings. This effect is moderated by the Honesty-Humility personality trait: individuals low in Honesty-Humility increase their contributions substantially when punishment is possible, while those high in Honesty-Humility contribute at a high level regardless of punishment. The study uses hypothetical (non-incentivized) PGGs, and the main outcome is contribution behavior, not actual efficiency or group payoff. A simulation based on observed contributions suggests that group efficiency (total group gain) increases with the number of high Honesty-Humility members, but this is not an experimental outcome. The findings are robust across two experiments, including one with a large, heterogeneous sample and a substantial time lag between personality and PGG measurement.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the presence of punishment increases contributions in PGGs, especially among individuals low in Honesty-Humility. However, because the experiments are hypothetical and do not use real monetary incentives, and because efficiency or group payoff is not directly measured as an experimental outcome, the findings are most useful for predicting changes in contribution rates rather than efficiency per se. The simulation of group efficiency based on observed contributions provides indirect evidence that punishment could increase efficiency, but this is not a direct experimental result. For prediction tasks focused on efficiency, this paper offers informative indirect evidence that punishment increases contributions (and thus likely efficiency), but the lack of real incentives and direct efficiency measurement are important limitations.

- source: Baldassarri, D. (2015). Cooperative Networks: Altruism, Group Solidarity, Reciprocity, and Sanctioning in Ugandan Producer Organizations. *American Journal of Sociology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The study finds that both communication and the threat of sanctioning increase contributions in a repeated PGG compared to baseline. Communication (reciprocity) has a stronger effect than punishment. However, only cooperation in the communication condition predicts real-life cooperative behaviors in the farmer organizations. The threat of sanctioning increases contributions in the lab, but this does not translate to real-world cooperation among council members. The paper does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes, so the effect of punishment on efficiency cannot be directly assessed.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in small, cohesive groups with repeated interaction, communication (reciprocity) is a more effective and relevant mechanism for promoting cooperation than punishment. While punishment increases contributions in the lab PGG, it does not predict real-world cooperative behavior in this context.
