Control efficiency (without punishment) is not reported, but the results imply that adding weak punishment may decrease efficiency, while strong punishment may increase it, conditional on the induced decision frame.

- source: Mulder, L. B. (2008). The difference between punishments and rewards in fostering moral concerns in social decision making. *Journal of Experimental Social Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, reward_exists
  findings: The paper finds that introducing the concept of punishment (without actual economic consequences) in a public-goods-like game increases cooperation and moral concerns compared to reward or control. Actual punishment (vs. reward) in a dictator-type allocation game leads to stronger moral disapproval of non-compliers and greater willingness to disadvantage them, even at a personal cost. The effect of punishment on cooperation is mediated by increased moral concerns and collective-interest motives. However, the study does not measure or report group efficiency, total payoff, or welfare outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that punishment (as a concept or mechanism) increases cooperation and moral concerns more than reward in public-goods-like settings. However, it does not provide direct evidence about the effect of punishment on group efficiency or payoff-based outcomes, which are the target for prediction. The findings suggest that, under similar game design dimensions, punishment is likely to increase cooperation, but the magnitude of efficiency gains or losses cannot be inferred from this paper. The results are most useful for predicting behavioral and attitudinal changes, not efficiency.

- source: Seip, E. C., van Dijk, W. W., & Rotteveel, M. (2009). On Hotheads and Dirty Harries The Primacy of Anger in Altruistic Punishment. *Values, Empathy, and Fairness Across Social Barriers*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds
  findings: The main finding is that anger, rather than the perception of unfairness, is the primary driver of altruistic punishment in social dilemma games. Experimental evidence from a one-shot, three-person public goods game with punishment shows that lower contributions from others evoke more anger and lead to harsher punishment, and that the effect of others' contributions on punishment is fully mediated by experienced anger. Priming participants with anger increases their tendency to punish defectors. The paper does not report on efficiency, group payoff, or welfare, so it does not directly inform the effect of punishment on efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency or group payoff in public goods games. However, it does suggest that the presence and intensity of punishment are strongly influenced by emotional responses (especially anger) to defection or low contribution. For prediction tasks, this implies that punishment is likely to be used when players experience anger at norm violations, but the paper does not address whether this increases or decreases group efficiency. The findings are most relevant for understanding when and why punishment is deployed, not its payoff consequences.

- source: Chávez, C. A., Murphy, J. J., & Stranlund, J. K. (2018). Managing and defending the commons: Experimental evidence from TURFs in Chile. *Journal of Environmental Economics and Management*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=close | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The paper finds that in a dynamic common-pool resource game with insiders (who can communicate) and outsiders (who cannot), the introduction of poaching without monitoring or sanctions dramatically reduces insiders' earnings and resource sustainability, nearly eliminating the efficiency gains from cooperation. Adding weak external enforcement (low-probability monitoring and fines) or allowing insiders to invest in monitoring (with the same fine) both significantly reduce poaching and increase insiders' earnings and harvests, but do not fully restore efficiency to baseline (no-poaching) levels. Combining external and local monitoring does not further reduce poaching or increase efficiency. The main constraint is insiders' inability to coordinate sufficient monitoring investment. The results are robust across field (TURF members) and student samples.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct evidence that, in a dynamic CPR game with communication among insiders, the introduction of punishment (via monitoring and fines for poaching) increases group efficiency relative to a poaching-only control, but the effect is partial and depends on the ability to coordinate monitoring. The effect is similar whether monitoring is provided externally or by insiders, and combining both does not yield additional efficiency. For prediction, the presence of punishment (even if weak) should be expected to increase efficiency above the poaching-only baseline, but not to the level of the no-poaching or full-cooperation baseline. The magnitude of the effect is limited by the monitoring probability and the coordination of monitoring investment.

- source: Freytag, A., Güth, W., Koppel, H., & Wangler, L. (2014). Is regulation by milestones efficiency enhancing? An experimental study of environmental protection. *European Journal of Political Economy*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=close | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: This laboratory experiment tests whether adding intermediate contribution targets ('milestones') to a cumulative threshold public goods game increases efficiency, measured by group payoffs and the probability of reaching a final target. The game is played in groups of 5 over 6 rounds, with treatments varying the strictness of milestones, the marginal per-capita return (mpcr), and the probability of group loss if targets are missed. The main findings are: (1) In a scenario with no continuous efficiency gains (i.e., only reaching the target matters), strict milestones increase both contributions and the probability of reaching the target, thus enhancing efficiency. (2) In a scenario with continuous efficiency gains (i.e., contributions always increase payoffs), strict milestones actually reduce average contributions and efficiency, as groups focus on meeting milestones rather than maximizing payoffs. (3) In an intermediate scenario, the positive and negative effects cancel out. The effect of milestones is thus context-dependent, and their efficiency impact is not universally positive.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct evidence that the effect of punishment-like milestone mechanisms on efficiency in public-goods-like games is highly sensitive to the underlying payoff structure. When the only way to generate group payoffs is to reach a threshold (no continuous efficiency gains), strict milestones (punishment for missing intermediate targets) can improve efficiency by increasing coordination and contributions. However, when continuous efficiency gains are present (i.e., contributions always increase payoffs), strict milestones can reduce efficiency, as they may distract from maximizing group welfare. For prediction, this means that the presence and structure of milestone/punishment mechanisms, the marginal per-capita return, and the risk of group loss must all be considered. Control efficiency (without milestones/punishment) is a strong moderator: if control efficiency is already high, milestones may not help and can even hurt. The findings are directly relevant for predicting treatment efficiency from design dimensions in cumulative threshold public goods games with milestone/punishment features.

- source: Sääksvuori, L. (2014). Intergroup conflict, ostracism, and the evolution of cooperation under free migration. *Behavioral Ecology and Sociobiology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=close | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=mixed | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: This paper experimentally studies a dynamic public goods game with endogenous group formation, free migration, and treatments varying the presence of intergroup competition and ostracism (social exclusion by majority vote). The main findings are: (1) Ostracism increases average group size and contributions to the public good, resulting in higher net payoffs and aggregate returns, both with and without intergroup competition. (2) Intergroup competition alone does not increase group size or contributions, and leads to more imbalanced group structures, which reduces aggregate returns. (3) The combination of ostracism and competition still produces higher cooperation and payoffs than competition alone. (4) The threat of ostracism reduces the number of free-agents and increases the desirability of joining groups, outweighing the effect of involuntary exclusion. (5) Intergroup competition reduces the likelihood of ostracism, as groups are less willing to expel members when facing external threats.
  prediction_guidance: For prediction tasks, this paper provides strong evidence that enabling a social sanctioning mechanism (ostracism) in a dynamic, migration-allowed public goods environment increases both cooperation and group-level payoffs (i.e., efficiency), regardless of whether intergroup competition is present. The effect of ostracism is robust and positive for efficiency-related outcomes. Intergroup competition alone does not improve efficiency and may even reduce it by fragmenting groups. The design features—such as endogenous group formation, free migration, and ostracism by majority vote—are important moderators. The paper does not use classic costly punishment (with explicit cost per punishment point), but ostracism functions as a group-level sanction. Thus, for environments with similar group formation and migration, the presence of ostracism is a strong positive predictor for efficiency gains from sanctioning.

- source: Littman, R., Estrada, S., Stagnaro, M. N., Dunham, Y., Rand, D., & Baskin-Sommers, A. (2020). Community Violence and Prosociality: Experiencing and Committing Violence Predicts Norm-Enforcing Punishment but Not Cooperation. *Social Psychological and Personality Science*.
  type: empirical | empirical=observational | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=close | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that, in a high-violence urban US sample, exposure to and commission of violence are not associated with increased cooperation in economic games (including a Public Goods Game), but are positively associated with norm-enforcing punishment (i.e., willingness to pay to punish selfish others in economic games). The association is specific to norm-enforcing punishment and not to antisocial punishment or general willingness to punish. No efficiency or group payoff outcomes are reported.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as it does not report efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes. However, it does provide indirect evidence that, in a US urban context, individuals with higher exposure to or commission of violence are more likely to engage in norm-enforcing punishment, but not more likely to cooperate. This suggests that the presence of punishment may not increase cooperation or efficiency in similar populations, but the study does not test the causal effect of enabling punishment on efficiency. The design and results are most relevant for understanding individual differences in punishment and cooperation, not for predicting treatment effects of punishment interventions.

- source: Bastian, B., Brewer, M., Duffy, J., & Van Lange, P. A. M. (2019). From cash to crickets: The non-monetary value of a resource can promote human cooperation. *Journal of Environmental Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=close | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that highlighting the non-monetary value of a resource (e.g., the moral significance of not harming crickets) leads to reduced personal consumption in a resource dilemma game, indicating increased cooperation. This effect is robust across two studies and is not moderated by individual differences in empathy or moral expansiveness. When a punishment mechanism is introduced (participants can pay to fine a greedy player), there is no significant difference in punishment behavior between the moral-value and control conditions. The main outcome is behavioral (points taken), not direct group efficiency or payoff, but the results suggest that moral framing can promote cooperation even in the presence of defection and punishment opportunities.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence that moral or non-monetary framing of the resource can increase cooperation (reduce consumption) in resource dilemma games, which may translate to higher group efficiency if mapped to payoff outcomes. However, the study does not directly report efficiency or group payoff, and the punishment mechanism (costly punishment of defectors) did not significantly affect behavior. For prediction tasks, the findings suggest that game design features that increase the perceived moral value of the resource may enhance cooperation, but the effect of punishment per se on efficiency cannot be determined from this paper. The design is close to a public goods/common pool resource game, but not a standard PGG, and the main outcomes are not efficiency or payoff-based.

- source: Lang, M. R., Chvaja, R., & Purzycki, B. G. (2024). The role of costly commitment signals in assorting cooperators during intergroup conflict. *Evolution and Human Behavior*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper demonstrates that costly commitment signals can facilitate the assortment of cooperators in public-goods-game-like environments with intergroup competition, leading to higher group efficiency and greater probability of winning. However, the relationship between signal cost and efficiency is not monotonic: low-cost signals are generally more effective at producing efficient, cooperative groups, except when the cost is directly subtracted from the investable endowment during intergroup conflict, in which case high-cost signals can create highly efficient, winning groups. Forced signaling (where participants are assigned to signal or not) does not reliably increase efficiency or group payoff. The study does not include a punishment phase or peer-sanctioning mechanism, so its direct relevance to punishment is limited, but the costly signal serves as a commitment device that can be interpreted as a form of self-imposed cost or pre-commitment. The findings suggest that the design of commitment mechanisms (including their cost structure and voluntariness) is crucial for predicting their effect on group efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper is highly informative for predicting the effect of costly commitment mechanisms (but not peer punishment) on group efficiency in public-goods-game-like settings with intergroup competition. It shows that voluntary, low-cost signals are generally more effective at producing efficient groups, except when the cost is directly subtracted from the investable endowment, in which case high-cost signals can be effective. Forced (non-voluntary) signaling does not reliably increase efficiency. The findings suggest that the effect of commitment mechanisms on efficiency is sensitive to the cost structure, voluntariness, and whether the cost reduces investable resources. However, because the study does not include peer punishment or sanctions, its direct applicability to standard punishment treatments is limited. For prediction tasks involving commitment devices or entry costs, the results are directly relevant; for peer punishment, the transfer value is more indirect.

- source: Hashim, M. J., Kannan, K. N., Maximiano, S., & Ulmer, J. R. (2014). Digital Piracy, Teens, and the Source of Advice: An Experimental Study. *Journal of Management Information Systems*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=mixed | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: Advice from parents, especially when they are punished for their child's piracy, increases purchasing and group profit and reduces piracy compared to other advice sources. Advice from record labels is the least effective and can even reduce group profit. Advice from a regulator is largely ignored. The effect of advice is strongest when the adviser has both a social tie and a stake in the outcome. The study does not include standard peer punishment or reward mechanisms; the only 'punishment' is an exogenous penalty to parents in one treatment. The game is a close variant of a public goods game, but not a standard PGG. Efficiency-related outcomes (group profit, purchasing) are reported, but not as a ratio to the fully cooperative outcome.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence for the prediction task. It shows that exogenous punishment of a third party (parents) for a player's free-riding can increase public good provision and group profit, especially when combined with a social tie. However, it does not test endogenous peer punishment or standard PGG punishment institutions. The findings suggest that punishment mechanisms may be more effective when they are socially salient and affect someone with a close tie to the player, but direct transfer to standard PGG with peer punishment is limited. Control efficiency (group profit without punishment) is reported, and the effect of introducing a punishment-like mechanism (parent penalty) can be inferred, but the mechanism differs from standard punishment. The absence of peer punishment, reward, or communication means the results are only adjacent for direct prediction of PGG punishment effects.

- source: Dickson, E. S., Gordon, S. C., & Huber, G. A. (2015). Institutional Sources of Legitimate Authority: An Experimental Investigation. *American Journal of Political Science*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=unclear
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The experiment finds that citizens are more willing to help salaried (fixed-compensation) authorities than those who personally benefit from collected penalties, but only when transparency is high. When transparency is low, support for salaried authorities collapses, while support for appropriator authorities is low regardless of transparency. Contribution rates and average earnings are reported, but do not differ significantly between treatments under full information; under limited information, contribution rates are lower for salaried authorities. The main outcome is a behavioral measure of legitimacy (citizen intervention), not efficiency or group payoff. The deterrence index (successful punishment of noncontributors minus punishment of contributors) is associated with higher contributions, but the causal effect of punishment on efficiency is not directly estimated.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence about how institutional features (transparency, authority incentives) affect citizen support for enforcement and, through deterrence, contribution rates. However, it does not directly estimate the effect of enabling punishment on group efficiency or payoff. The design is a binary (all-or-nothing) public goods game with centralized, probabilistic punishment and a unique intervention stage. The findings suggest that the legitimacy of punishment (as measured by citizen support) is sensitive to transparency and authority incentives, which may in turn affect deterrence and contributions, but the direct impact on efficiency is not quantified. For prediction tasks, this paper is informative about mechanisms and moderators but does not provide direct treatment effect estimates for efficiency.

- source: Harrell, A., & Traeger, M. L. (2025). Reputation-based reciprocity in human-bot and human-human networks. *Pnas Nexus*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, reward_exists
  findings: The presence of bots in indirect reciprocity networks reduces human generosity, even when bots are programmed to be strong reciprocators. This reduction is driven by humans' reluctance to help bots, due to weaker norms about bots deserving help, not by a lack of reputational concern. Both 'reputational giving' (giving when observed by a third party) and 'rewarding reputations' (helping those with a reputation for helping) are less effective when bots are present. The paper does not report group efficiency or total payoff, but the reduction in giving implies lower group welfare in hybrid networks compared to human-only networks.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence that the presence of non-human agents (bots) in indirect reciprocity games reduces generosity and, by implication, group welfare. However, it does not manipulate or analyze punishment, nor does it report efficiency or payoff outcomes directly. For prediction tasks about punishment effects on efficiency, this paper is only adjacent: it suggests that social context (e.g., presence of bots) can moderate the effectiveness of reciprocity-based mechanisms, but does not provide direct evidence about punishment or efficiency. The design and findings may be informative for understanding how social norms and recipient identity affect cooperation, but not for quantitative prediction of punishment effects.

- source: De Luca, G., Sekeris, P. G., & Spengler, D. E. (2018). Can violence harm cooperation? Experimental evidence. *Journal of Environmental Economics and Management*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The experiment compares two treatments: a 'conflict' treatment where violent appropriation (chance) is a viable option when resources are low, and a 'control' treatment where the cost of appropriation is so high that it is never optimal. In the conflict treatment, participants are significantly less likely to choose the cooperative extraction rate ('low') and more likely to choose the non-cooperative rate ('high') in early rounds, anticipating future conflict. The expectation of future conflict thus undermines cooperation even before conflict occurs. The paper does not report group efficiency or total payoffs, but the behavioral shift from cooperation to non-cooperation implies lower efficiency in the conflict treatment. Additional analyses show that experiencing conflict in previous games further reduces cooperation in subsequent games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that introducing a credible, destructive appropriation/conflict option in a dynamic CPR game reduces cooperative behavior and likely reduces efficiency, even before conflict is actually chosen. For prediction tasks, the presence of a credible destructive sanction (even if not a standard punishment mechanism) should be expected to reduce efficiency relative to a control with no such option. However, the paper does not study standard punishment (costly peer punishment for norm violations) but rather a destructive appropriation/conflict option, so the results are most relevant for settings where punishment is destructive and ends the game, not for standard public goods games with ongoing punishment. The findings suggest that the threat of destructive sanctions can unravel cooperation and efficiency, especially when the sanction is credible and not prohibitively costly.

- source: De Cremer, D., & van Dijk, E. (2009). Paying for sanctions in social dilemmas: The effects of endowment asymmetry and accountability. *Organizational Behavior and Human Decision Processes*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr, reward_exists, show_punishment_id
  findings: The study finds that in a step-level public good dilemma, high-endowment members contribute proportionally more to the public good than low-endowment members, regardless of accountability. However, when it comes to paying for a sanctioning system (which is framed as a second-order, continuous public good dilemma without a threshold), high-endowment members do not contribute more than low-endowment members unless they are made accountable and expect to be morally evaluated by the group. Accountability and evaluation expectancy increase contributions to the sanctioning system among high-endowment members, but these effects are not observed for contributions to the public good itself. The paper does not report efficiency or group payoff as outcomes, so the effect of these manipulations on efficiency is not directly assessed.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence about the psychological and social moderators of willingness to pay for punishment systems in public good dilemmas, especially under endowment asymmetry. However, it does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes, so it cannot be used directly to predict the effect of punishment on efficiency. The findings suggest that accountability and evaluation expectancy can increase willingness to pay for sanctions among high-endowment members, but the translation of this effect to efficiency or group payoff is not established. For prediction tasks focused on efficiency, this paper is only indirectly informative, mainly about the behavioral mechanisms that might affect the implementation of punishment systems.

- source: Lokhorst, A. M., van Dijk, E., & Staats, H. (2009). Public commitment making as a structural solution in social dilemmas. *Journal of Environmental Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that both dispositional trust and situational expectations interact to predict willingness to invest in a public commitment system in a public good dilemma. High trusters are more willing to install a commitment system when situational expectations are low, while low trusters are more willing when situational expectations are high. The motive to maximize collective outcomes partially mediates this effect. However, the study does not measure actual contributions, group payoff, or efficiency, and thus does not provide direct evidence on the effect of commitment systems (or punishment) on efficiency or payoff outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games. It is relevant for understanding when participants are likely to support or invest in structural solutions (like public commitment systems), but does not report on actual efficiency, group payoff, or the effect of punishment or sanctions. The findings may be useful for understanding the adoption of commitment or sanctioning systems, but not for predicting their efficiency effects.

- source: Mulder, L. B., & Nelissen, R. M. A. (2010). When Rules Really Make a Difference: The Effect of Cooperation Rules and Self-Sacrificing Leadership on Moral Norms in Social Dilemmas. *Journal of Business Ethics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that installing a cooperation rule increases personal moral norms and cooperative behavior (less resource extraction, shorter breaks, fewer lottery tickets taken) only when the rule is installed by a self-sacrificing leader. If the leader is self-benefiting, rules only increase cooperation when behavior is monitored (i.e., when there is supervision). The effect of rules on cooperation is mediated by the instigation of moral norms, but only for self-sacrificing leaders. The studies do not report group efficiency or total payoff, so the findings are limited to behavioral and attitudinal outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence that the effectiveness of rules (and by extension, possibly punishment) in increasing cooperation depends on the perceived legitimacy and self-sacrifice of the authority figure, and on the presence of monitoring. However, because the studies do not report efficiency or group payoff, the findings cannot be directly used to predict efficiency outcomes in public goods games with or without punishment. The results suggest that rules (and possibly punishment) may be more effective in increasing cooperation (and thus potentially efficiency) when installed by a self-sacrificing leader or when monitoring is present, but this is not tested in terms of payoff or efficiency. The design dimensions most relevant here are the presence of rules, monitoring (supervision), and leader characteristics, but not punishment per se.

- source: Keller, J., & Pfattheicher, S. (2011). Vigilant Self-Regulation, Cues of Being Watched and Cooperativeness. *European Journal of Personality*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=weak | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds
  findings: The studies show that a cue of being watched (reputational cue) increases cooperative behavior (donation to a public good) among individuals with a strong prevention focus, but decreases it among those with a weak prevention focus. The effect is specific to prevention focus; promotion focus does not predict donation behavior. The manipulation is not punishment or sanctioning, but rather a reputational cue. The outcome is individual donation, not group efficiency or payoff. No repeated interaction, no explicit public goods game structure, and no punishment or reward mechanisms are present.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or their variants. The design is a one-shot donation game with a reputational cue, not a repeated PGG with punishment. The findings may be relevant for understanding how reputational cues (such as being watched) interact with individual differences to affect cooperation, but they do not inform the prediction of efficiency outcomes when punishment is enabled versus disabled. The absence of punishment, reward, or group payoff outcomes limits its direct utility for the downstream prediction task.

- source: Diekmann, A., & Przepiorka, W. (2016). Take One for the Team! Individual Heterogeneity and the Emergence of Latent Norms in a Volunteer's Dilemma. *Social Forces*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that in repeated volunteer's dilemma games, efficient public good provision emerges through different behavioral patterns depending on group structure. In symmetric groups with fixed partners, turn-taking emerges, leading to high efficiency and equal payoffs. In asymmetric groups, especially when the lowest-cost actor is much stronger or when roles are randomized each round, solitary volunteering by the strong actor dominates, also leading to high efficiency. When distributional conflict is present (fixed roles), efficiency is somewhat lower due to reluctance of strong actors to always volunteer. The paper does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanisms, and thus provides no evidence on the effect of punishment on efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments, as no punishment or sanctioning mechanism is present in any condition. However, it is informative about baseline efficiency levels in repeated volunteer's dilemma games under various structural conditions (symmetry/asymmetry, matching protocol). For prediction tasks, this paper can inform the expected efficiency in control (no-punishment) conditions for step-level public good games with similar parameters, but cannot inform the effect of enabling punishment. It is not directly useful for predicting treatment efficiency when punishment is enabled.

- source: Mak, V., Zwick, R., Rao, A. R., & Pattaratanakun, J. A. (2015). Pay what you want as threshold public good provision. *Organizational Behavior and Human Decision Processes*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The experiments demonstrate that in a repeated threshold public good environment, pre-payment chat (communication) among players enables highly efficient coordination, resulting in sustained provision of the public good (PWYW) for most rounds. Without chat, groups fail to coordinate and the public good is rarely sustained. The effect of chat is robust to variations in feedback (full vs. partial information about others' payments) and market information (knowledge of player types). The main outcome is group efficiency, operationalized as the proportion of rounds in which the threshold is met and the public good continues. There is no punishment or sanctioning mechanism; instead, efficiency is achieved through communication and social contracting.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in threshold public good games without punishment, enabling pre-play communication (chat) can dramatically increase group efficiency, even when other forms of information are limited. For prediction tasks, the presence of chat should be treated as a major moderator of efficiency in repeated threshold public good environments. The absence of punishment means the results are not directly informative about the effect of punishment per se, but they do establish a high-efficiency baseline for games with communication and no punishment. Control efficiency (no chat) is low; treatment efficiency (with chat) is high. Other design dimensions (player count, MPCR, etc.) are held constant, so the main actionable dimension is chat.

- source: Efferson, C., Roca, C. P., Vogt, S., & Helbing, D. (2016). Sustained cooperation by running away from bad behavior. *Evolution and Human Behavior*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: Allowing players to move away from their current neighborhood (endogenous movement) in a spatial social dilemma leads to higher cooperation rates and, crucially, eliminates the average payoff disadvantage of cooperation compared to defection. This is achieved through positive assortment: cooperators cluster together, shielding themselves from defectors. In the control condition (assign location), where movement is exogenous, cooperation remains costly and less frequent. The experiment does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanism, so the results are about migration/partner choice as a mechanism for sustaining cooperation and efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence about the effects of punishment or sanctions on efficiency in public goods games or their variants. Instead, it demonstrates that allowing migration/partner choice can eliminate the payoff disadvantage of cooperation, even without punishment. For prediction tasks about punishment effects, this paper is not directly informative, but it does highlight that mechanisms other than punishment (e.g., migration) can also support efficiency. If a game design includes migration but not punishment, this paper is highly relevant; if the focus is on punishment, it is not.

- source: Kameda, T., Tsukasaki, T., Hastie, R., & Berg, N. (2011). Democracy Under Uncertainty: The Wisdom of Crowds and the Free-Rider Problem in Group Decision Making. *Psychological Review*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that in group decision-making tasks with diminishing marginal returns (a nonlinear public goods environment), cooperation and free-riding coexist in a stable mixed equilibrium. In both simulation and laboratory experiment (with 6-person groups, 24 rounds, no punishment or reward), about half the group members consistently cooperate (paying a cost to acquire information and vote), while the rest free ride. The majority/plurality rule for aggregating decisions yields higher individual net payoffs (efficiency) than a best-member (dictator) rule, provided cooperation costs are not too high. The equilibrium level of cooperation is locally Pareto efficient, with three contributors and three free riders per group. The study demonstrates that efficient group outcomes can be achieved without punishment, provided the group production function is nonlinear with diminishing returns.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in public-goods-like environments with diminishing marginal returns and no punishment, stable and efficient cooperation can emerge. The findings suggest that the pessimistic predictions of linear public goods games (where efficiency collapses without punishment) do not generalize to more realistic nonlinear settings. For the downstream prediction task, this paper indicates that the efficiency of the control game (without punishment) can be high if the production function is nonlinear and the group decision rule is majoritarian, even in the absence of punishment. The paper does not provide evidence about the effect of enabling punishment, as no punishment or sanctioning mechanism is present in the design. Therefore, it should be used as a control or baseline for understanding efficiency in the absence of punishment, especially in nonlinear group production environments.

- source: Hergueux, J., Henry, E., Benkler, Y., & Algan, Y. (2023). Social Exchange and the Reciprocity Roller Coaster: Evidence from the Life and Death of Virtual Teams. *Organization Science*.
  type: empirical | empirical=observational | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=none | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The study links laboratory measures of reciprocity (from a one-shot public goods game) to field measures of reciprocity and project-level outcomes in open-source software teams. It finds that, while teams with more highly reciprocal members can achieve higher success scores (activity percentile) if they survive, they are also more likely to fail. The mechanism is that reciprocity amplifies both positive and negative contribution dynamics: highly reciprocal teams excel when cooperation is high, but are more fragile and prone to collapse after negative shocks or periods of inactivity. The study does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanism, and thus does not provide evidence on the effect of punishment on efficiency. The outcomes are group performance and survival, not direct PGG efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence on the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or their variants. Its findings are relevant for understanding the dynamics of reciprocity and cooperation in repeated public goods-like environments without punishment. The absence of punishment is a key design feature. The results suggest that, in the absence of punishment, high reciprocity can lead to both high performance and high fragility, but do not inform how enabling punishment would affect efficiency. Therefore, this paper should not be used to predict the effect of punishment on efficiency, but may be useful as a control case for environments without punishment.

- source: Chambers, C. R., & Baker, W. E. (2020). Robust Systems of Cooperation in the Presence of Rankings: How Displaying Prosocial Contributions Can Offset the Disruptive Effects of Performance Rankings. *Organization Science*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The experiment finds that introducing performance rankings tied to differential rewards causes a sharp and persistent decline in cooperation (measured as transfer rates) in a generalized reciprocity game. This effect is especially pronounced in groups that were highly cooperative before the intervention. When reputation information (about others' past giving) is also provided, the initial drop in cooperation is similar, but cooperation rates recover over time to pre-intervention levels. The mechanism appears to be that actors use reputation information to reward those who have been cooperative, especially when they perceive inequity. The study does not include punishment or sanctioning, and does not report efficiency or group payoff as a primary outcome.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence on the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-like games, as there is no punishment or sanctioning mechanism in the design. The findings are relevant for understanding how performance rankings and reputation information affect cooperation rates, but not for predicting efficiency changes due to punishment. The game is a close variant of a PGG, but the outcome of interest is cooperation/transfer rate, not efficiency or group payoff. Therefore, this paper should not be used as primary evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency, but may be informative for understanding the impact of competitive incentives and reputation mechanisms on cooperation.

- source: Tazelaar, M. J. A., Van Lange, P. A. M., & Ouwerkerk, J. W. (2004). How to cope with Noise, in social dilemmas: The benefits of communication. *Journal of Personality and Social Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that negative noise (randomly reducing the partner's intended contribution) significantly reduces cooperation and benign impressions in a repeated two-player give-some game. Allowing the partner to communicate about noise (explaining/apologizing for the noise) restores cooperation and benign impressions to levels indistinguishable from the no-noise baseline. These effects are especially pronounced for participants with low dispositional trust. The main outcomes are cooperation rates and psychological impressions, not efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or their close variants. It does, however, provide strong evidence that communication can mitigate the negative effects of noise on cooperation in repeated continuous-contribution dilemmas. Since no punishment or sanctioning is present, and efficiency or group payoff is not analyzed, this paper should not be used to predict the effect of punishment on efficiency. It may be useful for understanding the role of communication and noise, but not for the core prediction task regarding punishment.

- source: Barclay, P., & Barker, J. L. (2020). Greener Than Thou: People who protect the environment are more cooperative, compete to be environmental, and benefit from reputation. *Journal of Environmental Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that (1) people who engage in more pro-environmental behaviors are more likely to cooperate in a continuous Prisoner's Dilemma; (2) people donate more to an environmental charity when their donations are observed, and even more when competing to be chosen as a partner; (3) observers preferentially choose and cooperate more with those who donated more to the environment; (4) the correlation between environmentalism and cooperation is partially mediated by concern for others. The study does not include punishment or sanctions, and does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments, as it does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanism. It does, however, provide informative evidence about the effects of observability and partner choice (reputation/competition) on cooperative and pro-environmental behavior in a continuous Prisoner's Dilemma. For prediction tasks focused on punishment, this paper is not directly relevant. For prediction tasks involving the effects of observability or partner choice on cooperation, the findings suggest that such mechanisms can increase cooperative behaviors, but no efficiency or payoff data are available.

- source: Chung, D., Yun, K., & Jeong, J. (2015). Decoding covert motivations of free riding and cooperation from multi-feature pattern analysis of EEG signals. *Social Cognitive and Affective Neuroscience*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that neural oscillations measured by EEG immediately after result presentation can predict whether a participant will cooperate or free ride in the next round, across three different payoff structures that manipulate motivations (fear and greed). Behaviorally, free riding rates are highest in the standard condition, lower in the 'no fear' condition, and lowest in the 'no greed' condition. The study does not include punishment or reward mechanisms and does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in PGG-like games, as it does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanism, nor does it report efficiency or group payoff. It may provide some indirect context about how payoff structure (fear/greed) affects cooperation rates, but is not informative for the downstream prediction task focused on punishment effects on efficiency.

- source: Kirkland, K., Van Lange, P. A. M., Charalambous, N., Strauss, C., & Bastian, B. (2024). Beauty Builds Cooperation by Increasing Moral Concern for the Environment. *Environment and Behavior*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds
  findings: The studies show that when participants believe their extraction from a common pool will have negative consequences for the environment (e.g., less money to a tree preservation charity), they take fewer points, indicating more cooperative/conservation behavior. This effect is stronger when the environmental consequence is paired with a beautiful image of a tree. The effect is mediated by increased concern for tree preservation and reduced concern for personal financial gain. Manipulating the anthropomorphic qualities of trees (e.g., emphasizing mind-like traits) increases perceived mind but does not further increase cooperation. The studies do not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanisms, and do not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for the prediction of efficiency or payoff outcomes in public goods games with or without punishment. It does, however, provide informative evidence that non-monetary moral value and aesthetic framing can increase cooperative behavior in resource dilemma games similar to PGGs. Since no punishment or sanctioning is present, and no efficiency or group payoff is reported, the paper is not directly useful for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency. It may be useful for understanding how non-monetary framing or environmental consequences can shift extraction behavior, but not for the core prediction task involving punishment.

- source: Frey, U. J. (2017). Cooperative strategies outside the laboratory - evidence from a long-term large-N-study in five countries. *Evolution and Human Behavior*.
  type: empirical | empirical=observational | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, show_other_summaries
  findings: The study finds that in a context-rich, long-term, repeated threshold public goods environment (an online game), the distribution of cooperative strategies differs from laboratory public goods games: free riding is as common as in the lab, but high cooperation is less frequent and conditional cooperation is somewhat less common. These strategies are stable over time and across different group contexts. The results suggest that context matters for the prevalence of cooperative strategies, but do not provide evidence on efficiency or payoff outcomes, nor on the effects of punishment or sanctions.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as it does not include punishment or analyze efficiency or payoff outcomes. However, it is informative about the baseline prevalence and stability of cooperative strategies in a long-term, context-rich threshold public goods environment without punishment. For prediction tasks, it suggests that in the absence of punishment, free riding remains common, high cooperation is rare, and conditional cooperation is less prevalent than in some lab settings. The findings may help calibrate expectations for control (no-punishment) efficiency in similar environments, but do not inform the effect of enabling punishment.

- source: Balliet, D., & Van Lange, P. A. M. (2013). Trust, Conflict, and Cooperation: A Meta-Analysis. *Psychological Bulletin*.
  type: empirical | empirical=observational | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that both state trust (expectations of others' cooperation) and dispositional trust are positively correlated with cooperation in social dilemmas. The strength of this relationship is moderated by the degree of conflict in the game: trust is a stronger predictor of cooperation when the conflict of interest is higher (lower index of cooperation). The type of social dilemma (public goods, prisoner's dilemma, resource dilemma) does not significantly moderate the trust-cooperation relationship. Other moderators (iterations, group size, country, intergroup vs. individual) are also explored. The paper does not analyze or report on punishment, sanctions, or efficiency/welfare outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. It does, however, provide informative context about how the structure of the game (degree of conflict, as measured by the index of cooperation) moderates the relationship between trust and cooperation. Since efficiency is not measured, and punishment is not studied, this paper should not be used as direct evidence for the downstream prediction task, but may be useful for understanding how baseline cooperation varies with game structure in the absence of punishment.

- source: Ahmad, M. G., & Loch, C. (2020). What do the followers want? The core functions of leadership. *Leadership Quarterly*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that in repeated two-player strategic games, allowing players to choose a punishment option (where one can assign a low-value payoff to the other after decisions are made) does not increase group efficiency. Instead, punishment is associated with lower group payoffs due to retaliation cycles and reduced cooperation. In all games, the average group benefit under punishment is lower than under individual decision-making or leadership. Leadership (especially recommending leadership) increases efficiency, while punishment governance leads to the lowest efficiency. The negative effect of punishment on efficiency is robust across games with and without monetary incentives.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in two-player repeated games with public-goods-like social dilemmas, enabling punishment as a governance option (without central leadership) reduces group efficiency compared to control conditions without punishment. The effect is robust across different game structures and incentive types. For prediction tasks, the presence of a punishment option (with moderate cost and impact, as in this design) should be expected to decrease efficiency relative to control. The findings are directly relevant for settings where punishment is decentralized and not embedded in a leadership structure.

- source: Macleod, W. B., Lara, V. V., & Zehnder, C. (2025). Worker Empowerment and Subjective Evaluation: On Building an Effective Conflict Culture. *Management Science*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that the effect of enabling punishment (conflict) in a principal-agent game with subjective evaluation is highly context-dependent. In the baseline, communication, and agreement (code of conduct) experiments, allowing workers to punish managers for not paying bonuses does not increase efficiency: the costs of conflict outweigh any small increases in effort, and total surplus is lower or unchanged compared to the no-punishment condition. Workers and managers fail to coordinate on an efficiency-enhancing norm, and punishment is often misapplied. However, when conflicts are institutionalized through a formal grievance process (grievance experiment), workers and managers coordinate more on a pay-for-output norm, effort increases significantly, and total surplus is higher in the punishment-enabled condition. Thus, punishment can enhance efficiency, but only when supported by strong institutional framing and norm coordination.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the effect of enabling punishment on efficiency is not uniform and is highly sensitive to the institutional environment, especially the presence of mechanisms that coordinate norms (such as a grievance procedure). In environments lacking such coordination, enabling punishment is likely to reduce efficiency due to the direct costs of conflict. In contrast, when a formal grievance process is present, punishment can increase both effort and efficiency. For prediction tasks, the presence or absence of institutionalized conflict resolution and norm-coordination mechanisms should be considered a key moderator. Control efficiency (no-punishment) is not a reliable predictor of treatment efficiency (with punishment) unless the institutional context is specified.

- source: Zhao, Y. K., Zhao, X. B., & Shen, Z. J. M. (2018). The hot-versus-cold effect in a punishment game: a multi-round experimental study. *Annals of Operations Research*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=mixed | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The experiment compares two elicitation methods (strategy vs. direct-response) in a multi-round, two-player punishment game. The strategy method leads to significantly higher punishment rates (84% vs. 62%) and more frequent cooperative sender actions (S1: 68% vs. 44%). Combined payoffs (group welfare) are higher for S1 actions, and the higher frequency of S1 under the strategy method results in higher average group payoffs. Regression and learning model analyses show that the difference in punishment rates is due to mental accounting: the strategy method allows receivers to integrate the cost and expected benefit of punishment, increasing its perceived utility. Over repeated rounds, senders learn to avoid punishment by choosing S1 more often, further increasing group welfare. Reward rates are low and do not differ between methods. The findings suggest that, in repeated games, the strategic value of punishment (deterrence) is more important than the immediate emotional satisfaction of punishing, and that the design of the punishment mechanism (including decision timing) can significantly affect cooperation and group outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in repeated two-player punishment games, higher punishment rates (enabled by the strategy method) lead to more cooperative sender behavior and higher group payoffs. While the game is not a standard public goods game, the structure is adjacent: one player can take a selfish or cooperative action, and the other can punish. The results suggest that, in repeated settings, enabling effective punishment (with high perceived strategic value) increases efficiency by deterring selfish behavior. For prediction tasks, this implies that, all else equal, punishment mechanisms that increase the perceived strategic value of punishment (e.g., via decision timing or repeated interaction) will lead to higher efficiency, especially when the baseline (control) efficiency is low due to frequent selfish actions. The findings are most directly applicable to two-player, repeated, punishment-enabled games, but the mechanism may generalize to other repeated social dilemmas with punishment.

- source: Fehr, E., & Fischbacher, U. (2004). Third-party punishment and social norms. *Evolution and Human Behavior*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The experiments show that third-party punishment is common and increases with the severity of norm violations in both dictator games (distribution norm) and prisoner's dilemma games (cooperation norm). However, the magnitude of third-party punishment is lower than that of second-party punishment. In dictator games, third-party punishment reduces but does not eliminate the profitability of unfair transfers, while second-party punishment makes unfair transfers unprofitable. In prisoner's dilemma games, third-party punishment reduces the gains from defection but does not make cooperation strictly dominant, whereas second-party punishment does. Thus, punishment mechanisms—especially when strong or when multiple punishers are present—can increase efficiency by reducing the incentive to violate norms, but the effect is stronger for second-party punishment. The paper does not directly report group efficiency as a percentage of the maximum, but provides enough data to infer that punishment increases efficiency-related outcomes by reducing the profitability of norm violations.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the presence of punishment (especially second-party, but also third-party) increases the cost of norm violations and can shift incentives toward more efficient, norm-compliant outcomes. For prediction tasks, the findings suggest that enabling punishment (with sufficient strength or multiple punishers) will increase efficiency or group payoff relative to control conditions without punishment, but the effect size depends on the punishment's strength and whether it is second- or third-party. The design dimensions most relevant for prediction are the punishment cost/impact ratio, the number of punishers, and whether punishment is second- or third-party. The results are most directly applicable to dictator and prisoner's dilemma games, which are adjacent to but not exact public goods games. The paper does not provide direct evidence for standard PGGs, but the mechanisms and patterns are likely to generalize to PGG-like environments with similar punishment structures.

- source: SCHMITT, D. R., & MARWELL, G. (1970). REWARD AND PUNISHMENT AS INFLUENCE TECHNIQUES FOR ACHIEVEMENT OF COOPERATION UNDER INEQUITY. *Human Relations*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that in a dyadic task with unequal cooperative payoffs, the ability to reward (transfer money) is much more effective than the ability to punish (subtract money) in inducing and sustaining high rates of cooperation. Groups where the reward strategy was used spent more than twice as much time cooperating compared to those using punishment or no strategy. Communication between subjects further increased the use of rewards and the likelihood of achieving steady-state cooperation, particularly among males. Punishment, when used alone, was generally ineffective in producing cooperation, and its use declined over time. The study does not report direct measures of group efficiency or total earnings, but the behavioral outcomes suggest that reward-based influence is more conducive to sustained cooperation than punishment.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence about the effects of punishment and reward on cooperation in a setting adjacent to public goods games. The findings suggest that punishment alone is generally ineffective at inducing sustained cooperation, while reward (and especially the combination of reward and communication) is much more effective. However, because the study does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes, it cannot directly inform predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency. The design is dyadic, with asymmetric payoffs and the ability for only one player to reward or punish, which differs from standard PGGs. Thus, the main value for prediction is as a caution that punishment may not always increase cooperation or efficiency, especially when reward is also available and more salient.

- source: Whitson, J. A., Wang, C. S., See, Y. H. M., Baker, W. E., & Murnighan, J. K. (2015). How, when, and why recipients and observers reward good deeds and punish bad deeds. *Organizational Behavior and Human Decision Processes*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that third-party observers are more likely to punish bad deeds (e.g., deception) than direct recipients, and that direct recipients are more likely to reward good deeds (e.g., honesty) than observers. These effects are mediated by feelings of obligation: observers feel a stronger obligation to punish, while recipients feel a stronger obligation to reward. The studies use one-shot, controlled scenarios with real monetary stakes, but do not involve repeated interaction, group provision, or public goods. The main outcomes are behavioral (punishment/reward assigned), not group efficiency or welfare.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence about the psychological and behavioral drivers of punishment and reward in one-shot, dyadic or triadic settings, but does not provide direct evidence about the effect of punishment on group efficiency or welfare in public goods games or similar repeated social dilemmas. The findings suggest that the presence of observers may increase the likelihood and intensity of punishment for norm violations, but do not inform how this affects group payoffs or efficiency. For prediction tasks focused on efficiency in public goods games, this paper is only indirectly informative, mainly about who is likely to punish and under what circumstances, not about the payoff consequences of punishment.

- source: Guo, Y. C., Zhao, X. R., Liu, Y. B., & Ma, J. H. (2024). The effect of third-party intervention on normative behavior of bystanders: An explanation of the social norm cueing effect. *Personality and Individual Differences*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that both third-party punishment and third-party compensation, when observed by bystanders, increase the bystanders' fairness in subsequent dictator game allocations. This effect is mediated by changes in descriptive norm perceptions. The effect is present when the intervention is presented as group summary information (Study 1) or as an individual action perceived as representative of group behavior (Study 2). The study does not measure or report efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes, and the game is a dictator game, not a public goods game or its direct variant.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence that observing third-party punishment or compensation can increase norm-compliant behavior (fairness in allocation) among bystanders, mediated by changes in perceived descriptive norms. However, because the study uses a dictator game (not a public goods game) and does not report efficiency or group payoff, its direct value for predicting efficiency effects of punishment in public-goods-game-like environments is limited. The findings may be useful for understanding norm transmission and the psychological mechanisms by which punishment or compensation can affect behavior, but do not provide quantitative or direct evidence for efficiency outcomes in PGGs.

- source: Böckler, A., Tusche, A., & Singer, T. (2016). The Structure of Human Prosociality: Differentiating Altruistically Motivated, Norm Motivated, Strategically Motivated, and Self-Reported Prosocial Behavior. *Social Psychological and Personality Science*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The paper finds that prosocial behavior is multidimensional, with four robust factors: altruistically motivated, norm motivated (including punishment and reciprocity), strategically motivated, and self-reported prosociality. Punishment behavior (in second and third person punishment games) loads on the 'norm motivated' factor, indicating its role in norm enforcement. The study replicates standard findings in economic games (e.g., people punish unfairness, give more in dictator than in punishment games, help more when reciprocated), but does not analyze efficiency or group payoff. The factor structure is stable across two large samples. Altruistic prosociality is associated with lower negative affect and higher cognitive skills. Gender, age, and income show differential associations with the factors.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence that punishment behavior is a distinct, norm-motivated component of prosociality, separable from altruistic and strategic motivations. However, it does not report on efficiency, group payoff, or welfare, so it cannot directly inform predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods or similar games. The design details of the punishment games (one-shot, anonymous, fixed punishment effectiveness) are informative for mapping to game dimensions, but the lack of payoff or efficiency outcomes limits its direct predictive value for the downstream task. The study is most useful for understanding the motivational structure underlying punishment and cooperation, not for quantifying efficiency effects.

- source: Falk, A., Fehr, E., & Fischbacher, U. (2005). Driving forces behind informal sanctions. *Econometrica*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The study finds that in a three-player one-shot PD with punishment, cooperators predominantly punish defectors, and this pattern is robust across different punishment effectiveness conditions and elicitation methods. Defectors also punish, but their punishment is more likely to be spiteful and disappears when punishment cannot increase payoff differences. The cost-effectiveness of punishment is manipulated, showing that cooperators are willing to spend more to punish defectors even when punishment is less effective. The results challenge fairness theories that focus only on group-level payoffs or inequality aversion, supporting instead a model where retaliation against unfairness is central. However, the paper does not report on group efficiency or total welfare outcomes, focusing instead on behavioral and motivational aspects of punishment.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence on the behavioral patterns and motives of punishment in a three-player PD with punishment, including how punishment is targeted and how its cost-effectiveness affects behavior. However, it does not report group efficiency, total earnings, or welfare, so it cannot directly inform predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency. It does suggest that punishment is frequently used by cooperators against defectors, and that the willingness to punish persists even when it is costly, which may imply potential for efficiency improvements in similar games, but this is not tested or quantified here. The design details (player count, one-shot, punishment cost/effectiveness) are well specified and can inform mechanistic expectations, but not direct efficiency predictions.

- source: Baumgartner, T., Schiller, B., Rieskamp, J., Gianotti, L. R., & Knoch, D. (2014). Diminishing parochialism in intergroup conflict by disrupting the right temporo-parietal junction. *Social Cognitive and Affective Neuroscience*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The paper finds that third-party punishment is more severe toward outgroup norm violators than ingroup violators (parochial punishment), especially in cases where the outgroup member defects against an ingroup member. Disrupting the right TPJ with rTMS reduces this parochial punishment, making third-parties treat outgroup and ingroup violators more similarly. This effect is mediated by a reduction in the motive to retaliate and a reduction in mentalizing bias (the tendency to take the perspective of ingroup more than outgroup members). The manipulation does not affect punishment in intragroup constellations or in non-defection cases. The study does not measure or report group efficiency or total payoffs.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence about the psychological and neural mechanisms underlying parochial punishment in third-party punishment games, but does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes. The design is adjacent to public goods games, as it uses a Prisoner's Dilemma for norm violation and a third-party punishment stage, but does not involve repeated public goods contributions or measure group efficiency. The findings are relevant for understanding when and why punishment is more severe toward outgroup members, and how neural interventions can reduce this bias. However, the results cannot be directly used to predict the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as no efficiency or payoff data are reported. The punishment technology (1 point spent reduces target's payoff by 3) is specified, but the main outcomes are behavioral (punishment assigned) and motivational (retaliation, perspective-taking), not payoff-based.

- source: Crockett, M. J., Özdemir, Y., & Fehr, E. (2014). The Value of Vengeance and the Demand for Deterrence. *Journal of Experimental Psychology-general*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The study demonstrates that both retributive and deterrence motives drive costly punishment: participants punish norm violators even when the punishment is hidden and cannot deter future violations, but punishment is stronger when it can be communicated. Second-party punishment (when the punisher is the direct victim) is stronger than third-party punishment (when the punisher is an observer). The findings suggest that retributive motives can lead to punishment that does not increase social welfare and may be inefficient. However, the paper does not report on efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes directly.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that punishment can be motivated by retribution alone, leading to costly punishment even when it cannot improve future cooperation or group outcomes. For prediction tasks, this suggests that the presence of punishment does not guarantee increased efficiency or group payoff, especially when punishment is driven by retributive motives rather than deterrence. However, because the study does not report efficiency or group payoff, it cannot be used to quantitatively predict efficiency changes due to punishment. The design is adjacent to public goods games, using a trust game with punishment, so findings may transfer in terms of understanding motives but not in terms of efficiency outcomes.

- source: de Kwaadsteniet, E. W., Rijkhoff, S. A. M., & van Dijk, E. (2013). Equality as a benchmark for third-party punishment and reward: The moderating role of uncertainty in social dilemmas. *Organizational Behavior and Human Decision Processes*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists
