  findings: The paper finds that third-party punishers and rewarders use the equality rule as a benchmark for sanctioning decisions only when the size of the common resource is certain. Under certainty, those who take more than an equal share are punished much more, and those who take an equal or smaller share are rewarded more. Under uncertainty, the amount of punishment or reward is linearly related to the amount harvested, with no sharp benchmark. The emotional basis for punishment is negative affect (anger), while for reward it is positive affect. The study does not measure or report group efficiency, total earnings, or welfare.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency or group payoff in public goods or common resource games, as it does not report any payoff-based outcomes. However, it provides informative context about how the clarity of the cooperation norm (equality rule) and environmental uncertainty affect the application of punishment and reward. In settings where the cooperation norm is clear and known, punishment is more sharply targeted at norm violators; under uncertainty, punishment is more diffuse. This may indirectly inform predictions about the effectiveness of punishment in promoting cooperation or efficiency, but the paper does not provide direct evidence for such effects.

- source: Guo, Z. Y., Guo, R., Xu, C. Y., & Wu, Z. (2022). Reflexive or reflective? Group bias in third-party punishment in Chinese and Western cultures. *Journal of Experimental Social Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The study finds that both Chinese and Western participants show ingroup favoritism in third-party punishment under reflexive (fast, high cognitive load) conditions, punishing ingroup norm violators less than outgroup violators. Under reflective (slow, low cognitive load) conditions, Western participants—especially those with high group identity—reduce ingroup favoritism and may even punish ingroup violators more, while Chinese participants continue to show strong ingroup favoritism regardless of decision mode. Group identity amplifies these effects. The main outcome is the amount of punishment assigned, not efficiency or group payoff. The game is a third-party punishment mini-dictator game, not a standard public goods game, and does not report on group efficiency or welfare.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence about how group identity, culture, and decision-making mode moderate the behavioral use of costly punishment in a third-party punishment context. However, it does not report on efficiency, group payoff, or welfare, so it cannot directly inform predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. The design is adjacent to PGGs (mini-dictator/TPP), and the outcome is punishment behavior, not payoff. The findings are useful for understanding when and how punishment is used, but not for predicting efficiency outcomes.

- source: Li, X. H., Molleman, L., & van Dolder, D. (2021). Do descriptive social norms drive peer punishment? Conditional punishment strategies and their impact on cooperation. *Evolution and Human Behavior*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The experiments show that individuals' punishment of defectors is conditional on descriptive social norms: people punish more when cooperation or punishment is more common in a reference group. There is substantial heterogeneity: some punish independently, some increase punishment with higher cooperation (norm enforcement), and some with higher punishment (conformist). The dynamic model demonstrates that these conditional punishment strategies can facilitate the emergence and maintenance of high cooperation rates in populations. However, the paper does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes, so the direct effect of punishment on efficiency is not measured.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that punishment behavior is conditional on social context, and that such conditional punishment can promote cooperation in repeated social dilemmas. However, because the experiment is a one-shot prisoner's dilemma with punishment and does not report efficiency or group payoff, it does not provide direct quantitative evidence for predicting efficiency changes when punishment is enabled in public goods games. The dynamic model suggests that conditional punishment can help sustain high cooperation rates, which may imply higher efficiency in analogous settings, but this is an indirect inference. The paper is most useful for understanding behavioral mechanisms and heterogeneity in punishment, and for modeling the potential for punishment to support cooperation, but less so for direct efficiency prediction.

- source: Kurzban, R., DeScioli, P., & O'Brien, E. (2007). Audience effects on moralistic punishment. *Evolution and Human Behavior*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The presence of an audience (even just the experimenter) increases the frequency and amount of third-party punishment of norm violators in both Trust Game and sequential Prisoner's Dilemma Game settings. Under anonymous conditions, punishment is infrequent and small; when observed, punishment is more common and larger. The effect is strongest when the audience includes other participants. The study does not report on group efficiency, welfare, or total payoffs, and does not use a public goods game, but it demonstrates that reputational concerns strongly increase costly punishment behavior.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting efficiency outcomes in public goods games with or without punishment. However, it offers informative evidence that the salience of observation (audience effects) can substantially increase the willingness to engage in costly punishment. If a public goods game design includes features that make punishment decisions observable to others, one might expect higher rates of punishment, which could (in other contexts) affect cooperation and possibly efficiency. The lack of efficiency or payoff data limits direct applicability to the prediction task, but the findings are relevant for understanding moderators of punishment behavior.

- source: Deutchman, P., Aellen, M., Bogese, M., Bshary, R., Drayton, L., Gil, D., Martin, J., Pretot, L., Raihani, N., Santos, L. R., & Mcauliffe, K. (2023). Punishment is sensitive to outside options in humans but not in cleaner fish (Labroides dimidiatus). *Animal Behaviour*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that human participants are sensitive to the intent behind cheating when deciding to punish: they punish more when a partner chooses to cheat despite having a cooperative option. Cleaner fish, in contrast, do not show this sensitivity; male fish punish female partners at similar rates regardless of whether the female had a choice to cooperate or not. The main outcome is the likelihood or amount of punishment, not efficiency or group payoff. The human experiment is a novel economic game inspired by cleaner fish mutualism, not a standard public goods game, and does not report efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or their close variants, as it does not report efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes. Its main contribution is to show that human punishment is sensitive to intent (alternative options), while cleaner fish punishment is not. For the downstream prediction task, this paper is only indirectly relevant: it suggests that the psychological basis for punishment in humans includes sensitivity to intent, which may affect when and how punishment is used, but does not provide quantitative or qualitative evidence about efficiency effects of punishment in public goods games.

- source: Spadaro, G., Molho, C., Van Prooijen, J. W., Romano, A., Mosso, C. O., & Van Lange, P. A. M. (2023). Corrupt third parties undermine trust and prosocial behaviour between people. *Nature Human Behaviour*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: Across five experimental studies, participants exposed to a corrupt (dishonest) third-party punisher (TPP) trusted strangers less and cooperated less in subsequent economic games (trust game, prisoner's dilemma, dictator game) with TPP present. The effect is robust to both second-hand and first-hand exposure to corruption, and to whether the TPP's corruption harms only the experiment or also a collective good. The negative effect of perceived TPP corruption on cooperation is mediated by reduced interpersonal trust. The studies do not report group efficiency or total payoff, so the direct effect of TPP corruption on efficiency is not measured. The design always includes a TPP; there is no comparison to a no-punishment baseline, so the effect of enabling punishment per se on efficiency cannot be determined.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the perceived integrity of a third-party punisher moderates the effectiveness of punishment institutions in promoting trust and cooperation. However, it does not provide direct evidence on the effect of enabling punishment (vs. no punishment) on group efficiency or payoff, nor does it report efficiency outcomes. The findings suggest that if punishment is administered by corrupt or untrusted third parties, its positive effect on cooperation may be undermined or even reversed, but the paper does not quantify efficiency. For prediction tasks, this paper is most informative about the moderating role of institutional trustworthiness, not about the baseline effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods or similar games.

- source: Goette, L., Huffman, D., Meier, S., & Sutter, M. (2012). Competition Between Organizational Groups: Its Impact on Altruistic and Antisocial Motivations. *Management Science*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The experiment shows that in a neutral environment, punishment is used prosocially to enforce cooperation, especially when the victim of defection is an in-group member. When competition between groups is introduced, punishment becomes antisocial: out-group members are punished more, regardless of whether they cooperate or defect. Competition increases in-group cooperation but does not reduce out-group cooperation. The paper does not report group efficiency or total payoff, but the authors argue that antisocial punishment under competition is wasteful and could reduce overall efficiency, while increased in-group cooperation could enhance it. The net effect on efficiency is not measured.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the presence of intergroup competition can fundamentally change the function of punishment in social dilemma games: from norm enforcement to antisocial, destructive punishment. While the design is not a standard repeated public goods game, the findings are highly relevant for predicting when punishment will be efficiency-enhancing versus efficiency-reducing. Specifically, in environments with salient group competition, enabling punishment may lead to resource-destroying antisocial punishment, undermining the efficiency gains typically associated with punishment. However, the lack of direct efficiency or payoff data means the findings should be used as qualitative guidance rather than quantitative prediction.

- source: Krasnow, M. M., Delton, A. W., Cosmides, L., & Tooby, J. (2016). Looking Under the Hood of Third-Party Punishment Reveals Design for Personal Benefit. *Psychological Science*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that third-party punishment is driven by the punisher's inference that a dictator who mistreats a recipient is also likely to mistreat the punisher. When punishers have information about how they themselves are treated, this information dominates their punishment decisions, and information about third-party treatment is largely ignored. The results support a deterrence-based account of punishment motivation. The study does not report or analyze efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency or group payoff in public-goods-like games. Its findings are relevant for understanding the psychological motivations for punishment, especially the conditions under which punishment is likely to be deployed, but it does not address whether punishment increases or decreases group efficiency. The design is a modified dictator/third-party-punishment game, not a standard public goods game, and no efficiency or total payoff outcomes are reported. Thus, it offers little direct support for the downstream prediction task focused on efficiency.

- source: Li, J. B., Pan, J. J., Zhu, C. K., & Wang, Y. W. (2022). Inter-brain synchronization is weakened by the introduction of external punishment. *Social Cognitive and Affective Neuroscience*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The introduction of external punishment (i.e., making responder rejection payoff-relevant in the rrUG) leads to significantly higher mean offers from proposers compared to the rrDG (no punishment). However, the main focus is on neural outcomes: inter-brain synchronization is significantly lower in the punishment condition (rrUG) than in the no-punishment condition (rrDG). There is no significant effect of punishment on the rejection rate of responders. The study interprets the neural results as evidence that punishment crowds out moral motivation, reducing shared cognitive/emotional processing between players. No direct measures of group efficiency, total earnings, or welfare are reported.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence that introducing punishment (via responder rejection in a two-player ultimatum game) increases proposer generosity (mean offer size), which could be associated with higher group payoffs if responders accept. However, the study does not report group efficiency, total earnings, or welfare, so it cannot be used directly to predict efficiency outcomes. The design is adjacent to public goods games but is not a PGG or close variant. The main outcome is neural (inter-brain synchronization), not payoff-based. Thus, the paper offers only weak, indirect support for predicting efficiency effects of punishment in PGG-like environments.

- source: Doering, L., & Ody-Brasier, A. (2021). Time and Punishment: How Individuals Respond to Being Sanctioned in Voluntary Associations. *American Journal of Sociology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=observational | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that the effect of being sanctioned (fined) for absence in voluntary microsavings groups changes over time. Early in group tenure, sanctioned individuals reduce their prosocial contributions to the collective fund, but as they become more dependent on the group (measured by time in the group or by having received emergency funds), their response to sanctions becomes increasingly prosocial, with higher allocations to the collective fund after being fined. The effect is robust to various controls and alternative specifications. The study does not measure group efficiency or total group payoff, but the findings suggest that the effectiveness of punishment in promoting prosocial behavior is temporally and relationally dependent.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence that the effect of punishment on prosocial behavior (here, contributions to a collective fund) is not static but depends on group tenure and individual dependence on the group. For prediction tasks involving efficiency or payoff in public-goods-like environments, this suggests that the impact of punishment may be weaker or even negative early in group formation, but can become more positive as group members become more dependent on each other. However, because the outcome is not efficiency or group payoff, but rather individual prosocial allocation, the findings should be transferred to efficiency prediction with caution. The study is most informative about the temporal dynamics and social context moderating punishment effects, rather than about the direct effect of punishment on efficiency.

- source: Delton, A. W., & Krasnow, M. M. (2017). The psychology of deterrence explains why group membership matters for third-party punishment. *Evolution and Human Behavior*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that third-party punishment is strongly influenced by group membership: punishers assign the most punishment when an outgroup dictator treats an ingroup recipient poorly, and the least when an ingroup dictator treats an outgroup recipient poorly. This pattern is best explained by deterrence psychology, where punishers infer that outgroup dictators who mistreat ingroup members are likely to mistreat the punisher as well. The group norm maintenance theory (fairness or parochialism norms) does not fit the observed punishment patterns. The study does not report efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes, focusing instead on punishment behavior and inferences about personal valuation.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides high-quality evidence about the psychological mechanisms underlying third-party punishment and how group membership moderates punishment behavior. However, it does not report efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes, so it cannot directly inform predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods or similar games. The findings are most useful for understanding when and why punishment is likely to be assigned, especially in relation to group membership, but not for predicting efficiency changes due to punishment interventions.

- source: Bone, J. E., & Raihani, N. J. (2015). Human punishment is motivated by both a desire for revenge and a desire for equality. *Evolution and Human Behavior*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that punishment is motivated by both a desire for revenge (punishment occurs even when it cannot restore equality) and a desire for equality (when possible, punishment is tailored to equalize payoffs). Punishment is more likely when stealing creates disadvantageous inequality and when punishment is effective. However, the outcomes measured are behavioral (punishment frequency and amount), not efficiency or group payoff. The experiment is a two-player, one-shot, anonymous game with no communication, and does not use a public goods game or its direct variants.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence about how punishment behavior is shaped by the effectiveness of punishment and the presence of inequality, but does not provide direct evidence about the effect of punishment on efficiency or group payoff in public goods games or similar multi-player settings. The findings may inform predictions about when punishment will be used and how it will be targeted, especially regarding the importance of punishment effectiveness (fee-to-fine ratio) and inequality, but cannot be used to quantitatively predict efficiency outcomes. The design is adjacent to PGGs and does not report efficiency or welfare, so its value for the prediction task is limited to qualitative insights about punishment motivation and targeting.

- source: Shinada, M., & Yamagishia, T. (2007). Punishing free riders: direct and indirect promotion of cooperation. *Evolution and Human Behavior*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The experiment shows that third-party punishment is group-based: cooperators in the gift-giving game punish in-group cheaters more severely than out-group cheaters, while noncooperators show the opposite pattern. Most punishment is directed at the least cooperative group members. Emotional responses (anger, unfairness, guilt) are correlated with punishment of in-group cheaters but not out-group cheaters. The design uses a gift-giving game with a public-goods-like structure, but does not directly measure or report group efficiency or total payoff outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence about the behavioral allocation of punishment in group-structured social exchange games, especially regarding in-group vs. out-group targeting. However, it does not report efficiency or group payoff, so it cannot directly inform predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-like games. The design and findings are most useful for understanding the social and motivational determinants of punishment, not its impact on group welfare or efficiency. The punishment mechanism (costly to punisher, 3x impact on target) and the absence of feedback or repeated interaction are relevant for mapping to other designs, but the lack of efficiency data limits direct predictive value for the downstream task.

- source: Cheung, G., & Siegel, D. A. (2025). The Costs of Collective Action: An Experimental Study of Risk, Punishment, and Immunity. *Journal of Politics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The experiment finds that higher punishment risk and cost reduce participation and group success among nonimmune individuals. Immune individuals are more likely to participate, but their participation is not affected by punishment risk or cost. As the number of immune individuals increases, nonimmune individuals participate less, consistent with free riding. Group size has no significant effect on nonimmune participation or group success, but immune individuals participate more in larger groups. The main outcomes are participation and group success, not efficiency or total group payoff. Average payoffs are reported, but not analyzed as a function of the experimental treatments. The findings support the theoretical model's predictions about the effects of punishment risk, cost, immunity, and group size on participation and collective action success.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides informative evidence about how external punishment risk and cost reduce participation and group success in a collective action setting, and how immunity and group size moderate these effects. However, the game is not a standard public goods game: the group benefit is all-or-nothing and depends on a random pivotal actor, and only participants can be punished by an external agent. There is no within-group punishment or contribution to a divisible public good. Efficiency as defined in public goods games (total group payoff relative to full cooperation) is not directly analyzed, though average payoffs and group success rates are reported. The results are most relevant for predicting how external punishment risk and cost affect participation and group-level success in threshold-like or all-or-nothing collective action settings, especially when some individuals are immune to punishment. The findings are less directly transferable to standard PGG efficiency predictions, but may inform models where external sanctions or risk of punishment for cooperation are present.

- source: FeldmanHall, O., Otto, A. R., & Phelps, E. A. (2018). Learning Moral Values: Another's Desire to Punish Enhances One's Own Punitive Behavior. *Journal of Experimental Psychology-general*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: Across four experiments, participants who learned about another's strong preference for punishment increased their own endorsement of punitive options when responding to unfair offers. This effect was observed both when participants actively implemented another's preferences and when they passively observed them. The effect generalized across online and lab settings, and from a justice game (with compensatory and punitive options) to the ultimatum game (where only costly punishment is available). The learning process is best described by a reinforcement learning model sensitive to the magnitude of unfairness. However, the paper does not report on group efficiency, total earnings, or welfare; the focus is on individual behavioral change in punitive choices.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that exposure to others' punitive preferences increases individuals' own willingness to punish in response to unfairness, even when punishment is costly. However, it does not report on efficiency or payoff outcomes, so it cannot directly inform predictions about the effect of punishment on group efficiency in public goods or similar games. The design is dyadic and focused on justice restoration, not multi-player public goods provision. The findings are most relevant for predicting changes in punitive behavior, not efficiency.

- source: Yudkin, D. A., Rothmund, T., Twardawski, M., Thalla, N., & Van Bavel, J. J. (2016). Reflexive Intergroup Bias in Third-Party Punishment. *Journal of Experimental Psychology-general*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: Across three experiments, participants assigned to a third-party role punished out-group transgressors more harshly than in-group transgressors, especially under conditions promoting reflexive judgment (fast response or cognitive load). The effect is robust across different group identity manipulations (sports teams, nationality). The punishment is costless to the punisher and only removes money from the transgressor. The study does not analyze or report group efficiency, total earnings, or welfare. The findings are about behavioral bias in punishment assignment, not about the impact of punishment on cooperation or efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence about the psychological determinants of punishment assignment (intergroup bias, reflexivity) in a third-party punishment context, but does not provide data on efficiency, group payoff, or the effect of punishment on cooperation. The game structure is adjacent to public goods games but is a one-shot, three-player 'stealing and punishment' scenario with costless punishment. Thus, the findings are not directly informative for predicting efficiency outcomes in public-goods-game-like environments with or without punishment. The main relevance is for understanding when and how punishment is assigned, not its effect on group welfare.

- source: Wang, C. S., Sivanathan, N., Narayanan, J., Ganegoda, D. B., Bauer, M., Bodenhausen, G. V., & Murnighan, K. (2011). Retribution and emotional regulation: The effects of time delay in angry economic interactions. *Organizational Behavior and Human Decision Processes*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: Across five lab experiments, the authors show that introducing a time delay before making punishment decisions (e.g., rejecting unfair offers or punishing norm violators) generally reduces costly punishment, but only if the delay is accompanied by distraction or cognitive reappraisal. If participants ruminate during the delay, punishment increases. The effect is mediated by negative emotions (anger). The experiments use one-shot or single-decision ultimatum games and third-party punishment trust games, with punishment measured as rejection rates or points spent. No direct group efficiency or total payoff outcomes are reported, but the reduction in costly punishment implies less wasteful destruction of resources. The findings highlight the importance of emotional regulation in moderating punitive impulses.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence relevant to predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-like environments. It shows that the context and psychological state of punishers (e.g., time delay, distraction, rumination) can strongly moderate the frequency and magnitude of costly punishment. While the games are not public goods games, the results suggest that interventions reducing negative emotion or promoting reappraisal/distraction could reduce inefficient punishment and thus improve group payoffs. However, because the paper does not report efficiency or group payoff directly, and the games are not repeated or multi-player PGGs, the findings should be used as informative but indirect evidence for prediction tasks.

- source: Gambetta, D., & Przepiorka, W. (2019). Sharing Compromising Information as a Cooperative Strategy. *Sociological Science*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The experiment shows that when a punishment mechanism is available (i.e., when norm-enforcement is possible), cooperation among less cooperative types ('hawks') increases, especially if both parties can mutually reveal their compromising information and thus make themselves vulnerable to punishment. In conditions without punishment, hawks rarely cooperate with each other. The effect is strongest when both the revelation of compromising information and the ability to punish are present and mutually chosen. The main outcomes are behavioral (cooperation, defection, information sharing), not efficiency or group payoff. The results suggest that punishment opportunities can enable cooperation among otherwise uncooperative individuals, but the paper does not quantify the effect on group efficiency or total earnings.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence that introducing a punishment mechanism in a trust-game-like environment can increase cooperation among less cooperative individuals, especially when mutual vulnerability is possible. However, because the main outcomes are behavioral (not efficiency or group payoff), the findings should be used as indirect support for the prediction task. The game is a one-shot trust game variant, not a standard repeated public goods game, and efficiency is not directly measured. The punishment mechanism is costly and only available in certain conditions. The results suggest that punishment can increase cooperation, but the magnitude of efficiency gains (if any) is not reported.

- source: Koenig, B. L., & Riley, C. M. (2017). To what reference point do people calibrate cost-free, third-party punishment?. *Personality and Individual Differences*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists, reward_cost, reward_tech, show_punishment_id
  findings: Across three experiments, participants assigned preferred punishments (fines) to a perpetrator in a theft scenario, with victim loss and perpetrator gain manipulated independently. Both victim loss and perpetrator gain influenced punishment preferences, with participants tending to select the larger of the two as a reference point. On average, preferred punishments exceeded the sum of victim loss and perpetrator gain. A substantial minority of participants preferred punishments much larger than this outcome differential, while a small minority preferred no punishment. When asked about compensation for the victim (rather than punishment for the perpetrator), participants' preferred amounts were lower and rarely exceeded the outcome differential. The study demonstrates that third-party punishment preferences are not simply proportional to harm or gain, but often exceed both, and that there is considerable heterogeneity in punitive preferences.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting efficiency or group payoff in public goods games with or without punishment. However, it is informative about the psychological calibration of punishment preferences in third-party contexts, showing that people often prefer punishments that more than offset both victim loss and perpetrator gain. This suggests that, in environments where third-party punishment is possible and costless, punishment levels may be set high, potentially deterring transgressions more strongly than would be predicted by models based solely on harm or gain. The findings are most relevant for understanding the psychological underpinnings of punishment, not for quantitative prediction of efficiency in PGG-like games.

- source: Lönnqvist, J. E., Rilke, R. M., & Walkowitz, G. (2015). On why hypocrisy thrives: Reasonable doubt created by moral posturing can deter punishment. *Journal of Experimental Social Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that in ultimatum games, proposers can reduce the likelihood of being punished for unfair offers by claiming that a random process (coin flip) determined the outcome. This effect holds even when responders do not believe the claim and is observed for both direct victims (responders) and third-party observers (judges). The reduction in punishment is not explained by responders' self-interest or gratitude. The studies do not report on group efficiency or total earnings, but the findings suggest that moral posturing can deter punishment and thus may allow unfair behavior to persist.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the presence of plausible deniability (moral posturing) can reduce the frequency of punishment in bargaining games, even when punishment is available and even if the claim is not believed. However, the study does not report on efficiency or group payoff, so it does not directly inform predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods or similar games. The findings are most relevant for understanding moderators of punishment behavior, especially the role of uncertainty or doubt about intent, rather than the direct effect of punishment on efficiency.

- source: Nelissen, R. M. A. (2008). The price you pay: cost-dependent reputation effects of altruistic punishment. *Evolution and Human Behavior*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_other_summaries, show_punishment_id
  findings: The main finding is that individuals who invest more in costly third-party punishment are perceived as more fair and trustworthy, and are subsequently entrusted with more money in a trust game. This effect is contingent on the cost being a reliable signal (i.e., known relative to endowment). The effect is mediated by perceptions of fairness, not by general likability or generosity. The study does not measure group efficiency or public goods provision, but rather the reputational and trust consequences for punishers.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on group efficiency in public goods games. However, it does provide indirect evidence that costly punishment can enhance the punisher's reputation and increase trust from others, but only when the cost is interpretable as a reliable signal. For prediction tasks focused on group efficiency in PGGs, this paper is only adjacent: it suggests that punishment may have reputational benefits for the punisher, which could, in theory, affect cooperation and efficiency in repeated or reputation-sensitive environments, but it does not test this directly. The design and outcomes are not directly transferable to PGG efficiency prediction.

- source: Perugini, M., Gallucci, M., Presaghi, F., & Ercolani, A. P. (2003). The personal norm of reciprocity. *European Journal of Personality*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: punishment_cost, reward_exists, reward_cost
  findings: The paper finds that individual differences in positive and negative reciprocity (as measured by the PNR questionnaire) predict how people allocate money to others in response to fair or unfair prior actions, both in hypothetical and incentivized settings. Positive reciprocity predicts greater rewarding of positive behavior, while negative reciprocity predicts greater punishment of negative behavior. These effects are robust across cultures (Italy and UK) and are distinct from general prosocial orientation or Big Five personality traits. However, the studies do not directly examine group efficiency, total welfare, or the effect of punishment on group-level outcomes in repeated public goods or social dilemma games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence that individual differences in the propensity to punish or reward (reciprocity norms) influence how people respond to others' actions in allocation games, including costly punishment and reward. However, it does not provide direct evidence about the effect of punishment on group efficiency or welfare in public goods games or similar repeated social dilemmas. The findings are most useful for understanding heterogeneity in punishment/reward behavior, not for predicting efficiency outcomes as a function of game design dimensions. The allocation tasks are adjacent to PGGs but lack repeated group interaction, explicit MPCR, or group efficiency measures.

- source: Mathew, S., & Boyd, R. (2014). The cost of cowardice: punitive sentiments towards free riders in Turkana raids. *Evolution and Human Behavior*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_punishment_id
  findings: The study finds that Turkana participants are significantly more likely to express punitive sentiments (both direct and indirect) toward hypothetical warriors who free ride (display cowardice) in combat, compared to those who fail to contribute due to inability (lack of skill). Direct sanctions include criticism and corporal punishment, while indirect sanctions include withholding social support, lending, and marriage opportunities. The findings support the idea that both direct punishment and indirect reciprocity are important mechanisms for sustaining large-scale cooperation in acephalous societies. However, the study does not measure actual payoffs, group efficiency, or the effect of punishment on group outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence that punitive motivations toward free riders are present in a real-world, high-stakes collective action context, and that both direct and indirect sanctions are socially endorsed. However, because the study does not measure efficiency, group payoff, or actual behavioral outcomes in a controlled game, it does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or their variants. The findings are most useful for understanding the psychological and social underpinnings of punishment in collective action, rather than for quantitative prediction of efficiency outcomes based on game design dimensions.

- source: Fitouchi, L., & Singh, M. (2023). Punitive justice serves to restore reciprocal cooperation in three small-scale societies. *Evolution and Human Behavior*.
  type: empirical | empirical=observational | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_punishment_id
  findings: Across three small-scale societies, punitive justice is primarily restorative and dyadic, not group-norm-enforcing. Third-party punishment is rare, and most interventions are aimed at mediating disputes and restoring relationships. Compensation to victims is common and often proportional to harm, especially among the Mentawai and Nuer. Kinship and interdependence reduce the likelihood or magnitude of punishment/compensation. When costs are not imposed on offenders, cooperation often breaks down and feuding may result. The paper does not use public goods games or measure efficiency, but provides evidence that punishment and compensation are more about restoring dyadic cooperation than increasing group-level efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect, contextual evidence about the function of punishment in environments that are adjacent to public goods games: real-world small-scale societies with reciprocal obligations and norm violations. It suggests that, in such contexts, punishment is not primarily about increasing group efficiency or enforcing group-level cooperation, but about restoring dyadic relationships and preventing escalation. For prediction tasks involving efficiency in public goods games, this paper suggests that the effect of punishment may depend on whether the context is more about dyadic reciprocity or group-level cooperation. However, because the paper does not use experimental PGGs or report efficiency or payoff outcomes, its direct predictive value for efficiency outcomes in PGGs is limited.

- source: Dhaliwal, N. A., Skarlicki, D. P., Hoegg, J., & Daniels, M. A. (2022). Consequentialist Motives for Punishment Signal Trustworthiness. *Journal of Business Ethics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, show_punishment_id
  findings: The paper finds that individuals who express or are perceived to have consequentialist motives for punishing norm violators are rated as more trustworthy, are entrusted with more money in Trust Game scenarios, and are chosen more often as cooperation partners compared to those with deontological motives. This effect is mediated by lower perceived psychopathy. The findings are robust across different phrasings of motives, experimental vignettes, and real-world recall. However, the studies do not examine actual group cooperation rates, efficiency, or payoff-based outcomes in public goods or similar games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on group efficiency or payoff in public goods games or similar environments. Its findings are relevant for understanding how the rationale for punishment affects the punisher's reputation and trustworthiness, which may indirectly influence cooperation, but it does not report on efficiency, group payoff, or welfare. Therefore, it should not be used as primary evidence for predicting efficiency outcomes from game design dimensions or control efficiency. It may be useful for understanding psychological mechanisms or for modeling how punishment motives affect trust and partner selection.

- source: Price, M. E., Cosmides, L., & Tooby, J. (2002). Punitive sentiment as an anti-free rider psychological device. *Evolution and Human Behavior*.
  type: empirical | empirical=observational | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: reward_exists
  findings: The study finds that individuals' willingness to participate in a collective action (e.g., military draft) is a strong predictor of their desire to punish nonparticipants (free riders), but not of their desire to reward participants. Perceived self-interest in the group goal predicts pro-reward sentiment but not punitive sentiment, once willingness to participate is controlled for. The results suggest that punitive sentiment is a specialized psychological adaptation for eliminating the fitness advantage of free riders, rather than for recruiting labor or maximizing group efficiency. The study does not measure or report efficiency, payoff, or actual behavioral outcomes in a public goods game or similar environment.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency or payoff in public goods games or similar experimental settings. Its findings are based on survey responses to hypothetical scenarios, not on observed behavior or payoffs. However, it does provide indirect support for the idea that punitive sentiment is psychologically linked to participation in collective action, and that punishment may function to deter free riding. For the prediction task, this paper is only weakly informative, as it does not report efficiency or payoff outcomes, nor does it manipulate or measure the game design dimensions relevant to public goods games.

- source: Dhaliwal, N. A., Patil, I., & Cushman, F. (2021). Reputational and cooperative benefits of third-party compensation. *Organizational Behavior and Human Decision Processes*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=weak
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The main finding is that third-party compensation leads to greater reputational and partner choice benefits than third-party punishment, as judged by observers in hypothetical scenarios and vignettes. Compensators are seen as more moral, trustworthy, and are more often chosen as partners in economic games (Trust Game, Dictator Game). These effects are robust across different contexts, including workplace and real-life vignettes. The paper also finds that compensators tend to have lower Dark Triad personality traits. However, the paper does not report on efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes, and does not provide evidence on how punishment affects efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency or group payoff in public goods or third-party punishment games. Its findings are relevant for understanding reputational and partner choice consequences of punishment versus compensation, but not for predicting efficiency outcomes. The game design dimensions are described in detail for the hypothetical games, but the dependent variables are not payoff-based. Therefore, this paper should not be used as primary evidence for predicting treatment efficiency from game design dimensions plus control efficiency.

- source: Thielmann, I., & Hilbig, B. E. (2018). Daring dishonesty: On the role of sanctions for (un)ethical behavior. *Journal of Experimental Social Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=weak
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study shows that increasing the probability of being checked for dishonesty and increasing the monetary penalty for being caught both substantially reduce the rate of cheating in individual tasks. In Experiment 1, tripling the probability of being checked (from 16.7% to 50%) nearly halved the estimated cheating rate. In Experiment 2, increasing both the probability of being checked and the penalty for being caught further reduced cheating, with the lowest dishonesty observed when both risk and severity were high. The results support utility-based theories of dishonesty, showing that external costs (sanctions) deter unethical behavior. However, the study does not measure or report efficiency, group payoff, or welfare, and the design is not a public goods game or a direct analog.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that increasing the risk and severity of monetary sanctions reduces individual cheating in private reporting tasks. While the findings are highly relevant for understanding the deterrent effect of punishment on norm violations, they do not directly inform predictions about efficiency or group payoff in public goods games or similar group cooperation settings. The results may be indirectly informative for predicting that punishment mechanisms with higher detection probability and higher penalty severity are more likely to reduce norm violations, but the transfer to efficiency outcomes in PGG-like environments is limited by the lack of group interaction, contribution, or payoff aggregation in the study.

- source: Eriksson, K., Stringing, P., Andersson, P. A., & Lindholm, T. (2017). Costly punishment in the ultimatum game evokes moral concern, in particular when framed as payoff reduction. *Journal of Experimental Social Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=weak
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: Across a series of online experiments, the authors find that framing costly punishment as 'reduction' (explicitly paying to reduce the other's payoff) leads to less frequent use of punishment, greater moral concern about using punishment, and a stronger association with punitive motives, compared to the standard 'rejection' framing in the ultimatum game. The reduction frame is more likely to be seen as immoral and as punishment, while rejection is more often seen as a quest for fairness. The study does not measure or report group efficiency, total payoff, or welfare, so it does not directly inform the effect of punishment on efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence about the effect of punishment on efficiency or payoff in public goods games or their close variants. Its findings are limited to the ultimatum game, which is structurally adjacent but not equivalent to the public goods game. The main outcomes are behavioral (frequency of punishment, motives, moral judgments), not payoff-based. Therefore, this paper should not be used to predict the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, except as a caution that framing and perceived morality can influence the use of punishment. It does, however, highlight that the way punishment is framed can substantially affect its use, which may be relevant for interpreting behavioral outcomes in other game contexts.

- source: Przepiorka, W., & Liebe, U. (2016). Generosity is a sign of trustworthiness-the punishment of selfishness is not. *Evolution and Human Behavior*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=weak
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The experiment finds that individuals who punish selfishness in a dictator game are not more trustworthy nor more trusted in a subsequent trust game, regardless of whether punishment is second-party or third-party. In fact, second-party punishers are trusted less than non-punishers. Generosity in the dictator game, however, is a strong predictor of trustworthiness and is perceived as such by others. The study does not report on efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes, focusing instead on behavioral and belief-based measures.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency or group payoff in public-goods-game-like environments, as it uses a binary dictator game with punishment and a trust game, and reports only on behavioral outcomes (punishment, generosity, trust, trustworthiness, beliefs). The findings suggest that punishment, at least in this context, does not enhance trust or trustworthiness, and by implication may not improve efficiency, but this is not directly tested. The design and results may be informative for understanding the signaling value of punishment versus generosity, but not for quantitative prediction of efficiency changes due to punishment in PGGs.

- source: Salcedo, J. C., & Jimenez-Leal, W. (2024). Severity and deservedness determine signalled trustworthiness in third party punishment. *British Journal of Social Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=weak
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: Across four experiments, the paper finds that (1) third-party punishment signals trustworthiness to observers, but only when the punishment is deserved and appropriately severe; (2) the trustworthiness signal increases with punishment severity, but only when higher severity entails higher cost to the punisher; (3) when punishment severity is increased without increasing cost, the trustworthiness signal does not increase; (4) refraining from punishing when punishment is undeserved can also signal trustworthiness, sometimes more than punishing. The main outcome is the amount entrusted to the punisher in a subsequent trust game, not group efficiency or total payoffs.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on group efficiency or payoff in public goods games or their close variants. Its findings are relevant for understanding how punishment is perceived and how it affects trust in subsequent interactions, but not for predicting changes in group efficiency when punishment is enabled. The experimental design is adjacent to PGGs (using TPP and trust games), and the main outcomes are behavioral (trust, partner choice) rather than payoff-based. Therefore, this paper should not be used as primary evidence for predicting efficiency effects of punishment in PGG-like environments, but may be informative for understanding reputational and signaling mechanisms.

- source: Deutchman, P., Bracic, M., Raihani, N., & McAuliffe, K. (2021). Punishment is strongly motivated by revenge and weakly motivated by inequity aversion. *Evolution and Human Behavior*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=weak
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: Punishment is primarily motivated by revenge (reciprocating losses), but disadvantageous inequity also motivates punishment, even in the absence of loss. The effect of inequity aversion is weaker than the effect of loss. The design manipulates whether the partner can steal from or augment the responder's payoff, and the resulting payoff differences. Punishment is most frequent when the partner steals, especially if this creates disadvantageous inequity. There is also some punishment when the partner does not augment, if this results in disadvantageous inequity. The study does not measure or report efficiency or group payoff, so it does not provide direct evidence about the effect of punishment on efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides high-quality evidence about the psychological motivations for punishment in two-player games, but does not report efficiency or payoff-based outcomes. It is not directly informative for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods or similar games. However, it does suggest that punishment is more likely to be used when losses or disadvantageous inequity are present, and that the cost and effectiveness of punishment can moderate its use. For the prediction task, this paper is only indirectly relevant, as it does not provide data on group efficiency or welfare with or without punishment.

- source: Otten, K., Buskens, V., Przepiorka, W., & Ellemers, N. (2025). Protecting Public Goods or Helping Free Riders? A Real-Life Moral Dilemma in Interethnic and Intraethnic Encounters. *Psychological Science*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=field_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=weak
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds robust evidence that bystanders are more likely to help in-group (native-majority) free riders and more likely to sanction out-group (ethnic-minority) free riders in a real-life public transport context. The behavioral outcome is whether the bystander helps the free rider (enabling norm violation) or sanctions (refuses or disapproves). The effect is consistent across locations, age groups, and sessions. The study does not measure or report efficiency, group payoff, or any explicit payoff-based outcome. The findings highlight the role of group membership in informal norm enforcement and the potential for double standards in sanctioning free riders.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence on informal sanctioning of free riders in a real-world context, but does not report efficiency or payoff-based outcomes. The design is adjacent to public goods games, as it involves a public good (fare compliance) and the possibility of informal punishment (sanctioning) or anti-punishment (helping). The main insight for prediction is that the presence and effectiveness of informal sanctions (and their social selectivity) can depend strongly on group membership, and that in some real-world settings, bystanders may choose to help free riders rather than sanction them, especially for in-group members. However, because no efficiency or group payoff is measured, the paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting efficiency changes due to punishment in standard PGG-like games. Its main value is in highlighting the complexity and social contingency of informal punishment in naturalistic settings.

- source: Mitsuishi, K., & Kawamura, Y. (2025). Avoidance of altruistic punishment: Testing with a situation-selective third-party punishment game. *Journal of Experimental Social Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=weak
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: Across three preregistered experiments, participants consistently avoided situations where they might have to punish unfairness, especially when direct punishment (with a real-effort cost) was possible. Avoidance was less pronounced when only indirect punishment (e.g., negative feedback) was available, and lowest when no punishment was possible. When participants could not avoid observing unfairness, they were likely to punish unfair behavior, regardless of their prior avoidance. The study provides evidence that the prevalence of third-party punishment in standard lab games may overstate its real-world frequency, as people often avoid such situations if possible. However, the study does not measure or report group efficiency, welfare, or total payoffs, so it does not directly inform the effect of punishment on efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper is not directly informative for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-like games, as it does not report efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes. Its main contribution is to show that the opportunity for punishment (especially direct, costly punishment) leads to avoidance of situations where punishment decisions are required, and that indirect punishment options reduce this avoidance. For prediction tasks focused on efficiency, this paper suggests that the structure of the punishment opportunity (direct vs indirect, costliness) may affect participation rates in punishment, but does not provide quantitative or qualitative evidence about efficiency outcomes.

- source: O'Gorman, R., Wilson, D. S., & Miller, R. R. (2005). Altruistic punishing and helping differ in sensitivity to relatedness, friendship, and future interactions. *Evolution and Human Behavior*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=other_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=weak
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr, punishment_cost, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The main finding is that participants report a strong desire to punish cheaters at personal cost, regardless of whether the cheater is a relative, friend, or stranger, and regardless of the likelihood of future interaction. In contrast, willingness to help is much more sensitive to relatedness and future interaction. The study does not use real economic games or measure group efficiency or payoffs, but rather relies on self-reported hypothetical willingness to punish or help. The results suggest that the psychological impulse to punish norm violators is less conditional on kinship or reciprocity cues than the impulse to help, at least in hypothetical settings.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency or group payoff in public goods games or similar environments. It does, however, suggest that the psychological willingness to punish is robust to changes in relatedness and future interaction cues, which may imply that, in real games, punishment behavior could be less sensitive to these variables than helping behavior. However, because the study does not use real payoffs, group outcomes, or actual public goods games, its value for predicting efficiency effects of punishment is limited and indirect.

- source: Kurz, T., Thomas, W. E., & Fonseca, M. A. (2014). A fine is a more effective financial deterrent when framed retributively and extracted publicly. *Journal of Experimental Social Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=field_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=weak
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_punishment_id
  findings: The field experiment demonstrates that the effectiveness of financial punishment in deterring undesired behavior (lateness) depends strongly on both the framing (retributive vs. compensatory) and the public/private nature of the sanction. Retributive and public fines significantly increase punctuality compared to compensatory or private fines, which are no more effective than no fine. A follow-up scenario study shows that compensatory fines reduce the perceived immorality of transgression, while retributive fines do not. The results support a theory that the moral framing and public visibility of punishment are key moderators of its behavioral impact.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the framing and public administration of punishment can substantially moderate its behavioral effectiveness, even when the material cost is held constant. While the context is not a public goods game, the findings suggest that in collective action settings, punishment will be more effective at promoting cooperation if it is framed as retributive (moral) and administered publicly. However, the study does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes, so direct prediction of efficiency effects is not possible. The results are most useful for informing the design of punishment mechanisms (e.g., public vs. private, moral vs. compensatory framing) rather than for quantitative prediction of efficiency.

- source: Price, M. E. (2005). Punitive sentiment among the Shuar and in industrialized societies: cross-cultural similarities. *Evolution and Human Behavior*.
  type: empirical | empirical=observational | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=weak
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, punishment_cost
  findings: The study finds that among the Shuar, as in industrialized societies, higher contributors to collective action (mingas) express stronger punitive sentiment toward free riders, and that only intentional absences (free riding) are considered deserving of punishment. The main dependent variable is the amount of fine each member thinks should be imposed for unexcused absences. There is a strong positive correlation between contribution (attendance) and punitive sentiment. The study does not measure or report group efficiency, total payoff, or welfare, so it does not directly inform the effect of punishment on efficiency. However, it provides cross-cultural evidence that punitive sentiment toward free riders is a robust feature of collective action settings.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency or group payoff in public goods games or their variants. It does, however, support the generalizability of punitive sentiment toward free riders across cultures and suggests that the psychological mechanisms underlying punishment are similar in small-scale and industrialized societies. For prediction tasks, this paper is useful for understanding the universality of punitive motivations, but not for quantifying efficiency effects or moderating variables. It does not provide parameter values or outcome data for the key game design dimensions relevant to efficiency prediction.

- source: Przepiorka, W., & Berger, J. (2016). The Sanctioning Dilemma: A Quasi-Experiment on Social Norm Enforcement in the Train. *European Sociological Review*.
  type: empirical | empirical=observational | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=weak
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count
  findings: The study finds that peer-sanctioning (negative sanctioning of a norm violator) occurs in about 50% of interventions. Contrary to the classic diffusion of responsibility hypothesis, the likelihood of sanctioning increases with the number of passengers present. Passengers who are closer to the norm breaker and those whose activities require silence are more likely to sanction. The likelihood of sanctioning decreases as the train approaches its destination. No significant difference in sanctioning rates is found between silent-area and non-silent-area train cars. The study does not measure or report any payoff, efficiency, or group welfare outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency or payoff in public goods games or their variants. While it is conceptually adjacent—framing norm enforcement as a second-order public good and using volunteer's dilemma theory—the outcomes are strictly behavioral (sanctioning occurrence and timing). There is no measurement of group payoff, efficiency, or welfare, nor any manipulation of punishment cost, effectiveness, or related game design dimensions. Thus, this paper should not be used as direct evidence for predicting efficiency effects of punishment in PGG-like environments, though it may provide some indirect, conceptual support for the importance of heterogeneity and group size in norm enforcement contexts.

- source: Rosati, A. G., DiNicola, L. M., & Buckholtz, J. W. (2018). Chimpanzee Cooperation Is Fast and Independent From Self-Control. *Psychological Science*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=weak
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: Chimpanzees show a fast response bias for prosocial actions (including punishment of theft), but the frequency of such actions is modest and varies across individuals. More prosocial individuals are faster to act in both helping and punishment contexts. There is no evidence that self-control (temporal or motoric) predicts prosocial or punishment behavior. The three measured domains of prosociality (donation, helping, punishment) are uncorrelated, suggesting distinct underlying mechanisms. The study does not measure or report group efficiency, total payoff, or welfare, and thus does not provide direct evidence on the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-like games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency or payoff in public-goods-game-like environments, as it does not report efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes. Its design is adjacent to PGGs, using individual-level donation, helping, and punishment tasks with chimpanzees, but not repeated group games or explicit payoff structures. The main relevance is in showing that punishment behavior exists and is associated with faster response times in chimpanzees, but not with self-control. For prediction tasks focused on efficiency, this paper offers only indirect, weak support: it suggests that punishment can be a salient behavioral response, but does not inform whether it increases or decreases group efficiency.

- source: Turpie, J., & Letley, G. (2021). Would community conservation initiatives benefit from external financial oversight? A framed field experiment in Namibia's communal conservancies. *World Development*.
  type: empirical | empirical=observational | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=weak
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: none
  findings: The paper finds that individuals' willingness to impose social sanctions (disapproval, social exclusion) for norm violations is conditional on their beliefs about the frequency of such violations: the more common a violation is believed to be, the less likely people are to sanction it. This conditionality is strongest for weaker norms (e.g., tax evasion, fare dodging) and among more educated respondents. For strong norms (e.g., drunk driving), sanctioning is high regardless of beliefs. The study uses survey data from Austria and applies instrumental variable analysis to establish causality. No efficiency, payoff, or group welfare outcomes are reported.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence on the determinants of willingness to sanction norm violations in real-world, non-experimental settings, showing that sanctioning is conditional on perceived prevalence for weaker norms. However, it does not study public goods games or variants directly, nor does it report efficiency or payoff outcomes. Thus, it does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games. Its main relevance is conceptual: it supports the idea that the effectiveness of punishment as a deterrent may depend on beliefs about norm prevalence, which could inform mechanism design or interpretation of experimental results, but it does not provide quantitative or design-specific guidance for efficiency prediction.

- source: van Dijk, E., De Dreu, C. K. W., & Gross, J. (2020). Power in economic games. *Current Opinion in Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=observational | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=weak
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: none
  findings: The paper finds strong evidence for conditional norm enforcement: individuals' stated willingness to sanction norm violations (e.g., tax evasion, fare dodging) is lower when they believe such violations are more common. This effect is most pronounced for weaker norms and among more educated respondents. For strong norms (e.g., drunk driving), sanctioning is high and largely unconditional. The study establishes a causal link between beliefs about norm prevalence and willingness to sanction using an instrumental variables approach. However, all outcomes are based on self-reported intentions in a survey, not on actual behavior or payoffs.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence on efficiency or payoff outcomes in public goods games or their variants. Its main contribution is to show that willingness to sanction norm violations is conditional on beliefs about others' compliance, especially for weaker norms. For the downstream prediction task, this suggests that the effectiveness of punishment in increasing cooperation or efficiency may depend on participants' beliefs about the prevalence of norm violations, but the paper does not provide quantitative or experimental evidence on efficiency or group payoff. The findings are most relevant for understanding psychological and social moderators of punishment behavior, not for predicting efficiency outcomes from game design dimensions.

- source: Rai, T. S. (2022). Material Benefits Crowd Out Moralistic Punishment. *Psychological Science*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=none
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The main finding is that paying people to engage in third-party punishment of unfair or immoral behavior reduces their willingness to do so, compared to when no payment is offered. This effect is robust across both real-stakes economic games and hypothetical vignettes. The reduction in willingness is attributed to the corruption of the moral signal that punishment provides; payment makes the act appear less morally motivated. Willingness to punish can be restored either by offering a sufficiently large payment (which compensates for the lost moral signal) or by providing moral assurances that payment does not undermine the punisher's virtue. The study does not address efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes, and does not use a repeated public goods game structure.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency or group payoff in public goods games or similar environments. Its main contribution is to show that introducing material incentives for punishment can reduce individuals' willingness to punish, unless the incentives are large or are reframed to preserve moral signaling. For prediction tasks focused on efficiency or payoff, this paper is only indirectly relevant: it suggests that the design of punishment incentives (especially the presence and framing of material rewards for punishers) could affect the frequency of punishment, which in turn could affect cooperation and efficiency in other settings. However, no direct evidence is provided about efficiency or payoff outcomes.
