- source: Dimant, E., & Gesche, T. (2023). Nudging enforcers: how norm perceptions and motives for lying shape sanctions. *Pnas Nexus*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=none
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that third-party punishment increases with the size of the lie and is harsher when the lie increases inequity or is for personal gain, compared to when it restores equity. Norm-nudges (empirical or normative information about others' behavior or approval) increase punishment, especially when preexisting norms are ambiguous. The effect of norm-nudges is strongest in scenarios where the social norm is less clear. These findings are robust across a behavioral experiment, a vignette experiment, and a norm elicitation study. However, the study does not report or analyze efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides high-quality evidence on how punishment behavior is shaped by norm-nudges and the context of norm violations, but it does not report efficiency or payoff outcomes. For prediction tasks focused on efficiency in public-goods-like games, this paper is only indirectly informative: it shows that punishment is sensitive to context and can be increased by norm-nudges, especially when norms are ambiguous. However, it does not provide evidence on whether or how punishment affects group efficiency or welfare, nor does it use a public goods game structure. Thus, it should be used as indirect evidence about the determinants of punishment behavior, not about efficiency effects.

- source: Martin, J. W., Jordan, J. J., Rand, D. G., & Cushman, F. (2019). When do we punish people who don't?. *Cognition*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=none
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The main finding is that people are more likely to punish (or endorse punishing) third-party observers who fail to punish norm violators than second-party victims who fail to punish. This pattern is robust across economic games and vignettes, and holds for both severe and non-criminal norm violations. The relationship between the third party and the victim modulates HOP: authorities are punished most for failing to punish, followed by friends, then strangers. The study does not examine or report on efficiency, group payoff, or welfare, and thus does not provide evidence about the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods or similar games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence relevant to predicting the effect of enabling punishment on efficiency or group payoff in public-goods-like games. Its outcomes are exclusively about the social psychology of higher-order punishment—when people punish non-punishers—not about the consequences of punishment for cooperation, contribution, or efficiency. The economic game used is not a public goods game or a close variant, and no group-level payoff or efficiency measures are reported. Thus, this paper should not be used to inform quantitative predictions of efficiency changes due to punishment in PGG-like environments.

- source: Eriksson, K., Andersson, P. A., & Strimling, P. (2016). Moderators of the disapproval of peer punishment. *Group Processes & Intergroup Relations*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=none
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that peer punishment of norm violators is typically disapproved of by observers, especially when the punishment is severe or appears aggressive. This disapproval extends to viewing the punisher as a problem for the group and the act as inappropriate. Disapproval is moderated by the observer's own trait aggression (less disapproval among more aggressive raters), the severity of punishment (more severe = more disapproval), and whether the punishment is collective or individual (collective = slightly less disapproval). Economic sanctions are disapproved of less than physical ones, and unselfish economic sanctions (where the punisher does not profit) are disapproved of less than selfish ones. Verbal sanctions are also disapproved of, even when effective. The study does not measure or report efficiency, group payoff, or any related outcome.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency or group payoff in public goods games or similar environments. Its findings are relevant for understanding social approval and the perceived legitimacy of peer punishment, but not for predicting how punishment affects group efficiency or earnings. The design is adjacent to PGGs (common resource, norm violation, sanctioning), but the outcomes are purely attitudinal and perceptual. Thus, this paper should not be used as direct evidence for the downstream prediction task of treatment efficiency, though it may inform hypotheses about the social acceptability of punishment mechanisms.

- source: Pedersen, E. J., McAuliffe, W. H. B., & McCullough, M. E. (2018). The Unresponsive Avenger: More Evidence That Disinterested Third Parties Do Not Punish Altruistically. *Journal of Experimental Psychology-general*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=none
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: Across five lab experiments, participants who were directly harmed (insulted) by another person reliably punished the insulter using an aversive sound blast. Participants who witnessed a friend being insulted also punished the insulter, but less strongly. However, participants who witnessed a stranger being insulted generally did not punish the insulter more than they punished uninvolved bystanders, except in one experiment where social censure may have been a factor. The results suggest that third-party punishment is not a robust, general tendency, but is contingent on welfare interdependence (e.g., self or friends). The findings cast doubt on the external validity of economic game results that report high rates of third-party punishment, suggesting those may be inflated by experimental demand. No efficiency or payoff-based outcomes are reported.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting efficiency or group payoff in public goods games with or without punishment. Its main contribution is to question the generalizability of third-party punishment effects observed in economic games, suggesting that such effects may be overestimated due to experimental demand. For the downstream prediction task, this paper signals that the presence of punishment opportunities does not guarantee increased norm enforcement or efficiency, especially when punishment is not personally relevant or when welfare interdependence is low. However, because no efficiency or payoff outcomes are measured, the paper cannot inform quantitative predictions of efficiency changes due to punishment in PGG-like environments.

- source: Wang, L., & Murnighan, J. K. (2017). The Dynamics of Punishment and Trust. *Journal of Applied Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=none
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that punishment increases observers' trust in the punisher when the punished individual is seen as deserving, but decreases trust when the punishment is undeserved or too severe. Medium or large punishment for deserving targets increases trust, but large punishment for less deserving targets decreases trust. Punishers who are perceived as acting out of self-interest (e.g., who benefit from the punishment) are trusted less than altruistic punishers. These effects are robust across scenario-based and behavioral lab studies. However, the paper does not report any efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes, and all dependent variables are trust-related or perception-based.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting efficiency or group payoff in public goods or social dilemma games with or without punishment. Its findings are relevant for understanding how punishment affects trust in the punisher, especially as a function of deservingness, punishment magnitude, and punisher motives. For the downstream prediction task (treatment efficiency from game design plus control efficiency), this paper offers no direct evidence, as it does not report efficiency or payoff outcomes. It may be useful for understanding psychological or reputational effects of punishment, but not for quantitative prediction of efficiency.

- source: White, C. J. M., & van de Vondervoort, J. W. (2025). Third-party punishment, vigilante justice, or karma? Understanding the dynamics of interpersonal and cosmic justice. *Journal of Experimental Social Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=none
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: punishment_cost, show_punishment_id
  findings: Across three vignette-based experiments, the paper finds that: (1) third-party punishment (i.e., antisocial behavior directed at a transgressor) is evaluated more negatively than impersonal misfortune or reprimand, even when the punisher knows the victim's past bad behavior; (2) knowledge of the victim's past transgression makes punishment slightly less negative, but not positive; (3) reprimand is evaluated much more positively than actual punishment; (4) belief in karma predicts more positive evaluations of impersonal misfortune for bad actors, but does not increase approval of interpersonal punishment; (5) costliness of punishment to the punisher does not significantly affect evaluations. No efficiency, payoff, or group outcome measures are reported.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence relevant to predicting efficiency or payoff outcomes in public goods games or similar economic games with or without punishment. Its findings are about social and moral evaluations of punishment, not about behavioral or payoff consequences. It does, however, suggest that third-party punishment is often viewed negatively by observers, and that alternative responses (reprimand, impersonal punishment) are preferred, but this is not linked to efficiency or group payoff. The paper is not informative for the downstream prediction task of treatment efficiency.

- source: Pedersen, E. J., McAuliffe, W. H. B., Shah, Y., Tanaka, H., Ohtsubo, Y., & McCullough, M. E. (2020). When and Why Do Third Parties Punish Outside of the Lab? A Cross-Cultural Recall Study. *Social Psychological and Personality Science*.
  type: empirical | empirical=observational | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=none
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: none
  findings: The study finds that third-party punishment in real-life conflicts is infrequent, especially when the third party does not value the welfare of the victim (i.e., low WTR). Intervention (helping the victim without punishing the transgressor) is more common than punishment. The main predictor of both punishment and intervention is the third party's WTR for the victim, with higher WTRs associated with greater likelihood of action. WTR for the transgressor predicts less anger but does not significantly predict intervention or punishment. The results are consistent across U.S. students, U.S. MTurkers, and Japanese students, though Japanese students intervene and punish less often. The study concludes that real-world third-party punishment is much less common than laboratory experiments suggest, likely due to the lack of anonymity, the availability of alternative responses, and the importance of social relationships.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting efficiency or payoff outcomes in public goods games with or without punishment. However, it is highly informative about the external validity of laboratory punishment rates: it suggests that real-world third-party punishment is rare, especially among strangers, and that laboratory designs may overstate the prevalence and impact of punishment. For prediction tasks, this paper supports the view that punishment's effect on cooperation or efficiency in real-world or naturalistic settings may be much smaller than in lab-based PGGs, particularly when social distance is high and alternative responses are available. The findings highlight the importance of relationship context and available response options as moderators of punishment behavior.

- source: Fabbri, M., Barbieri, P. N., & Bigoni, M. (2019). Ride Your Luck! A Field Experiment on Lottery-Based Incentives for Compliance. *Management Science*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=field_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: reward_exists, reward_cost, reward_tech
  findings: A field experiment on city buses in Italy tested a lottery-based reward for buying tickets, layered on top of existing probabilistic sanctions for fare-dodging. Treated buses saw a significant increase in on-board ticket sales (18–30% increase per hour), with no overall reduction in off-board ticket sales, indicating a net increase in compliance and revenue. The increased revenue covered the cost of the prizes actually claimed, but not the total value of all prizes offered, so cost-effectiveness depended on unclaimed prizes. The effect was not persistent after the lottery ended. Suburban routes showed a substitution from off-board to on-board ticket purchases. The study provides evidence that combining probabilistic sanctions with lottery-based rewards can increase compliance and revenue in a real-world public good context, but the net efficiency gain is limited by the cost of rewards and the sustainability of the effect.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides field evidence that adding a lottery-based reward to an environment with probabilistic sanctions increases compliance and revenue (a proxy for efficiency), but the net efficiency gain depends on the cost of the reward and whether all prizes are claimed. The effect is not persistent after removal. For prediction, the presence of both probabilistic sanctions and a reward mechanism (lottery) increases efficiency relative to sanctions alone, but the magnitude of the gain may be modest and sensitive to the cost structure and salience of the reward. The design is not a standard PGG, but the compliance/public good context and outcome measures are closely related. The findings are most informative for settings where compliance is individually observable and can be incentivized with both sanctions and probabilistic rewards.

- source: Mendoza, S. A., Lane, S. P., & Amodio, D. M. (2014). For Members Only: Ingroup Punishment of Fairness Norm Violations in the Ultimatum Game. *Social Psychological and Personality Science*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The main finding is that in the ultimatum game, participants are more likely to reject marginally unfair offers from in-group members than from out-group members, even though rejection is costly to both parties. This pattern is interpreted as stricter enforcement of fairness norms within groups. The effect is most pronounced for offers that are only slightly unfair (e.g., $8/$20), and is stronger among participants with higher in-group identification. The study does not report on group efficiency or total payoffs, and the outcome is measured in terms of behavioral responses (accept/reject) rather than efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence about the psychological and social moderators of costly punishment in a bargaining context (ultimatum game), specifically the role of group membership and social identity. However, it does not provide data on efficiency or group payoff, nor does it manipulate or report on most game design dimensions relevant to public goods games. The findings may inform predictions about when punishment is more likely to be used (e.g., in-group contexts, marginal norm violations), but do not directly inform the effect of punishment on efficiency or welfare in public-goods-game-like environments.

- source: Molina, M., Nee, V., & Holm, H. (2023). Cooperation with Strangers: Spillover of Community Norms. *Organization Science*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that among Chinese CEOs, stronger belief that norm violations are met with retaliatory sanctions in their business community is associated with a higher probability of cooperating with a stranger in a one-shot PD. This association is especially strong in some cities (Nanjing, Wenzhou). An online experiment with managers and nonmanagers shows that priming reciprocity norms increases cooperation with strangers in some subgroups and cities. However, the outcome is always the individual choice to cooperate, not group payoff or efficiency. There is no direct evidence on how punishment or norm enforcement affects efficiency or total group earnings.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting efficiency or group payoff in public goods games with or without punishment. Its main contribution is showing that belief in norm enforcement (especially retaliatory sanctions) is associated with higher cooperation rates in a one-shot PD. While this suggests that punishment norms can promote cooperation, the lack of payoff or efficiency outcomes, and the use of a PD rather than a PGG, limits its direct applicability to the efficiency prediction task. The findings may be useful for understanding the role of punishment norms in promoting cooperation, but not for quantitative prediction of efficiency changes due to punishment in PGG-like environments.

- source: Chambers, C. R. (2024). Nonmonetary Reward Systems, Counterproductive Behavior, and Responses to Sanctions in Open Collaboration Environments. *Organization Science*.
  type: empirical | empirical=observational | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: reward_exists
  findings: The study finds that proximity to nonmonetary reward thresholds is associated with a higher likelihood of counterproductive behavior (suspension), suggesting that reward systems can create performance pressures leading to negative behaviors. However, individuals who are suspended tend to increase their productive contributions just before suspension, and their contribution rates drop after suspension. The paper does not measure efficiency or group payoff, but suggests that formal sanctions may have unintended negative consequences for sustaining contributions in open collaboration environments.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence on efficiency or group payoff, so it cannot be used to quantitatively predict the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments. However, it offers indirect evidence that formal sanctions (suspensions) may reduce subsequent contribution rates, which could imply a negative effect on group output or efficiency if mapped to a PGG context. The findings suggest that while sanctions may deter counterproductive behavior, they may also discourage future productive contributions, highlighting a potential tradeoff. For prediction tasks, this paper signals caution in assuming that punishment will always improve group outcomes, especially in voluntary, open collaboration settings.

- source: Gurven, M., & Winking, J. (2008). Collective action in action: Prosocial behavior in and out of the laboratory. *American Anthropologist*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=field_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=none
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that among the Tsimane, offers in the Dictator Game, Ultimatum Game, and Third-Party Punishment Game are low, and punishment (rejection or third-party punishment) is infrequent. There is little to no correlation between individuals' behavior in these games and their real-world prosocial actions such as food sharing, labor for public goods (well construction), or contributions to communal feasts. The results suggest that context and payoff structure are more important than stable individual traits in predicting prosocial behavior. The paper does not report on efficiency or group payoff, and thus does not inform the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or their variants. While it includes a third-party punishment game (TPPG), the outcome measures are individual offers and punishment rates, not group efficiency or payoff. The study does show that punishment is rare and offers are low in this context, suggesting that, at least in this population and game structure, punishment does not play a strong role in promoting prosocial behavior. However, because efficiency is not measured, the paper cannot be used to calibrate or predict efficiency outcomes in PGG-like environments with or without punishment.

- source: Li, L. F., & Xiao, E. (2014). Money Talks: Rebate Mechanisms in Reputation System Design. *Management Science*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, reward_exists, reward_cost
  findings: The experiment demonstrates that when feedback reporting is costly, market efficiency (proportion of efficient trades and participant earnings) declines. Introducing a rebate mechanism, where sellers can offer a partial rebate to buyers for leaving feedback, increases efficiency—especially when sellers frequently offer rebates. The rebate acts as a signal of seller cooperativeness, increasing buyers' willingness to purchase and sellers' willingness to ship. The effect is robust even when the rebate does not fully cover the reporting cost. The more often sellers offer rebates, the higher the efficiency and earnings for both buyers and sellers. If sellers do not offer rebates, efficiency can be even lower than in the no-rebate, high-cost condition. Over time, sellers learn to offer rebates more frequently, further improving efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct evidence that introducing a mechanism for sellers to precommit to (partially) covering buyers' feedback costs increases efficiency in a repeated trust game with feedback, especially when sellers frequently use the mechanism. The effect is strongest when the rebate is offered often, and the mechanism works even if the rebate is less than the full reporting cost. For prediction, if a game design allows for a precommitment rebate mechanism and sellers are likely to use it, efficiency should be higher than in a comparable game with high feedback costs and no rebate. The control efficiency (with high feedback cost and no rebate) is a lower bound; the treatment efficiency (with rebate) will be higher, approaching the efficiency of the low-cost or automatic feedback conditions as rebate frequency increases. The findings are most directly relevant to trust/reputation games with feedback as a public good, and less directly to standard PGGs or punishment games, but the mechanism is analogous to reward/precommitment interventions in PGGs.

- source: Barker, J. L., & Barclay, P. (2016). Local competition increases people's willingness to harm others. *Evolution and Human Behavior*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The paper demonstrates that when competition is local (i.e., participants compete for prizes within small groups), people are more willing to pay a cost to harm others, resulting in lower group and individual payoffs (efficiency). In contrast, under global competition (competing against all participants), less money is spent on harming, and payoffs are higher. This effect holds whether endowments are earned or given. The findings are robust across three studies, including one where participants could harm both ingroup and outgroup members: under local competition, harming is targeted at ingroup members (the direct competitors), further reducing within-group efficiency. The results are interpreted as a 'tragedy of the commons' driven by local competition, where wasteful harming reduces overall welfare.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in economic games where players can pay to reduce others' payoffs (money-burning), the structure of competition (local vs global) is a critical moderator of efficiency. When competition is local, efficiency is much lower due to increased costly harming, even when the cost-effectiveness of harming is high (burning $1 to reduce $4). The findings suggest that, for prediction tasks, the presence of local competition (small group contests) should be expected to strongly reduce efficiency when punishment/harming is possible, compared to global competition. The results are robust to whether endowments are earned or given, and to whether harming is targeted at ingroup or outgroup members. However, the game is not a standard public goods game or punishment game: the main action is costly harming, not punishment for norm violation or contribution decisions. Still, the efficiency implications are directly relevant for settings where costly sanctions are possible.

- source: Bolton, G., Greiner, B., & Ockenfels, A. (2018). Dispute Resolution or Escalation? The Strategic Gaming of Feedback Withdrawal Options in Online Markets. *Management Science*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: In laboratory markets with two-sided moral hazard (both buyer and seller can defect), the introduction of a mutual feedback withdrawal option leads to more strategic negative feedback, less honest feedback, and lower efficiency (measured as realized gains from trade divided by maximum possible gains). The feedback system becomes less informative, and trust/trustworthiness are reduced. In one-sided markets (only seller can defect), the feedback withdrawal option does not significantly affect efficiency or the informativeness of feedback. The negative effect of FBW on efficiency is thus conditional on the presence of two-sided moral hazard and mutual withdrawal. The paper also finds that some buyers engage in 'altruistic punishment' by refusing to withdraw negative feedback even at a cost to themselves, partially mitigating the detrimental effects.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in environments structurally similar to public goods games with two-sided moral hazard and mutual punishment/retaliation options, introducing a mutual feedback withdrawal (punishment-like) mechanism reduces efficiency relative to a control without such a mechanism. The effect is not observed in one-sided environments. For prediction tasks, this suggests that the presence of mutual, bilateral punishment/retaliation options in two-sided games is likely to reduce efficiency, even if the control game is already inefficient. The effect is mediated by strategic retaliation and the breakdown of reputation as an incentive for cooperation. The findings are directly relevant for predicting efficiency in games with similar two-sided, bilateral punishment/retaliation structures.

- source: Arai, S., Tooby, J., & Cosmides, L. (2023). Why punish cheaters? Those who withdraw cooperation enjoy better reputations than punishers, but both are viewed as difficult to exploit. *Evolution and Human Behavior*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The experiment demonstrates that efficiency (normalized group payoff) in repeated prisoners' dilemma games is highest when public monitoring is perfect and decreases as noise in the public signal increases. For low and moderate noise, efficiency is significantly above the one-shot Nash equilibrium, but for high and infinite noise, efficiency approaches or falls below the one-shot level. Subjects use simple threshold strategies, conditioning their cooperation on the most recent public signal. The effect of noise is robust and statistically significant. The study does not include explicit costly punishment or reward mechanisms; instead, punishment is implemented as a phase of mutual defection triggered by low public signals. The results provide strong evidence that the ability to sustain efficient cooperation in repeated games is highly sensitive to the quality of public monitoring.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides high-quality evidence that, in repeated games with endogenous punishment (via reversion to Nash equilibrium), efficiency is strongly moderated by the quality of public monitoring. When public signals are accurate (low noise), efficiency is high; as noise increases, efficiency declines. The findings are directly relevant for predicting efficiency in repeated games with endogenous punishment, but less so for public goods games with explicit costly punishment options. The results suggest that, holding control efficiency constant, the effect of enabling punishment (via reversion to Nash) is highly dependent on the observability of others' actions. For prediction tasks, the paper supports the view that efficiency gains from punishment are greatest when monitoring is accurate, and that these gains diminish or disappear as monitoring becomes noisy.

- source: Brown, M., Falk, A., & Fehr, E. (2004). Relational contracts and the nature of market interactions. *Econometrica*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries, show_punishment_id
  findings: The absence of third-party enforcement leads to the endogenous formation of long-term firm-worker relationships, where contract renewal is contingent on high effort. This informal punishment mechanism (firing/termination) disciplines workers and results in higher effort, higher wages, and much higher efficiency and surplus compared to settings where such relationships are not possible. The effect is robust: both firms and workers earn more in long-term relationships, and the distribution of surplus is more equal. When long-term relationships are ruled out (random rematching), effort and efficiency drop sharply, and minimal effort becomes common. When third-party enforcement is available, the market behaves competitively, with low wages, low rent sharing, and little incentive for long-term relationships. The findings show that informal, endogenous punishment (via contract renewal/firing) can substitute for formal enforcement and substantially improve efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in repeated market-like environments with incomplete contracts, the ability to endogenously form long-term relationships and use contract renewal/firing as a sanction leads to much higher efficiency and surplus than settings where such informal punishment is not possible. For prediction tasks, the presence of repeated interactions, the ability to condition future play on past behavior, and the absence of third-party enforcement are key moderators of punishment's positive effect on efficiency. The findings suggest that, in environments where informal punishment via relationship termination is feasible, efficiency can approach the level seen with formal enforcement, provided that agents can build reputations and relationships. The results are most directly relevant to labor market and relational contract settings, but the underlying mechanism is adjacent to public goods games with endogenous partner choice and repeated play.

- source: Oberholzer-Gee, F., Waldfogel, J., & White, M. W. (2010). FRIEND OR FOE? COOPERATION AND LEARNING IN HIGH-STAKES GAMES. *Review of Economics and Statistics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=observational | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study documents that, over two seasons of the game show, players shift from unconditional to conditional cooperation based on observable opponent characteristics (e.g., gender, race, age). In the first season, cooperation rates are unrelated to opponent attributes; in the second season, players are more likely to defect (choose 'foe') against opponents whose attributes predict less cooperation. This learning process leads to a substantial decline in overall efficiency: the average share of stakes converted to winnings drops from 39% in season 1 to 30% in season 2. The decline is especially pronounced for players with attributes associated with low cooperation (e.g., men, black players, younger players), whose average efficiency falls sharply. In contrast, players with more cooperative-associated attributes (e.g., women, white players) maintain their efficiency. The findings suggest that conditional cooperation, when based on imperfect signals, can reduce group efficiency in one-shot, high-stakes settings.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in one-shot, simultaneous-move games structurally similar to the prisoner's dilemma, the emergence of conditional cooperation based on observable attributes can reduce overall efficiency, especially when players use imperfect signals to predict others' behavior. For prediction tasks, this suggests that enabling mechanisms that allow players to condition on opponent characteristics (e.g., through history, reputation, or observable traits) may decrease efficiency relative to baseline, particularly if the environment is not anonymous and players can stereotype. The findings are most relevant for games with binary (all-or-nothing) choices, no explicit punishment or reward mechanisms, and high stakes. The results caution that learning and conditional strategies do not always improve efficiency and may exacerbate payoff inequality.

- source: Sabin, N., & Reed-Tsochas, F. (2020). Able but Unwilling to Enforce: Cooperative Dilemmas in Group Lending. *American Journal of Sociology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=observational | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds
  findings: The study finds that group repayment (a direct measure of economic cooperation/efficiency) is highest in groups with moderate social cohesion and no disconnected subgroups. Both very high and very low cohesion are associated with worse repayment. Disconnected subgroups further reduce repayment, and the negative effect is mitigated by higher overall cohesion. The mechanisms are traced to a trade-off between ability and willingness to punish: high cohesion increases ability but reduces willingness to enforce, while disconnected subgroups increase willingness to punish out-group members but reduce ability to do so effectively. The net effect on efficiency is determined by the lesser of ability or willingness. These results are robust to controls for financial ability, group composition, and alternative outcome measures.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the effect of informal punishment mechanisms on efficiency in public-goods-like settings is highly sensitive to group social structure. In environments where punishment is informal and depends on social ties, both the ability and willingness to punish must be considered. Efficiency is maximized at moderate levels of cohesion; too much cohesion reduces willingness to punish, undermining enforcement. Disconnected subgroups are detrimental to efficiency, even if willingness to punish out-group members is high, because ability is low. For prediction tasks, this suggests that the effect of punishment on efficiency is not monotonic in group connectedness and is context-dependent. The findings are most relevant for settings with informal, endogenous punishment and may not generalize to formal, externally imposed punishment mechanisms.

- source: Kas, J., Corten, R., & van de Rijt, A. (2020). Reputations in mixed-role markets: A theory and an experimental test. *Social Science Research*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The experiment shows that in a repeated dyadic trust/lending game, providing information about a participant's past behavior in both roles (mixed-role reputation) increases the rate at which trust is placed and honored in the Lending Game (free exchange, where lending is costly and not compensated), but not in the Trust Game (paid exchange, where lending is compensated). Specifically, in the Lending Game, the trust rate and the 'success rate' (trust placed and honored) are significantly higher under mixed-role reputation than under single-role reputation. In the Trust Game, there is no significant difference between reputation conditions. The paper interprets these results as evidence that mixed-role reputation systems facilitate reciprocal lending and increase overall cooperation and welfare in free exchange settings.
  prediction_guidance: For prediction tasks involving public-goods-like environments where the main intervention is a change in the information structure (e.g., adding mixed-role reputation), this paper provides evidence that such interventions can increase efficiency-related outcomes (success rate) in free exchange settings, but not in paid exchange settings. The results are most relevant for environments where cooperation is costly and not directly compensated, and where reputation can be built across multiple roles. The findings suggest that enabling richer reputation signals (beyond single-role) can improve group outcomes in these contexts. However, the paper does not directly study classic public goods games or explicit punishment mechanisms, so the transfer to PGGs with punishment should be made with caution.

- source: Smith, K. B. (2006). Representational altruism: The wary cooperator as authoritative decision maker. *American Journal of Political Science*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds
  findings: The experiments show that individuals acting as allocators in one-shot, anonymous dictator/ultimatum-like games often divide resources evenly or even disadvantage themselves to appear fair, supporting the 'wary cooperator' hypothesis. The presence or absence of a veto (ultimatum vs. dictator) does not significantly affect allocations; rather, information asymmetry (who values the resource more) and the framing of the allocator's role (merit vs. trust) are key. When allocators are told they are entrusted with responsibility, they are more likely to allocate less to themselves than when told they earned the role by merit. The findings suggest that internalized social preferences, not external punishment or veto threats, drive representational altruism and fairness in allocation decisions.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence relevant to predicting efficiency in public-goods-like environments with and without punishment. While it does not use a standard PGG or explicit punishment mechanism, it shows that internalized social preferences can lead to fair or altruistic outcomes even in the absence of external sanctions. The findings suggest that, in some contexts, adding external punishment or veto power may have limited additional effect on efficiency if internalized fairness norms are strong. However, context (information asymmetry, framing of authority) can shift behavior toward more or less self-interested allocations. For prediction, this implies that game design features affecting perceived fairness and social expectations may be as important as formal punishment mechanisms.

- source: Diekmann, A., Jann, B., Przepiorka, W., & Wehrli, S. (2014). Reputation Formation and the Evolution of Cooperation in Anonymous Online Markets. *American Sociological Review*.
  type: empirical | empirical=observational | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: show_other_summaries, show_punishment_id
  findings: Using large-scale eBay auction data, the study shows that sellers with more positive feedback (reputation) are more likely to sell their items and receive higher prices, while negative feedback reduces both outcomes. The effect of negative feedback is stronger than that of positive feedback. Participation in the feedback system is high, and feedback behavior is consistent with strong reciprocity and altruism, which helps maintain the reputation system. The reputation system thus mitigates trust problems and supports cooperation in one-shot, anonymous exchanges, increasing overall market efficiency and welfare.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that reputation-based feedback systems in anonymous, one-shot exchange environments (adjacent to PGGs) increase efficiency by incentivizing cooperative behavior. While not a direct PGG or punishment experiment, the findings are highly relevant for predicting the effect of introducing or strengthening reputation/feedback mechanisms in similar environments: efficiency (measured as successful trade and higher prices) increases when reputation mechanisms are active and well-participated. The effect is stronger for negative feedback (punishment) than for positive feedback (reward). The study also highlights the importance of high participation in the feedback system, which is sustained by reciprocity and altruism. For prediction, the presence of a reputation/feedback mechanism (even if not formal monetary punishment) should be expected to increase efficiency relative to a baseline with no such mechanism.

- source: Nakashima, N. A., Halali, E., & Halevy, N. (2017). Third parties promote cooperative norms in repeated interactions. *Journal of Experimental Social Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: Third party intervention in repeated adversarial interactions (modeled as a Peacemaker Game) increases cooperation rates among adversaries. Early intervention is more effective at establishing persistent cooperative norms than late intervention. Third parties are more likely to intervene when adversaries have a history of cooperation, and adversaries are more likely to cooperate following third party intervention. The main outcomes are behavioral (cooperation and intervention rates), not efficiency or group payoff. The study provides indirect evidence that mechanisms which combine reward and punishment can transform competitive repeated interactions into more cooperative ones, but does not provide direct evidence on efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect support for the idea that enabling third-party interventions (which combine reward and punishment) can increase cooperation in repeated Prisoner's Dilemma-like settings. However, because the main outcomes are cooperation and intervention rates, not efficiency or group payoff, the findings should be used as suggestive rather than directly predictive for efficiency outcomes. The game structure is adjacent to public goods games, and the intervention mechanism is not a standard peer punishment or reward system. The results suggest that early, consistent intervention is more effective at establishing cooperation than late intervention, and that the presence of a third party who can both reward and punish can transform group behavior. For prediction tasks, this paper suggests that enabling such mechanisms is likely to increase cooperation, but the quantitative effect on efficiency is not directly reported.

- source: Gillenkirch, R. M., & Kreienbaum, H. (2017). What guides subjective performance evaluation: Incentive alignment or norm enforcement?. *Review of Managerial Science*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The experiment shows that superiors are primarily motivated by norm enforcement: they pay more for information that allows them to reward high and punish low effort, and their allocation decisions reflect a willingness to punish norm violations. Subordinates increase their effort when they know their effort can be observed and evaluated. The study does not directly report group efficiency or total surplus, but higher effort levels in conditions with more informative evaluation suggest that group output and subordinate payoffs increase when norm enforcement is possible. However, the focus is on behavioral mechanisms (effort, information acquisition, allocation) rather than on efficiency as a formal outcome.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence that enabling punishment (via subjective evaluation and information acquisition) increases effort and likely increases group output in a team production setting with a free rider problem. However, it does not report efficiency or total payoff as a primary outcome, nor does it use a standard public goods game. The design is adjacent to PGGs: two subordinates choose effort (costly), output is the sum of efforts plus noise, and a superior (not a group member) allocates the output. The 'punishment' is implemented via allocation decisions, not explicit costly punishment. For prediction tasks, this study suggests that mechanisms enabling norm enforcement (punishment) can increase effort and likely improve group outcomes, but the lack of direct efficiency data and the non-standard game structure limit its direct applicability to PGG efficiency prediction.

- source: Garrett, J. B., Livingston, J. A., Tayler, W. B., Cade, N. L., & McVay, S. E. (2019). Controls and Cooperation in Interactive and Non-Interactive Settings. *Contemporary Accounting Research*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, reward_exists, reward_cost
  findings: The study finds that introducing a control (audit with bonus) in a repeated prisoner's dilemma increases subsequent cooperative behavior (measured as investment in a trust game) for both participants who directly experienced the control and those who only observed it, but the effect is significantly larger for those who directly benefited from cooperation. The effect is mediated by observed cooperation in the initial game. The results suggest that controls can foster trust and reciprocity, and that these effects are stronger in interactive settings. However, the study does not report efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes, and the main outcome is behavioral (investment/cooperation), not payoff-based.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence that control mechanisms (akin to monitoring or incentive-based controls) can increase cooperative behavior in subsequent social dilemmas, especially in interactive settings. However, because the main outcome is investment in a trust game (a behavioral measure) and not efficiency or group payoff, the findings should be used with caution for predicting efficiency effects of punishment in public goods games. The design is adjacent to PGGs and does not involve explicit punishment or sanctions, but rather a positive incentive (bonus for high contribution). The results suggest that mechanisms increasing observed cooperation can have positive spillovers on future cooperation, but do not directly inform the efficiency impact of punishment in PGG-like environments.

- source: Kuwabara, K. (2015). Do Reputation Systems Undermine Trust? Divergent Effects of Enforcement Type on Generalized Trust and Trustworthiness. *American Journal of Sociology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: Reputation systems, as a form of diffuse, non-material enforcement, reinforce generalized trust and trustworthiness more than contractual (material punishment) or relational (repeated partner) enforcement. After exposure to reputational enforcement, participants are more trusting and trustworthy in subsequent unenforced exchanges than after exposure to contracts or repeated partners. Contractual enforcement, in contrast, can undermine intrinsic motivation to trust or be trustworthy once enforcement is removed. These effects are robust across multiple studies and are supported by both behavioral and self-report measures. However, the study does not report on efficiency, group payoff, or welfare, and the experimental paradigm is the investment game, not a public goods game.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence about the effects of punishment-like enforcement (contracts) and reputation systems on subsequent cooperation, but not on efficiency or group payoff. The findings suggest that material punishment (contracts) can undermine intrinsic motivation for cooperation, while reputation systems can reinforce it. However, because the outcomes are trust/trustworthiness (not efficiency) and the game is not a public goods game, the results should be used with caution for predicting efficiency effects of punishment in PGG-like environments. The evidence is more relevant for understanding how enforcement mechanisms affect willingness to cooperate after enforcement is removed, rather than direct effects on group efficiency during enforcement.

- source: Burum, B., Nowak, M. A., & Hoffman, M. (2020). An evolutionary explanation for ineffective altruism. *Nature Human Behaviour*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, reward_exists, reward_cost
  findings: The paper finds that people are generally insensitive to the efficacy (impact) of their charitable giving, in contrast to their sensitivity when making decisions for themselves or family. Social rewards (reputation, praise, monetary reward from third parties) are given for the act of giving, not for giving effectively or giving more. This insensitivity is not due to cognitive or emotional limitations, but rather to the evolutionary logic that social rewards are easier to coordinate around observable, well-defined actions (like giving) than around less observable or harder-to-define features (like efficacy or amount). The studies do not use public goods games or punishment, but the reward mechanism is conceptually adjacent to sanctioning. No efficiency or group payoff outcomes are reported.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games. However, it offers indirect evidence that social incentives (rewards) are most effective when they are based on observable, well-defined actions, and that people are not motivated by efficiency or impact when giving is socially rewarded. The findings suggest that, in environments where social rewards or punishments are based on easily observed behaviors (like contributing at all), these incentives may not improve efficiency if they do not target effective or high-impact contributions. The lack of sensitivity to efficacy in social rewards may limit the effectiveness of punishment or reward mechanisms in increasing group efficiency, unless the mechanisms are designed to be sensitive to impact. The paper is most relevant for understanding the limitations of social incentives in promoting efficient cooperation, rather than for direct prediction of efficiency outcomes in PGGs with punishment.

- source: Karagonlar, G., & Kuhlman, D. M. (2013). The role of Social Value Orientation in response to an unfair offer in the Ultimatum Game. *Organizational Behavior and Human Decision Processes*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: Across two lab experiments, the paper finds that Prosocials (as measured by SVO) are less likely to reject an unfair offer (20% of the pie) in the Ultimatum Game than Proselfs. This difference is not due to differences in fairness perceptions or expectations, but is mediated by more positive emotions and more positive attributions toward the proposer among Prosocials. The findings support the view that emotion regulation and cognitive reappraisal, rather than altruistic punishment, drive acceptance of unfair offers among Prosocials. The study does not manipulate or measure efficiency, group payoff, or the effect of punishment in a public goods context.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides little direct support for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. The Ultimatum Game is structurally different from public goods games, and the outcome of interest is the accept/reject decision rather than group payoff or efficiency. The findings are relevant for understanding individual differences in responses to norm violations and costly punishment, but do not inform the quantitative effect of punishment on efficiency. The study does, however, suggest that motivations for punishment (altruistic vs. spiteful) may differ by individual orientation, which could be contextually relevant for interpreting punishment effects in other games.

- source: Van Lange, P. A. M., Klapwijk, A., & Van Munster, L. M. (2011). How the shadow of the future might promote cooperation. *Group Processes & Intergroup Relations*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that the mere anticipation of repeated interaction (10 rounds vs. 1 round) increases cooperation (measured as coins given to the partner) among individualists, but not among prosocials, in a sequential give-some dilemma. Prosocials maintain high cooperation regardless of repetition, while individualists reciprocate more when repeated interaction is expected. The effect is robust to the presence or absence of 'noise' in the translation of choices. The study does not include explicit punishment or reward mechanisms, nor does it report on efficiency or group payoff outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence that repeated interaction (longer games) can increase cooperation among individualistically oriented participants, even in the absence of explicit punishment or reward mechanisms. However, it does not directly inform predictions about efficiency or payoff outcomes in public goods games with or without punishment. The design is adjacent to PGGs and does not manipulate or include punishment, so its relevance to the prediction of efficiency under punishment is limited. The main implication is that repeated interaction may substitute for some effects of punishment among individualists, but this is not tested in a payoff or efficiency framework.

- source: Vandermeer, J., Hosey, C., Epley, N., & Keysar, B. (2019). Escalation of negative social exchange: Reflexive punishment or deliberative deterrence?. *Journal of Experimental Social Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that negative escalation in repeated taking/giving games is driven by reflexive punishment rather than strategic deterrence. Deliberation reduces escalation, while reflexive or high cognitive load conditions increase it. Escalation occurs only in response to perceived negative actions (taking), not positive ones (giving). Explicitly instructing participants to focus on future consequences or to deter does not increase escalation; only a goal to punish does. The findings are robust across one-shot and repeated interactions, and across different manipulations of deliberation and cognitive resources. However, the study does not use public goods games, does not implement a punishment stage, and does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. The experimental paradigm is a repeated dictator/taking game, not a public goods game, and the main outcomes are escalation and behavioral responses to harm, not group efficiency or payoff. There is no punishment stage where players can pay to reduce others' payoffs, and no analysis of group welfare or efficiency. The findings may be relevant for understanding the psychological drivers of punitive escalation, but do not inform the quantitative effect of punishment mechanisms on efficiency in PGG-like games.

- source: Harth, N. S., & Regner, T. (2017). The spiral of distrust: (Non-)cooperation in a repeated trust game is predicted by anger and individual differences in negative reciprocity orientation. *International Journal of Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that senders who receive feedback about low back transfers from trustees experience increased anger, which in turn leads to reduced cooperation (lower amounts sent) in subsequent rounds. Individuals with a high negative reciprocity orientation are more prone to anger and are less willing to cooperate, even in the absence of anger-eliciting feedback. The interaction between anger and negative reciprocity orientation further reduces cooperation. The absence of feedback results in lower anger and relatively higher cooperation, especially among those low in negative reciprocity orientation. The study does not analyze efficiency, group payoff, or total earnings as outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods or similar games, as it studies a repeated trust game without any punishment or sanctioning mechanism. The main outcomes are behavioral (amount sent/cooperation) and emotional (anger), not payoff-based. However, it provides indirect evidence that negative emotions and individual differences in reciprocity orientation can reduce cooperation in repeated interactions, especially when feedback about others' behavior is available. For prediction tasks focused on efficiency or payoff, this paper is only weakly informative and should not be weighted heavily unless the prediction context is specifically about trust games or the psychological mechanisms of cooperation.

- source: Liu, W. X., Wang, H., Zhu, H. J., Zhu, X. Y., He, X. Y., & Zhang, W. (2022). Morality is Supreme: The Roles of Morality, Fairness and Group Identity in the Ultimatum Paradigm. *Psychology Research and Behavior Management*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that in the ultimatum game, responders are more likely to accept offers (including unfair ones) from proposers with positive moral backgrounds than from those with negative moral backgrounds. This effect is robust across fair, moderately unfair, and extremely unfair offers. Group identity, manipulated via moral priming, has only a partial effect: positive or negative moral priming increases acceptance rates for positive moral proposers in unfair allocations, but does not significantly affect responses to negative moral proposers. The gender of the proposer does not significantly affect acceptance rates. The study does not analyze efficiency, group payoff, or total earnings, so its findings are not directly transferable to efficiency prediction tasks.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence that in ultimatum game settings, the perceived morality of the proposer strongly influences acceptance rates, even for unfair offers. However, because the outcome measured is acceptance rate (not efficiency or group payoff), and because the ultimatum game is not a public goods game or a direct variant, the findings are only indirectly relevant for predicting efficiency effects of punishment in PGG-like environments. The study does not manipulate or analyze punishment mechanisms, costs, or efficiency, so it does not provide direct evidence for the prediction task. Its main value is in highlighting the importance of social/moral context in responder behavior, which may inform broader models of cooperation and norm enforcement.

- source: Schram, A., & Charness, G. (2015). Inducing Social Norms in Laboratory Allocation Choices. *Management Science*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds
  findings: The experiment demonstrates that providing impartial advice about what one 'ought to do' (shared understanding) only affects behavior when choices are made public, i.e., when social (not just moral) norms are salient. In the advice + public condition, dictators are significantly less selfish than in other conditions. Advice alone (private) does not change behavior, nor does public observability alone without advice. The effect is interpreted as the creation of a social norm, enforced by the threat of social disapproval (shame), not by material punishment. There is no direct measurement or analysis of efficiency, group payoff, or surplus. The findings are relevant for understanding norm induction and the role of observability, but not for predicting efficiency effects of punishment in public goods games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the efficiency effects of punishment in public goods games or similar environments. It does not implement punishment, rewards, or repeated interaction, and does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes. However, it does provide informative context about how social norms (via advice and observability) can shift behavior toward less selfish choices in allocation games. For prediction tasks, the main takeaway is that nonmaterial social sanctions (public observability) combined with shared understanding can induce prosocial behavior, but the absence of payoff-based outcomes and the lack of a PGG or punishment mechanism limit its direct applicability.

- source: Rand, D. G., Brescoll, V. L., Everett, J. A. C., Capraro, V., & Barcelo, H. (2016). Social Heuristics and Social Roles: Intuition Favors Altruism for Women but Not for Men. *Journal of Experimental Psychology-general*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that promoting intuition (via time pressure, conceptual priming, or cognitive load) increases altruistic giving in the Dictator Game for women, but not for men. This effect is further moderated by gender role identification: women who identify with masculine attributes become less altruistic when deliberating, while those who identify with feminine attributes are unaffected by deliberation. Men are generally less altruistic, and their giving is not significantly affected by cognitive processing mode or gender role identification. The study does not involve strategic interaction, punishment, or group efficiency outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence relevant to predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. The experimental paradigm is the Dictator Game, which is a unilateral giving game with no strategic interaction, no punishment or sanctioning mechanisms, and no group payoff or efficiency concept. While the paper discusses social norms and the internalization of altruism (and mentions that women are punished more for failing to be altruistic), it does not experimentally manipulate or measure punishment, nor does it report any efficiency or group payoff outcomes. Thus, it offers little direct support for the downstream prediction task, except as very indirect context about gender, social heuristics, and norm compliance.

- source: Mellers, B. A., Haselhuhn, M. P., Tetlock, P. E., Silva, J. C., & Isen, A. M. (2010). Predicting Behavior in Economic Games by Looking Through the Eyes of the Players. *Journal of Experimental Psychology-general*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds
  findings: The main findings are: (1) Players' self-defined want and ought motives predict their offers in ultimatum and dictator games; (2) in the ultimatum game, more players act according to what they believe is fair (ought), while in the dictator game, want and ought are equally influential; (3) positive mood induction increases the likelihood of making equal offers in the dictator game; (4) when given an exit option, players experiencing greater conflict between want and ought are more likely to exit. The paper does not report on efficiency, group payoff, or the effect of punishment in public goods or repeated games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. It does not manipulate or analyze punishment institutions, marginal per-capita return, or group efficiency. Its findings on rejections in the ultimatum game and exit behavior in the dictator game are only loosely related to the prediction task. The main value is in highlighting the psychological mechanisms (want/ought selves) that may underlie cooperation and sanctioning, but it does not inform the quantitative effect of punishment on efficiency given game design dimensions.

- source: Wang, X. J., Chen, Z. S., Krumhuber, E. G., & Chen, H. (2021). Money and flexible generosity. *British Journal of Social Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds
  findings: Across three studies, the authors find that using money as the currency in DG/UG games reduces generosity compared to candy or food, but only when there are no sanctions (DG) or reputation concerns (private condition). When punishment is possible (UG) or when allocations are public (reputation at stake), the negative effect of money on generosity is reduced or eliminated. Imbuing money with prosocial meaning (charity) also attenuates the effect. The main outcome is generosity rate, not efficiency or group payoff. The findings suggest that the presence of sanctions or reputation mechanisms can offset the self-serving effects of money on prosocial behavior, but do not provide direct evidence about efficiency in public goods games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence that the presence of punishment or reputation mechanisms can mitigate the negative effect of monetary incentives on prosocial behavior, but it does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes, nor does it use a public goods game structure. The results are most relevant for predicting how punishment or reputation might moderate the effect of monetary incentives on generosity or cooperation, but less directly informative for predicting efficiency changes in public goods games with or without punishment. The findings suggest that punishment/reputation mechanisms may help maintain prosocial behavior even in monetary contexts, but the lack of efficiency or group payoff data limits direct applicability to the prediction task.

- source: Azevedo, R. T., Panasiti, M. S., Maglio, R., & Aglioti, S. M. (2018). Perceived warmth and competence of others shape voluntary deceptive behaviour in a morally relevant setting. *British Journal of Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that the perceived warmth and competence of the target modulate the likelihood of lying for self-gain: participants lie less to high-warmth individuals and more to high-competence individuals. The risk of disclosure (reputation risk) reduces lying, particularly toward low-warmth/low-competence targets. These effects are interpreted as reflecting malleable personal moral standards and social evaluation processes. However, the study does not analyze efficiency, group payoff, or welfare, and the game is not a public goods game or direct variant. The outcomes are behavioral (lying frequency) rather than payoff-based.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence about how social context, reputation risk, and target characteristics can modulate dishonest behavior in a zero-sum, two-player setting. It does not provide direct evidence for predicting efficiency or payoff outcomes in public goods games with or without punishment. The manipulation of reputation risk is conceptually adjacent to punishment/sanction mechanisms, but there is no explicit punishment, and the outcome is not efficiency. Thus, the paper is of limited direct use for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, but it may inform understanding of how social evaluation and disclosure risk affect norm-violating behavior.

- source: Gomes, C. M., Mulhinch, M. R., & McCullough, M. E. (2025). People decide whether to share social information-and whether to share it accurately-on the basis of self-interest. *Evolution and Human Behavior*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The experiment shows that people are more likely to share reputational information (gossip) when they have behaved generously in a prior economic game, and less likely when they have behaved greedily. The tendency to share information honestly or dishonestly is strongly influenced by whether the participant stands to benefit from being chosen as a partner for a subsequent economic game. When lying is possible and being chosen is incentivized, greedy or indifferent participants are more likely to share dishonest, self-serving gossip. Generous participants are more likely to share honest information, especially when honesty is required and being chosen is incentivized. The results support reputation-based partner choice and social competition theories over indirect reciprocity theory. No direct measures of group efficiency or total payoff are reported.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or their close variants. However, it offers informative context about the strategic use of social information (gossip) in economic games with partner choice and reputation. The findings suggest that the effectiveness of information-sharing mechanisms (and their honesty) depends on participants' incentives and prior behavior, which may be relevant for understanding the role of reputation and information in environments with punishment or partner choice. Since no efficiency or payoff outcomes are reported, this paper should not be used as a primary source for predicting efficiency effects of punishment, but may inform mechanistic expectations about when information-based interventions (including punishment or reputation) are likely to be effective or undermined by dishonesty.

- source: Simpson, B., Harrell, A., & Willer, R. (2013). Hidden Paths from Morality to Cooperation: Moral Judgments Promote Trust and Trustworthiness. *Social Forces*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: Across three experiments, the authors show that (1) making moral judgments of others' immoral behavior increases subsequent trustworthiness in a trust game (measured by amount returned), (2) this effect is mediated by increased self-perceived moral identity, and (3) observers trust and prefer to interact with strong moral judges, perceiving them as more moral and trustworthy. The behavioral outcomes are individual-level trust and trustworthiness, not group-level efficiency or payoff. The design does not include public goods games, explicit punishment, or sanctioning mechanisms.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. Its findings are adjacent: it shows that making moral judgments (not material punishment) can increase trustworthiness in trust games, and that observers trust moral judges more. However, there is no manipulation of punishment, no public goods game, and no group payoff or efficiency outcome. The results may be relevant for understanding how non-material social judgments can affect prosocial behavior, but they do not inform the quantitative prediction of efficiency changes due to punishment in PGG-like settings.

- source: Adam, T. C. (2010). Competition encourages cooperation: client fish receive higher-quality service when cleaner fish compete. *Animal Behaviour*.
  type: empirical | empirical=observational | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: none
