  findings: Clients (ornate butterflyfish) with access to multiple cleaner fish stations receive higher-quality service from cleaners: they are cleaned for longer per event, are less likely to be ignored, and have fewer interactions terminated by the cleaner. However, the frequency of cheating by cleaners does not decrease with increased partner choice. Aggressive punishment (chasing) by clients in response to cheating is common and does not differ significantly between clients with and without partner choice. The study supports the role of partner choice (competition among cleaners) in promoting cooperative service, but suggests that punishment is used as a fixed strategy rather than being contingent on partner choice. No direct measures of efficiency or group payoff are reported.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence relevant to the prediction task: it shows that increased partner choice (analogous to increased competition or exit options in PGG-like games) leads to higher-quality cooperative behavior (service) from the provider, but does not reduce cheating frequency. Punishment (aggression) is used regardless of partner choice. However, the study does not use a public goods game, does not manipulate punishment costs or technology, and does not report efficiency or payoff outcomes. Thus, while the behavioral mechanisms (partner choice, punishment) are relevant, the findings cannot be directly mapped to predictions about efficiency changes due to punishment in PGGs. The main value is in supporting the importance of partner choice and the robustness of punishment as a behavioral response.

- source: Yamagishi, T., Cook, K. S., & Watabe, M. (1998). Uncertainty, trust, and commitment formation in the United States and Japan. *American Journal of Sociology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=weak
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The experiments show that when social uncertainty is high (i.e., risk of being exploited is greater), participants are more likely to form committed, stable relationships with specific partners, rather than seeking new partners or maximizing short-term gains. This effect is robust across both US and Japanese samples, and is stronger among individuals with lower general trust. The presence or absence of opportunities for exploitation (extortion/appropriation) is manipulated, but the main outcome is the frequency of commitment formation, not efficiency or group payoff. The results support the theory that social uncertainty drives commitment formation, and that general trust 'emancipates' individuals from closed, committed relationships. However, the study does not provide direct evidence on the effect of punishment or sanctions on efficiency in public goods or similar games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence relevant to the prediction task. It shows that in environments with higher social uncertainty (risk of exploitation), individuals are more likely to form stable, committed relationships, which may facilitate cooperation. However, the experimental design does not include a public goods game, nor does it measure group efficiency or total payoff as an outcome. The 'punishment' or 'sanction' mechanism is limited to opportunities for exploitation (negative actions), not costly punishment of free riders. Therefore, while the findings may inform understanding of how uncertainty and trust affect partner choice and commitment, they do not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games. The paper is best used as contextual or indirect evidence about the role of uncertainty and trust in cooperation and commitment, rather than as a source of quantitative estimates for efficiency outcomes.

- source: Kaltwasser, L., Hildebrandt, A., Wilhelm, O., & Sommer, W. (2016). Behavioral and neuronal determinants of negative reciprocity in the ultimatum game. *Social Cognitive and Affective Neuroscience*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=weak
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that both prosociality (as measured by social value orientation) and assertiveness predict the likelihood of rejecting unfair offers in the ultimatum game, supporting both the strong reciprocity and status defense models of negative reciprocity. Neural data (feedback negativity) show that individuals with higher negative reciprocity have stronger neural responses to proposer fairness. The study does not report on efficiency, group payoff, or welfare, and does not manipulate punishment as a treatment variable. The findings are limited to the behavioral and neural correlates of negative reciprocity in the UG, not to efficiency or payoff outcomes in public goods or similar games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. The design is an ultimatum game, not a public goods game, and the main outcome is the rejection rate of unfair offers (a form of costly punishment), not group payoff or efficiency. While the study is relevant for understanding individual motivations for costly punishment (negative reciprocity), it does not inform how punishment affects group efficiency or payoff under different game design dimensions. Thus, it should not be weighted strongly for the downstream prediction task focused on efficiency outcomes.

- source: Li, O., Shi, Y., & Li, K. (2025). Red, rather than blue can promote fairness in decision-making. *Humanities & Social Sciences Communications*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=weak
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that the color red (vs. blue) increases (vs. decreases) the amount offered by proposers in the ultimatum game, and this effect is mediated by perceived competitiveness. In the impunity game, where responders cannot punish proposers by rejecting offers, color has no effect on offers. The results suggest that contextual cues like color can influence fair behavior in allocation games, but only when strategic motives (i.e., the threat of rejection/punishment) are present. The study does not report efficiency, group payoff, or total earnings, and does not use a public goods game or direct punishment mechanism.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting efficiency or payoff outcomes in public goods games with or without punishment. However, it offers indirect evidence that contextual cues (like color) can affect proposer behavior in allocation games when strategic punishment is possible (as in the UG), but not when it is absent (as in the IG). The findings suggest that the presence or absence of punishment mechanisms (or their analogs) can moderate the effect of contextual factors on fair behavior, but the lack of efficiency or group payoff data limits its direct utility for the prediction task.

- source: Ellingsen, T., & Johannesson, M. (2008). Anticipated verbal feedback induces altruistic behavior. *Evolution and Human Behavior*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=weak
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, reward_exists, show_n_rounds
  findings: The opportunity for recipients to send anonymous verbal feedback (messages) to dividers in a one-shot dictator game significantly increases the average donation from 24.8% to 34.1% of the endowment. The fraction of zero donations drops from 42% to 21%, and equal splits rise from 30% to 48%. Negative feedback is almost always given when no money is received, and positive feedback is almost always given for equal splits. The results suggest that anticipated symbolic rewards and punishments (praise/blame) can promote altruistic behavior, even in anonymous, one-shot settings. However, the study does not measure efficiency or group payoff, and the design is not a public goods game.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence that anticipated symbolic sanctions (verbal feedback) can increase prosocial behavior in allocation games, suggesting that non-material punishment/reward mechanisms may have a positive effect on cooperation or generosity. However, because the study uses a one-shot dictator game (not a repeated public goods game), does not report efficiency or group payoff, and the 'punishment' is symbolic rather than material, its direct predictive value for efficiency in public goods games with material punishment is limited. The findings may be most relevant for predicting the effect of adding symbolic feedback or communication channels, rather than material punishment, in similar environments.

- source: Testori, M., Cruz, T. D. D., & Beersma, B. (2024). Punishing or praising gossipers: How people interpret the motives driving negative gossip shapes its consequences. *Social and Personality Psychology Compass*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment

  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that receivers' interpretation of gossip motives (pro-social vs. pro-self) significantly shapes their trust in both the sender and the target of gossip. Correctly interpreting pro-social motives leads to higher trust in the sender, while pro-self motives reduce trust. The ability to overcome intuition (measured by CRT) increases the likelihood of correctly identifying pro-social motives. However, the study does not use a public goods game or analyze efficiency, group payoff, or welfare. The behavioral outcome is the amount invested in a trust game vignette, which is not linked to real payoffs or group efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments. The experimental design is adjacent to PGGs, using a trust game vignette, and focuses on trust and motive interpretation rather than payoff or efficiency. While the findings may inform understanding of how social information and its interpretation affect trust and possibly cooperation, they do not provide quantitative or qualitative evidence about efficiency or payoff outcomes under punishment or sanction conditions. Thus, this paper should not be weighted heavily for the downstream prediction task focused on efficiency.

- source: Oda, R., Niwa, Y., Honma, A., & Hiraishi, K. (2011). An eye-like painting enhances the expectation of a good reputation. *Evolution and Human Behavior*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment

  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds
  findings: The presence of a stylized eye image in a dictator game increases the amount of money allocated to the recipient. This effect is not due to fear of punishment but is mediated by the expectation of a positive reputation or reward, likely from a third party. The study uses a one-shot dictator game with no communication, no punishment or reward mechanisms, and no group-level payoff structure. The findings are relevant to understanding how subtle cues of observation can increase prosocial behavior, but do not provide evidence on efficiency or payoff outcomes in public goods or punishment-enabled environments.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. The design is a one-shot dictator game with no punishment or reward mechanisms, and the main outcome is the amount given by the dictator, not group efficiency or total payoff. The findings suggest that subtle cues of being watched can increase prosocial behavior via reputation concerns, but do not inform the effect of actual punishment mechanisms or efficiency outcomes. Thus, this paper should not be weighted heavily in predicting efficiency changes due to punishment in public goods games.

- source: Singh, M., & Garfield, Z. H. (2022). Evidence for third-party mediation but not punishment in Mentawai justice. *Nature Human Behaviour*.
  type: empirical | empirical=observational | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=weak
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: chat, punishment_cost, show_other_summaries, show_punishment_id
  findings: The paper finds that, in the Mentawai context, third-party punishment is absent: no cases were found where third parties (outside the victim, their close kin, or spouse) directly punished or demanded penalties from transgressors. Indirect or second-order sanctions by third parties are also not observed. Instead, penalties (tulou) are negotiated and paid primarily to restore dyadic cooperation, with kinship playing a major role in the likelihood and size of payments. Mediation by third parties (especially shamans and elders) is common, particularly in more severe or between-clan disputes, but the mediator's role is to facilitate negotiation, not to enforce punishment. The study suggests that, in this small-scale society, institutionalized penalties serve to repair relationships rather than to enforce group-level norms through punishment. The findings are based on retrospective interviews and ethnographic observation of 302 reliable cases of transgression and response.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides little direct support for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments. The studied context is not a PGG or close variant, and the outcomes are not efficiency or group payoff. The findings suggest that, in small-scale, kin-based societies, third-party punishment is rare or absent, and penalties serve to restore dyadic cooperation rather than to enforce group norms or increase group efficiency. If extrapolating to PGG-like settings, the results would suggest that the presence or absence of third-party punishment may depend on the social structure and the nature of cooperation (private vs. public goods), but the paper does not provide quantitative or experimental evidence on efficiency outcomes. Thus, it is of limited use for the downstream prediction task, except as a caution that punishment mechanisms may not always function as assumed in small-scale, kin-based societies.

- source: Delton, A. W., Cosmides, L., Guemo, M., Robertson, T. E., & Tooby, J. (2012). The Psychosemantics of Free Riding: Dissecting the Architecture of a Moral Concept. *Journal of Personality and Social Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=weak
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The main finding is that people spontaneously and robustly categorize others as free riders only when they perceive intentional, exploitive undercontribution, not merely low contribution or accidental failure. This categorization is specific and not explained by general tendencies to categorize, by return rate rules, or by a general moral violator rule. Free riders elicit more punitive sentiment and are less preferred as cooperative partners, but these are attitudinal outcomes, not measured payoffs. The study provides evidence for a specialized psychological mechanism for free rider detection, supporting evolutionary game theory predictions about the need to identify and sanction intentional exploiters in collective action. However, the study does not use actual public goods games, does not manipulate or measure punishment institutions, and does not report efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. It does not manipulate punishment institutions, nor does it report group payoff, efficiency, or related economic outcomes. Instead, it provides evidence that people are psychologically prepared to identify and punish intentional free riders, and that this categorization is specific and robust. For the prediction task, this suggests that punishment mechanisms may be more effective at targeting intentional exploiters than accidental undercontributors, but the paper does not provide quantitative or experimental evidence about efficiency effects. Its main value is in informing the psychological underpinnings of punishment and norm enforcement, not in providing payoff-based effect sizes or moderators.

- source: Price, M. E. (2006). Judgments about cooperators and freeriders on a Shuar work team: An evolutionary psychological perspective. *Organizational Behavior and Human Decision Processes*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=weak
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that Shuar villagers overwhelmingly judge higher contributors to a collective work project more positively (more respect, less punishment) than lower contributors, regardless of how much the worker benefits from the project. When contribution is held constant and benefit varies, those who participate more in collective actions (mainly men) are more likely to favor 'altruists' (high contributors, low benefit) and disfavor 'freeriders' (low contributors, high benefit), while low-participation subjects (mainly women) tend to favor those who benefit more, regardless of contribution. The study does not measure actual group payoffs, efficiency, or the effects of real punishment or reward options.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. It does, however, provide indirect evidence that social judgments (including support for punishment) are strongly influenced by perceived contribution, and that individuals who participate more in collective actions are more likely to support punishing freeriders and favoring cooperators. The findings suggest that in environments where social judgment or informal sanctions are possible, support for punishing low contributors may be widespread, especially among frequent cooperators. However, because the study does not implement real punishment, measure actual payoffs, or use a public goods game design, its value for predicting efficiency outcomes is limited and indirect.

- source: Vonasch, A. J., Reynolds, T., Winegard, B. M., & Baumeister, R. F. (2018). Death Before Dishonor: Incurring Costs to Protect Moral Reputation. *Social Psychological and Personality Science*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=weak
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: none
  findings: The paper finds that people value their moral reputation extremely highly, often above wealth, success, or even life itself. In hypothetical scenarios, large proportions of participants preferred jail, amputation, or death to acquiring a bad reputation. In lab experiments, many participants chose to endure pain or disgust (cold pressor, touching worms) rather than have information suggesting they were racist shared with others. The motivation to avoid reputation damage is strong and can drive costly behavior. However, the paper does not use public goods games, does not manipulate or measure punishment or sanctions in a game-theoretic sense, and does not report efficiency or payoff outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. It does, however, support the broader claim that reputation concerns can motivate costly prosocial or norm-compliant behavior, which is a mechanism often invoked in explaining why punishment can sustain cooperation. The findings may be useful for contextualizing why individuals might comply with norms or avoid free-riding, but they do not inform quantitative predictions about efficiency or the effects of specific game design dimensions. For the prediction task, this paper is only indirectly relevant and should not be weighted heavily for estimating efficiency outcomes.

- source: Hart, E., Mellers, B. A., & Bicchieri, C. (2019). Bad luck or bad intentions: When do third parties reveal offenders' intentions to victims?. *Journal of Experimental Social Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=none
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: Third party observers are more likely to inform victims when harm is accidental than when it is intentional. Moral anger increases the likelihood of informing about intentional harm, while empathy increases the likelihood of informing in both cases. Observers are more likely to inform when they believe the victim's beliefs about the offender's intent are inaccurate, and when the information has consequences for future interactions (e.g., potential for punishment). However, the paper does not study or report on group efficiency, total payoff, or the effects of punishment mechanisms as in public goods games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence relevant to predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency or payoff in public goods game environments. The design is adjacent in that it uses economic games and discusses punishment and fairness, but the dependent variables are about information sharing, not payoffs or efficiency. Thus, it should not be used to inform quantitative predictions about efficiency changes due to punishment in PGG-like settings.

- source: Kim, M., & Sivan, E. W. Z. (2017). Faking It Is Hard to Do: Entrepreneurial Norm Enforcement and Suspicions of Deviance. *Sociological Science*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=none
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries, show_punishment_id
  findings: The paper finds that when norm enforcement is mandated (i.e., actors are required to respond to deviance), enforcers are perceived as more sincere and less likely to be suspected of ulterior motives, even in high-accusability contexts. In contrast, when enforcement is entrepreneurial (i.e., voluntary and not mandated) and the context is high accusability (where actors have reason to fear being accused of deviance), enforcers are viewed with greater suspicion and are more likely to be seen as having ulterior motives. In low-accusability contexts, entrepreneurial enforcement does not arouse suspicion. The study does not measure or report on efficiency, group payoff, or any economic outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency or payoff in public goods games or similar economic games. Its findings are about social perceptions of norm enforcers, not about behavioral or payoff consequences of punishment. The design is adjacent to PGG-like environments in that it involves norm enforcement and public goods logic, but it is not an economic game and does not manipulate or measure efficiency, group payoff, or related outcomes. Therefore, it should not be used as direct evidence for predicting efficiency effects of punishment in PGGs, but may be useful for understanding psychological or reputational moderators of norm enforcement.

- source: Benard, S., Doan, L., Nicholson, D. A., Meanwell, E., Wright, E. L., & Lista, P. (2024). An Eye For an Eye Versus Turning The Other Cheek? The Status Consequences of Revenge and Forgiveness in Intergroup Conflict. *Social Forces*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=field_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=none
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: none
  findings: The main findings are: (1) Forgiveness in intergroup conflict is perceived as more status-worthy than revenge, across national, political, and sports identities. (2) Justifying actions as group-motivated (versus individually motivated) has little effect on perceived status, except for a small positive effect for forgiveness in the sports scenario. (3) Open-ended responses show that forgiving actors are more often described as competent, warm, and high-status, while vengeful actors are more often described as dominant and conflict-seeking. (4) There is some evidence that group-motivated revenge is seen as making the group look bad. (5) The study does not measure or analyze efficiency, group payoff, or any material or welfare outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. While it conceptually links intergroup conflict to public goods problems and discusses status as a selective incentive, all outcomes are attitudinal (perceived status, trait attributions) rather than behavioral or payoff-based. There is no manipulation of actual contribution, punishment, or reward in a game-theoretic or economic sense, and no measurement of group payoff, efficiency, or welfare. Thus, the paper is not informative for predicting efficiency outcomes in experimental public goods games with or without punishment.

- source: Jin, S. X., Balliet, D., Romano, A., Spadaro, G., van Lissa, C. J., Agostini, M., Bélanger, J. J., Gützkow, B., Kreienkamp, J., & Leander, N. P. (2021). Intergenerational conflicts of interest and prosocial behavior during the COVID-19 pandemic. *Personality and Individual Differences*.

  dimensions: none
  findings: The study finds that age is associated with perceived costs of the pandemic (older people perceive higher health risks, younger people perceive more daily life disruption and job insecurity), but age is not robustly associated with prosocial COVID-19 behaviors (such as social distancing or staying at home) or support for behavioral regulations (such as mandatory quarantine or vaccination). The study conceptualizes pandemic-related prosocial behaviors as a social dilemma but does not use a public goods game or direct analog, nor does it measure or analyze efficiency or payoff outcomes. No experimental manipulation of punishment or sanctions is present; support for behavioral regulations is measured as an attitude, not as an implemented or experienced sanctioning institution.

While it discusses social dilemmas and support for behavioral regulations (as a proxy for second-order cooperation), it does not manipulate or implement punishment, nor does it measure efficiency, group payoff, or welfare. The findings are based on self-reported attitudes and behaviors in a real-world pandemic context, not on controlled game-theoretic experiments. Therefore, its value for predicting efficiency effects of punishment in PGGs is limited and indirect at best.

- source: Ocampo, J., & Keltner, D. (2024). Dispositional compassion shifts social preferences in systematic ways. *Journal of Personality*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=weak | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, show_n_rounds
  findings: The studies show that people higher in dispositional compassion express less satisfaction with both advantageous and disadvantageous monetary allocations relative to others, indicating a preference for equality. These effects are robust to controls for prosociality, positive affect, and other personality traits. Experimental inductions of state compassion or pride do not reliably shift these social preferences. The tasks are abstract, one-shot, and hypothetical, with no real economic consequences or group interaction. There is no analysis of efficiency, group payoff, or the effects of punishment or reward mechanisms.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting efficiency outcomes in public goods games with or without punishment. The design is adjacent to economic games but lacks key features: there is no group interaction, no repeated rounds, no punishment or reward, and no real payoffs. The findings may inform how dispositional compassion relates to preferences for equality in allocation, but do not inform the effect of punishment on efficiency or payoff in PGG-like environments. Thus, its value for the downstream prediction task is very limited.

- source: Bravo, G. (2010). Voluntary contribution to public goods in mutual-help forums: reciprocity or group attachment?. *Socio-economic Review*.
  type: empirical | empirical=observational | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=weak | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, show_other_summaries
  findings: The study finds that the success of the mutual-help forum depends on the complementary roles of a small core of altruistic contributors and a larger group of reciprocators. Reciprocity is a significant motivator for most users, while the core group is driven by intrinsic motivation and group attachment. The forum achieves high performance in terms of questions answered and response speed, but this is not analyzed in terms of efficiency or group payoff. There is no evidence of formal or informal punishment mechanisms affecting contribution or efficiency. The findings suggest that, in the absence of sanctioning, a privileged core group can sustain cooperation and public good provision in online communities.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides limited direct support for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments, as it studies a real-world mutual-help forum without any explicit punishment or sanctioning mechanism. The findings are relevant for understanding how cooperation can be sustained without punishment, especially through reciprocity and intrinsic motivation. However, the absence of efficiency or payoff outcomes, and the lack of any punishment treatment, mean that this paper should not be used as direct evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency. It may be useful as contextual evidence for baseline cooperation in the absence of sanctions.

- source: Mifune, N., Hashimoto, H., & Yamagishi, T. (2010). Altruism toward in-group members as a reputation mechanism. *Evolution and Human Behavior*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=weak | payoff=weak
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds
  findings: The presence of eye images (cues of monitoring) on participants' computer screens increased the frequency of fair (equal) allocations in a one-shot dictator game, but only when the recipient was an in-group member. No such effect was found for out-group recipients. The effect is interpreted as evidence that reputation concerns (triggered by monitoring cues) selectively enhance altruism toward in-group members. The study does not involve repeated interaction, public goods, punishment, or efficiency outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides little direct support for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. The design is a one-shot dictator game with no punishment or sanctioning mechanism, and the outcome is allocation behavior rather than group payoff or efficiency. The only relevant dimension is the effect of monitoring cues (eyes) on prosocial behavior, which may be loosely analogous to reputation or observability manipulations in public goods games, but not to punishment. Thus, this paper should not be weighted heavily for the downstream prediction task focused on punishment and efficiency.

- source: Ernest-Jones, M., Nettle, D., & Bateson, M. (2011). Effects of eye images on everyday cooperative behavior: a field experiment. *Evolution and Human Behavior*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=field_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=weak | payoff=none
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: none
  findings: Displaying images of eyes in a cafeteria significantly reduced the likelihood of littering (i.e., increased norm compliance/cooperation), independent of whether the accompanying message was relevant to littering. The effect was strongest when there were fewer people present (i.e., when natural cues of observation were sparse). The study provides evidence that subtle cues of being watched can promote cooperative behavior in real-world settings, but does not address efficiency or payoff outcomes, nor does it involve punishment or sanction mechanisms.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting efficiency or payoff outcomes in public goods games with or without punishment. It is relevant for understanding how cues of observation (such as eye images) can increase norm compliance in real-world settings, especially when actual observation is low. However, it does not manipulate or analyze punishment, marginal per-capita return, or other core PGG design dimensions, nor does it report any payoff-based outcomes. Its findings may be indirectly relevant for predicting the effect of observation cues on cooperation, but not for the efficiency effects of punishment in PGG-like environments.

- source: Bernhard, R. M., & Cushman, F. (2022). Extortion, intuition, and the dark side of reciprocity. *Cognition*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=field_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, reward_exists
  findings: The experiment finds that both communication and subsidies for NCH can shift groups toward more cooperative, higher-efficiency outcomes, but also that some groups coordinate on Nash equilibria (heavy spraying) that are also efficient in the game’s payoff structure. Group-level characteristics are more predictive of cooperative behavior than individual traits. The game does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanism, so no evidence is provided about the effects of punishment on efficiency or cooperation. The findings are most relevant for understanding the effects of communication and positive incentives (subsidies) on coordination and efficiency in public-goods-like environments, but not for punishment.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence about the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments, as no punishment or sanctioning mechanism is present in the experimental design. It does provide informative evidence about the effects of communication and subsidies, and about the importance of group-level characteristics for coordination and efficiency. For prediction tasks focused on punishment, this paper is not directly informative. For tasks involving communication or positive incentives, the findings are more relevant.

- source: Robbins, B., Karell, D., Siegenthaler, S., & Kamm, A. (2023). Pathways to prosocial leadership: an online experiment on the effects of external subsidies and the relative price of giving. *European Sociological Review*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, reward_exists, reward_cost
  findings: External subsidies (private piece-rate transfers) to leaders increase the proportion of community tokens given to recipients, thus increasing group welfare. The effect is robust to the timing of subsidy introduction or removal, with no evidence of crowding out or reduced giving after subsidy removal. The relative price of giving (multiplier) does not significantly affect giving. Norms about giving are not affected by either subsidies or the multiplier. The design does not include punishment or sanctioning mechanisms, and the game is a modified dictator game rather than a public goods game, but the main outcome is directly related to group welfare/efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct evidence that external subsidies to leaders (akin to positive incentives or rewards) can increase group welfare in resource allocation settings, even when leaders have full discretion. However, it does not address punishment or sanctioning, so it does not inform predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency. The design is adjacent to public goods games, as it involves redistribution rather than voluntary contribution to a public pool. The findings suggest that positive incentives can increase efficiency, but cannot be directly transferred to punishment effects. The absence of punishment, the dictator game structure, and the focus on leader-driven redistribution limit its direct applicability to PGG-like punishment predictions, but the evidence is informative for the effect of positive incentives on efficiency.

- source: Panchanathan, K., Frankenhuis, W. E., & Silk, J. B. (2013). The bystander effect in an N-person dictator game. *Organizational Behavior and Human Decision Processes*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds a strong bystander effect in an N-person dictator game: when there is only one dictator, recipients receive significantly more than when there are two or three dictators. The average transfer as a fraction of the 'equal share' drops from 57% (one dictator) to 36% (two or three dictators). Recipient payoffs are about 50% higher in the one-dictator condition than in the two- or three-dictator conditions. This effect persists even when uncertainty about others' actions is removed (strategy method) and when dictators can communicate and coordinate (bargaining method). The results suggest that most participants transform the situation into a prisoner's dilemma, leading to low transfers in groups. The paper does not study punishment or sanctions, but discusses the possibility that introducing punishment could alleviate the bystander effect.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that increasing the number of potential contributors in a many-to-one helping game (adjacent to PGG) reduces efficiency/welfare, even when communication or strategy methods are used. The absence of punishment or sanctions means the paper does not directly inform the effect of punishment on efficiency, but it does establish a robust baseline: in the absence of punishment, efficiency declines with group size. For prediction tasks, this paper supports the expectation that, in similar games without punishment, efficiency will be lower as group size increases. It also suggests that communication alone may not be sufficient to restore efficiency. However, since punishment is not present, the paper cannot be used to predict the effect of enabling punishment, only to set a control baseline.

- source: Johnson, T., & Obradovich, N. (2025). Testing for completions that simulate altruism in early language models. *Nature Human Behaviour*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, default_contrib, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study demonstrates that certain LLMs (notably text-davinci-003 and GPT-4) generate completions that simulate both payoff-maximizing behavior in non-social tasks and altruistic sharing in dictator game scenarios. The simulated sharing rates for text-davinci-003 are close to those observed in human dictator game experiments, while less advanced models do not consistently maximize payoffs or share resources. The findings are robust to prompt variations, model parameters, and resource framing. No punishment, sanctioning, or public goods game (PGG) structure is present; the focus is on one-shot dictator games and non-social maximization tasks.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or their variants. It does, however, offer high-quality evidence on LLMs' simulated behavior in dictator games and non-social maximization tasks, including payoff-based outcomes. Since there is no punishment or sanctioning, and the game is not a PGG or close variant, this paper should not be used to predict the effect of punishment on efficiency in PGG-like environments. It may be useful for understanding LLMs' simulated social preferences or for comparison with human dictator game data, but not for the downstream prediction task focused on punishment effects.

Honesty-Humility is also positively associated with giving. The study does not analyze efficiency, group payoff, or welfare, and does not include punishment or sanction mechanisms.

  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment

  findings: The experiment finds that workers' effort (reciprocity) increases with their own wage (direct reciprocity) and, when their own wage is unobservable, also with the coworker's wage (indirect reciprocity via signaling). When both wages are observable, the coworker's wage has no effect on effort, indicating that indirect reciprocity is driven by signaling about one's own treatment, not by altruism toward the coworker. There is no evidence that inequality aversion explains the observed patterns. The main outcome is effort, not efficiency or group payoff. No punishment or sanctioning is present.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments. It is adjacent in that it studies reciprocity and information in a gift-exchange game, but the main outcome is effort, not efficiency, and there is no punishment mechanism. The findings may inform predictions about the role of information and signaling in reciprocity, but not about punishment or efficiency. Thus, its value for predicting treatment efficiency from game design dimensions plus control efficiency is limited.

- source: Dunning, D., Anderson, J. E., Schlösser, T., Ehlebracht, D., & Fetchenhauer, D. (2014). Trust at Zero Acquaintance: More a Matter of Respect Than Expectation of Reward. *Journal of Personality and Social Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The main finding is that trust in zero-acquaintance trust games is driven more by internalized moral norms (especially a norm of respect) than by expectations of reward or risk tolerance. Participants report that they 'should' trust more than they 'want' to, and this sense of obligation predicts trust behavior. Making trust decisions public does not increase trust, suggesting the norm is moral rather than social. When the trust game is altered so that the recipient's character is not at stake (e.g., outcome determined by coin flip), trust rates drop. The paper does not study public goods games, punishment, or efficiency outcomes, and thus does not provide direct evidence for the prediction task regarding punishment effects on efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. It does, however, suggest that in trust games without punishment, trust rates can be high due to internalized moral norms, even when efficiency or payoff maximization would not predict such behavior. The findings are only indirectly relevant to public goods games, and not at all relevant to punishment or efficiency outcomes. Thus, this paper should not be weighted heavily in predicting efficiency changes due to punishment in PGG-like settings.

- source: Lamba, S., & Mace, R. (2010). People recognise when they are really anonymous in an economic game. *Evolution and Human Behavior*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The study finds that in the ultimatum game, proposers' offers are sensitive to whether their decisions are public or anonymous, but not to the mere presence of others if anonymity is maintained. The presence of known others increases offers in public conditions but decreases them in anonymous-in-public conditions. Responders' minimum acceptable offers are not affected by anonymity or social context. The findings suggest that explicit information about anonymity can override social cues, and that reputation concerns drive higher offers when decisions are public. The paper does not address punishment, sanctions, or efficiency outcomes, and is not directly about public goods games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. It is adjacent in relevance, as it studies the effect of anonymity and social context on cooperative behavior in the ultimatum game, but does not include punishment, sanctions, or efficiency outcomes. The findings may be indirectly informative for understanding how social context and reputation concerns affect cooperative choices, but do not inform the prediction task regarding punishment-enabled efficiency in PGG-like games.

- source: Gross, J., & De Dreu, C. K. W. (2021). Rule Following Mitigates Collaborative Cheating and Facilitates the Spreading of Honesty Within Groups. *Personality and Social Psychology Bulletin*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The study finds that individuals who are more likely to follow arbitrary, costly rules are also more likely to behave honestly in situations where cheating is possible and profitable. In dyadic and four-person group die-rolling tasks, groups with more high rule-followers exhibit less collaborative cheating (i.e., lower rates of reporting doubles and lower average reported die-rolls). The presence of even a single high rule-follower in a group can reduce collaborative cheating among low rule-followers. However, the study does not manipulate or analyze punishment, sanctions, or any formal enforcement mechanisms, and does not report efficiency or payoff-based outcomes beyond the behavioral proxies for dishonesty.

It does not manipulate or analyze punishment, sanctions, or related enforcement mechanisms, and does not report group efficiency, total payoff, or welfare. Its findings are relevant to understanding the role of individual differences in rule-following and the dynamics of collaborative cheating in the absence of punishment, but it does not inform the effect of punishment on efficiency or payoff. Therefore, it should not be used as direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in PGG-like games.

- source: Insko, C. A., Kirchner, J. L., Pinter, B., Efaw, J., & Wildschut, T. (2005). Interindividual-intergroup discontinuity as a function of trust and categorization: The paradox of expected cooperation. *Journal of Personality and Social Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds
  findings: The experiments demonstrate that groups are less cooperative and more competitive than individuals in a one-shot, three-choice PDG-Alt game. Manipulating trust (high vs. low) increases cooperation and decreases withdrawal for both groups and individuals, but does not significantly affect competition. Manipulating categorization (same vs. different) increases cooperation and decreases withdrawal for both, but increases competition only for groups, especially among those high in guilt proneness. The main dependent variables are behavioral choices, not payoffs or efficiency. No punishment or reward mechanisms are present. The findings are relevant for understanding how group context, trust, and categorization affect cooperation and competition, but do not provide direct evidence about efficiency or payoff outcomes, nor about the effects of punishment.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments, as it does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanisms, nor does it report efficiency or group payoff outcomes. However, it does provide informative context about how group structure, trust, and categorization affect cooperation and competition in a PDG-variant. For prediction tasks involving punishment, this paper is not directly informative, but it may be useful for understanding baseline group vs. individual differences in cooperation in the absence of punishment. The absence of punishment and the focus on behavioral choices rather than payoffs limit its direct applicability to the prediction of efficiency outcomes with or without punishment.

  findings: The study finds that both kinship and herding group membership predict gift-giving among pastoralists, with some cross-cultural differences: Saami herders in Norway emphasize reciprocity and group membership, while Tibetan herders in China emphasize kinship and giving to poorer herders. Gifts are more likely to be given within herding groups and to close kin, but the relative importance of these factors varies by site. There is no evidence of punishment, sanctions, or explicit efficiency/payoff outcomes. The association between gifts received and herd size (wealth) is negative in Tibetan sites (gifts go to poorer herders) and absent in Norwegian sites.

It does not manipulate or analyze punishment, nor does it report group efficiency or payoff outcomes. The design is adjacent to PGGs in that it involves allocation of resources in a social context, but the absence of punishment and efficiency outcomes means it offers little direct support for the downstream prediction task.   findings: The paper finds that requiring additional cash payments from homeowners (when the reserve fund is insufficient) increases the consent rate for maintenance projects. This effect is robust across OLS, IV, and Tobit models, and is stronger for projects proposed by homeowners' associations and for elevator maintenance. The mechanism appears to be increased salience and information provision, which motivates participation. However, the study does not analyze efficiency, group payoff, or the effect of punishment/sanctions, and the outcome is the behavioral approval rate, not a payoff-based measure.

  findings: The study finds that Martu hunters who share a higher proportion of their foraging income are more central in cooperative hunting networks, suggesting that prosocial generosity (pecuniary distancing) is rewarded with more cooperative opportunities. High producers do not keep or receive more for themselves, and the best hunters often keep less than others. The benefits of generosity are indirect, via increased access to cooperation, not direct material gain. No evidence is provided on efficiency, group payoff, or the effects of punishment or sanctions.

- source: Ember, C. R., Skoggard, I., Ringen, E. J., & Farrer, M. (2018). Our better nature: Does resource stress predict beyond-household sharing?. *Evolution and Human Behavior*.
  type: empirical | empirical=observational | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: none
  findings: The study finds that beyond-household food and labor sharing is nearly universal in nonindustrial societies. Societies experiencing higher resource stress (e.g., famine, natural hazards, chronic scarcity) are more likely to have frequent and extensive sharing customs, especially seasonal sharing. However, increased resource stress does not predict broader social or geographic scope of sharing (i.e., with non-relatives or beyond the community); in some cases, sharing with non-relatives is less common under high resource stress. The study does not analyze efficiency, group payoff, or welfare, and does not involve experimental manipulation or public goods game paradigms.

It does not involve experimental games, punishment, or payoff-based outcomes. However, it provides contextual evidence that cooperation (in the form of sharing) is more frequent in environments with higher resource stress, suggesting that environmental risk can increase cooperative customs. This may be indirectly relevant for understanding baseline cooperation in environments with or without punishment, but does not inform the effect of punishment or game design dimensions on efficiency.

- source: Swakman, V., Molleman, L., Ule, A., & Egas, M. (2016). Reputation-based cooperation: empirical evidence for behavioral strategies. *Evolution and Human Behavior*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=weak
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The experiment shows that people condition their helping decisions on both first-order (recipient's past helping) and second-order (context of recipient's past helping/defection) information. Donors are especially interested in 2nd-order information about defection, and tend to reward 'justified defection' (denying help to defectors). There is strong individual heterogeneity: some use only first-order information, some use both, and a minority are unconditional cooperators or defectors. The availability of 2nd-order information (free or costly) does not significantly increase overall helping rates compared to the control, though helping rates are numerically higher when 2nd-order information is available. The study does not report on group efficiency, total payoff, or welfare.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods or similar games, as there is no punishment or sanctioning mechanism in the design. The game is an iterated indirect helping game with binary helping decisions, and the main outcomes are behavioral (helping rates, information use, strategy classification). While the findings are relevant for understanding mechanisms of reputation-based cooperation, they do not inform prediction of efficiency changes due to punishment. The only indirect relevance is that richer information about others' behavior can (non-significantly) increase helping rates, but this is not about efficiency or payoff, nor about punishment.

- source: Molho, C., Soraperra, I., Schulz, J. F., & Shalvi, S. (2025). Guilt drives prosociality across 20 countries. *Nature Human Behaviour*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=weak
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: Across 20 countries, participants were more likely to make prosocial choices in dictator games when they had full information about the consequences of their actions (guilt condition) compared to when they could remain ignorant. Making choices observable to others (shame condition) had negligible or inconsistent effects on prosociality. Individual-level guilt-proneness predicted greater responsiveness to information, but country-level differences were not robust moderators. The study does not include punishment, sanctions, or public goods game structures, and does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. It does not include punishment, sanctions, or group interaction, and does not report efficiency, welfare, or group payoff outcomes. Its findings on the effect of information and observability on individual prosocial choice in dictator games may be contextually informative for understanding mechanisms of prosociality, but are not directly transferable to the prediction task focused on punishment and efficiency in PGG-like settings.

- source: Haley, K. J., & Fessler, D. M. T. (2005). Nobody's watching? Subtle cues affect generosity in an anonymous economic game. *Evolution and Human Behavior*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=weak
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that subtle visual cues of being watched (eyespots) significantly increase generosity in a one-shot dictator game, as measured by both the mean amount allocated and the proportion of dictators who give any money. The effect of reducing auditory cues (earmuffs) is small and not statistically significant. The results suggest that even in anonymous, noniterated economic games, intuitive or affective cues of observability can increase prosocial behavior. However, the study does not address punishment, sanctions, or efficiency/welfare outcomes, and is not a public goods game or close variant.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence relevant to predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or their variants. It does not manipulate or analyze punishment, sanctions, or efficiency/welfare outcomes, nor does it use a public goods game structure. Its findings about subtle cues of observability increasing generosity in dictator games may be contextually interesting for understanding prosocial behavior, but do not inform the prediction task regarding punishment effects on efficiency in PGG-like environments.

- source: Boulu-Reshef, B., & Schulhofer-Wohl, J. (2022). The impact of distance on parochial altruism: An experimental investigation. *European Journal of Political Economy*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=none
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, default_contrib, mpcr
  findings: The experiment finds that parochial altruism (allocation to the between-group account, which benefits the in-group and harms the out-group) is higher when both in-group and out-group distances are high. Low distance to the out-group reduces both altruism and parochial altruism. The main behavioral outcomes are the propensity and amount allocated to within-group and between-group accounts. There is no analysis of group efficiency, total payoff, or welfare. The game does not include punishment or reward mechanisms. The findings are robust to alternative distance measures and controls for group composition.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence relevant to predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods or similar games. The experimental design does not include punishment, sanctions, or efficiency/welfare outcomes. The behavioral outcomes (parochial altruism, altruism) are not directly transferable to payoff-based efficiency predictions. The game is an intergroup conflict variant (IPD-MD), not a standard PGG, and the main manipulations concern group distance, not institutional design features like punishment or reward. Thus, this paper should not be used to inform predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency.

- source: Wiessner, P. (2019). Collective Action for War and for Peace: A Case Study among the Enga of Papua New Guinea. *Current Anthropology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=observational | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=weak | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, chat, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that collective action for war is often easier to mobilize than for peace, due to emotional triggers, private benefits for some individuals (especially young men), and the contagiousness of anger and revenge. Peacemaking, by contrast, requires overcoming internal rifts, mistrust, and the lack of unifying emotions, but is facilitated by strong leadership, compensation payments, and the involvement of broader social networks (including women and affinal kin). The development of rules and institutions for peacemaking (e.g., compensation, mediation) reduced the costs and destructiveness of war, promoted social complexity, and shifted incentives toward peace. However, the breakdown of traditional norms (e.g., with the introduction of guns) led to more destructive wars and greater reluctance to participate. Sanctions against free riders in war are rare due to the importance of brotherhood, but in peacemaking, non-contributors can be gradually reintegrated. The paper does not report efficiency or group payoff in a formal sense, but describes the social and material costs and benefits of war and peace.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments. It offers contextual and theoretical insights into how collective action, norm enforcement, and compensation (a form of sanction) operate in a real-world society, and how these mechanisms affect the costs and benefits of group action. However, it does not manipulate or measure game design dimensions such as player count, MPCR, or punishment cost, nor does it report efficiency or group payoff in a way that can be mapped to PGG outcomes. Its relevance is mainly in illustrating the complexity of sanctioning, the importance of leadership and social structure, and the potential for compensation to restore balance after conflict. It should be used as contextual background rather than as direct input for quantitative prediction.
